Master’s Thesis in Computational Mathematics Infinite-Dimensional Port-Hamiltonian Systems with a Moving Interface
Alexander Kilian matriculation number: 77856 supervised by: Prof. Dr. Fabian Wirth, Dr. Andrii Mironchenko Chair of Dynamical Systems University of Passau Prof. Dr. Bernhard Maschke Laboratoire d’Automatique et de Génie des Procédés Claude Bernard University Lyon 1

Abstract




Eidesstattliche Erklärung

Hiermit erkläre ich,
Alexander Kilian
(Matrikelnummer 77856), gegenüber der Fakultät für Informatik und Mathematik der Universität Passau, dass ich die Masterarbeit mit dem Thema
Infinite-Dimensional Port-Hamiltonian Systems with a Moving Interface
selbstständig angefertigt und keine anderen als die angegebenen Quellen und Hilfsmittel benutzt habe. Alle wörtlich und sinngemäß übernommenen Ausführungen wurden als solche gekennzeichnet. Weiterhin erkläre ich, dass ich diese Arbeit in gleicher oder ähnlicher Form nicht bereits einer anderen Prüfungsbehörde vorgelegt habe.

Passau, den 20.12.2021

Alexander Kilian

Summary

This thesis deals with the formulation and analysis of two systems of conservation laws defined on two complementary intervals and coupled by some moving interface as a single infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian system. This approach may be regarded as an extension of the well-established mathematical framework of boundary port-Hamiltonian systems, i.e., infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian systems interacting with its physical environment.

A vast variety of multi-physics systems can be modeled as a Hamiltonian system, a special type of ordinary and partial differential equations. Such systems have a Hamiltonian, which describes the internally stored energy of the system. By allowing such systems to be open, one usually refers to this class of systems as (boundary) port-Hamiltonian systems. For the analysis of infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems, one combines the energy-based approach by means of Hamiltonians with the poweful framework of strongly continuous semigroups applied in infinite-dimensional systems theory. This approach has led to fundamental well-posedness and stability results in the field of infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems. Furthermore, the power flow at the spatial boundary has been utilized to control port-Hamiltonian systems.

The great success of this system description motivates to extend the port-Hamiltonian formulation to systems of conservation laws that are coupled by some moving interface. In order to keep track of the power flow at the position of the interface, one has to introduce interface port variables. In this thesis, we provide a well-posed port-Hamiltonian model of two distinct systems of conservation laws defined on two complementary, 1-dimensional spatial domains that are coupled by a fixed interface, and illustrate this result by reference to two transmission lines coupled through a resistor. Furthermore, we discuss under which boundary and interface conditions this system is exponentially stable. Since the formulation in case of a moving interface is quite troubled, we present some results that may be regarded as a first step towards a well-posed model, and debate the main issues that have to be tackled in the future.

Zusammenfassung

Ziel der Masterarbeit ist es, zwei auf komplementären Intervallen definierte Systeme von Energieerhaltungssätzen, die durch eine mobile Schnittstelle gekoppelt werden, mathematisch als ein unendlich-dimensionales port-Hamiltonsches System zu modellieren und zu analysieren. Dieses Modell kann als eine Verallgemeinerung der port-Hamiltonschen Formulierung linearer unendlich-dimensionaler Systeme, die mit ihrer Umgebung interagieren, verstanden werden.

Zahlreiche physikalische Prozesse können mathematisch mit Hilfe sogenannter Hamiltonscher Systeme modelliert werden. Diese besitzen eine Hamilton-Funktion, die in vielen Anwendungen die Systemenergie beschreibt. Erlaubt man einen Energieaustausch mit der Umgebung des Systems, so spricht man von port-Hamiltonschen Systemen, die sowohl durch gewöhnliche als auch partielle Differentialgleichungen beschrieben werden können. Bei der Untersuchung unendlich-dimensionaler port-Hamiltonscher Systeme verbindet man den abstrakten, funktionalanalytischen Ansatz mittels der Halbgruppentheorie mit dem auf Hamilton-Funktionen basierten, physikalisch motivierten Ansatz. Mit dieser Methodik ließen sich elementare Wohlgestelltheits- und Stabilitätsresultate auf dem Gebiet unendlich-dimensionaler port-Hamiltonscher Systeme beweisen. Darüber hinaus eignet sich das port-Hamiltonsche Modell aufgrund des Energieaustauschs mit der Umgebung bestens zur energiebasierten Regelung solcher Systeme.

Aufgrund des beachtlichen Erfolgs dieser Systembeschreibung soll in dieser Arbeit der Versuch einer port-Hamiltonschen Formulierung zweier Systeme von Energieerhaltungssätzen, die durch eine mobile Schnittstelle gekoppelt sind, unternommen werden. Dazu werden Schnittstellen-Port-Variablen eingeführt, um den Energiefluss an der sich bewegenden Schnitt- stelle zu modellieren. Wir werden ein wohlgestelltes System des eben beschriebenen Szenarios im Falle einer fixen Schnittstellenposition präsentieren und dieses Resultat anhand zweier Übertragungsleitungen mit unterschiedlichen physikalischen Eigenschaften, die durch einen elektrischen Widerstand gekoppelt werden, illustrieren. Ferner werden wir charakterisieren, unter welchen Rand- und Schnittstellenbedingungen ein solches System exponentiell stabil ist. Die port-Hamiltonsche Formulierung im Falle einer mobilen Schnittstellen ist problembeladen. Wir werden daher Probleme und offene Fragen, die es in Zukunft noch zu klären gilt, ausführlich diskutieren und für den Wohlgestelltheits-Nachweis essentielle Eigenschaften des modellierten Systems präsentieren.

Chapter 1
Introduction

1
Background

The port-Hamiltonian systems theory
[
11
,
17
,
27
]
is a systematic framework for modeling systems comprising various physical domains, such as mechanical, electric, hydraulic, and thermal domains. This approach is realized by classifying every (ideal) system component subject to its main physical characteristics, and by taking energy to be medium of communication between the physical domains in multi-physics systems. The eponymous concepts used for this modeling framework are the system’s Hamiltonian (that is, its total energy) stemming from mechanics
[
2
]
, and the port-based modeling approach
[
22
]
, which is based on the power-conserving interconnection of various system components by means of ports. They consist of physical quantities whose product yields power (e.g., current and voltage in an electrical network, or force and velocity in a mechanical system). That way, the power flow between the system’s components can be expressed. The resulting network structure can be geometrically described by a power-conserving interconnection structure, called the Dirac structure, which has proved to be highly valuable in the field of port-based modeling and analysis
[
7
,
28
]
.

There are three main features of port-Hamiltonian systems worth highlighting. Firstly, the (power-conserving) interconnection of port-Hamiltonian systems is again a port-Hamiltonian system. This is a crucial property and emphasizes the network structure of this framework. Secondly, this framework allows the inclusion of energy-dissipating elements, which is why port-Hamiltonian models are broadly applicable. Lastly, the port-Hamiltonian framework is greatly suitable for control purposes. It allows to model the interaction of the system with its environment by means of external ports which are, in particular, accessible for controller action.

Based on the theory of infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian systems in
[
21
]
, the port-Hamiltonian formulation has been extended to infinite-dimensional systems
[
29
]
. The interconnection structure is generalized to a Stokes-Dirac structure, where the power flow through the boundary of the spatial domain, that is, the interaction with the system’s environment, is encompassed. Just as in the finite-dimensional case, this is essential for the interconnection of several Hamiltonian systems, and it is a good starting point for the purpose of control, hence the name boundary port-Hamiltonian systems. For the analysis of infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems one usually considers abstract Cauchy problems of the form

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=A_{\mathcal{Q}}x(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

\displaystyle x(0)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X,

with
A_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon D(A_{\mathcal{Q}})\subset X\to X
a linear (usually unbounded) operator on some Hilbert space
X
that is associated with the port-Hamiltonian system. This port-Hamiltonian operator stores information concerning the interdomain coupling of the modeled multi-physics system as well as the mechanical and physical properties of the system. More precisely, these operators are usually given by
A_{\mathcal{Q}}=\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{Q}\cdot)
, with
\mathcal{J}
a formally skew-symmetric matrix differential operator modeling the interdomain coupling, and
\mathcal{Q}
a coercive and bounded multiplication operator representing the physical properties. To prove well-posedness and stability results, one applies the well-documented theory of strongly continuous semigroups
[
8
,
12
,
23
]
. The most important class of semigroups in the framework of infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems are contraction semigroups, which are closely related to energy dissipation. Typically, one incorporates dissipation phenomena into the domain of the operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}}
by means of the port variables, and proves that this operator is the infinitesimal generator of a contraction semigroup. For control purposes, it seems natural to let the controls and observations act at the boundary of the spatial domain, leading to the notion of (port-Hamiltonian) boundary control systems
[
13
]
,
[
25
, Chapter 10]
. These systems have been extensively studied in the PhD theses
[
4
,
31
]
.

Due to the great success of this system description, quite recently it was investigated whether the port-Hamiltonian formulation of infinite-dimensional systems can be extended to first-order systems that are coupled by some moving interface
[
10
]
. There are several instances where a system’s spatial domain might be subdivided into areas where the intrinsic physical properties or even the aggregate phase differ. For example, one may consider two distinct gases separated by a moving piston, or liquid-solid phase transitions. The mathematical model of the latter is known as the Stefan problem
[
16
]
. A port-Hamiltonian formulation of the two-phase Stefan problem has already been tested in
[
32
]
. In contrast to boundary port-Hamiltonian systems, this formulation yields an abstract port-Hamiltonian control system with the input being the velocity of the interface, and offers a new field of research in terms of interface control.

In this thesis, we review the port-Hamiltonian formulation of two systems of conservation laws defined on two complementary intervals that are coupled by a moving interface, as elaborated in
[
10
]
, and further examine this class of systems. Our final objective is to collect some useful results concerning the underlying port-Hamiltonian operators by means of the theory of strongly continuous semigroups that may help to prove well-posedness and stability results with respect to the position of the moving interface. The main contributions made in this thesis are the following:

\bullet

We specify the Stokes-Dirac structure of the model proposed in
[
10
]
with respect to a formally skew-symmetric matrix differential operator in case of a fixed interface, see Proposition
17.5
.

\bullet

If we allow for dissipation at the boundary and at the interface position, then the port-Hamiltonian operator associated with two systems of conservation laws that are coupled by a fixed interface is the infinitesimal generator of a contraction semigroup on the respective energy space. In particular, the underlying model is well-defined, see Theorem
19.9
.

\bullet

Under some more restrictive assumption concerning the boundary condition, the generated semigroup is exponentially stable, see Theorem
19.16
.

\bullet

We consider a simplified model which is closely related to the one formulated in
[
10
]
, and present criteria for the stability of the associated family of port-Hamiltonian operators that encompasses the position of the moving interface, see Subsection
19.6
.

2
Outline of this Thesis

The remaining chapters are organized as follows:

Chapter
2
aims to present the basic concepts and notions of port-based modeling. We deal with the port-Hamiltonian formulation of finite-dimensional multi-physics systems, where we restrict ourselves to linear systems. To that end, we introduce the fundamental concept of a power-conserving interconnection structure, called a Dirac structure, and briefly discuss its constituting port variables. This allows us to define the port-Hamiltonian dynamics by means of the underlying Dirac structure.

Chapter
3
covers some well-known notions and results from the literature that are used in the upcoming chapters. We will mainly recall the basics of infinite-dimensional systems theory and, with that said, we will recall the concept of strongly continuous semigroups and some important results that characterize their infinitesimal generators. Furthermore, we will discuss formally adjoint operators of matrix differential operators.

In
Chapter
4
we generalize the port-Hamiltonian approach of Chapter
2
to infinite-dimen- sional systems, where we restrict ourselves to idealized systems with a 1-dimensional spatial domain. We discuss the main differences to finite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems, and show how the power flow at the boundary of the spatial domain is incorporated into the definition of the Dirac structure, leading to the notion of a boundary port-Hamiltonian system. This chapter sets a basis for the discussion in the following chapter.

Chapter
5
is the centerpiece of this thesis and studies the port-Hamiltonian formulation of a system of two conservation laws with a moving interface. The main goal is to provide a well-posed model for a system of two conservation laws on two complementary intervals separated by an interface, where we take the interface’s velocity to be the input variable of the system. It turns out that this formulation poses delicate problems. Therefore, we vastly simplify the original problem, and work out some important properties of the simplified system that may help to further investigate the initial one.

In
Chapter
6
we summarize the covered topics and reflect the main contributions made in this work. Furthermore, we draw our conclusions from the discussion in Chapter
5
and give some comments on open problems and potential future work concerning this rather new class of systems.

Chapter 2
Finite-Dimensional Port-Hamiltonian Systems

In this chapter, we want to study basic concepts of port-based network modeling and provide the definition of finite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems and their underlying Dirac structures. Section
3
is devoted to the description of the main characteristics of port-based network modeling, which originates in the work of Paynter
[
22
]
. To this end, we consider the model of a simple mass-spring system. This motivates to introduce the port-Hamiltonian formulation of multi-physics systems. The dynamics of port-Hamiltonian systems are geometrically specified by so-called Dirac structures. In Section
4
we will introduce this fundamental concept and give some examples of Dirac structures. In the Sections
5
,
6
, and
7
we briefly discuss energy-storing, energy-dissipating, and external ports, respectively. In the finite-dimensional case, these are the constitutive ports of a Dirac structure.
After that, we are finally able to geometrically define the dynamics of port-Hamiltonian systems, which we are going to do in Section
8
. As mentioned in the beginning, the port-Hamiltonian framework is particularly convenient for control purposes. Therefore, we will provide a state-space representation of a huge class of port-Hamiltonian systems that is suitable to be taken as the starting point for investigating control theoretical aspects. We will mainly focus on linear port-Hamiltonian systems.

The whole chapter relies heavily on the Chapters 2, 3, and 4 in
[
27
]
, and on
[
11
, Section 2.2]
.

3
A Motivating Example

In order to motivate the framework of port-based modeling, we start with a simple example.

Example 2.1
(Mass-Spring System; Example 2.1 in
[
27
]
)

Consider a point mass with mass
m\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, moving in one direction and without friction under the influence of a
spring force corresponding to a linear spring with spring constant
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. The usual way of modeling this mass-spring system is by means of the second-order differential equation

\displaystyle m\ddot{z}(t)=-k(z(t)-z_{0}),

(1)

where
z(t)\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the position of the mass at time instant
t\geq 0
, and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is the length of the spring at rest. In order to present the basic principles of port-based network modeling, we consider this problem from a different perspective. The mass-spring system is now regarded as the interconnection of two subsystems storing energy, namely the spring system storing potential energy and the mass system storing kinetic energy, see Figure
1
.

Figure 1
:
Mass-Spring System Regarded as the Interconnection of two Subsystems
[
27
, Figure 2.1]

For the spring system the potential energy is expressed in terms of the elongation
q=z-z_{0}
of the spring. Assuming a linear
spring, according to Hooke’s law the potential energy is given as
E_{\text{pot}}(q)=\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}
. This leads to the spring system equations

\displaystyle\text{spring system}\colon\begin{cases}\dot{q}=-f_{k},\\
e_{k}=\frac{d}{dq}E_{\text{pot}}(q),\end{cases}

where
-f_{k}
denotes the velocity of the endpoint of the spring where
it is attached to the mass, and
e_{k}=kq
describes the spring force at this endpoint.

For the mass system we obtain similar equations. Denoting the momentum of the mass by
p\in\mathbb{R}
, the kinetic
energy is given as
E_{\text{kin}}(p)=\frac{1}{2m}p^{2}
, which leads to the following mass system equations:

\displaystyle\text{mass system}\colon\begin{cases}\dot{p}=-f_{m},\\
e_{m}=\frac{d}{dp}E_{\text{kin}}(p).\end{cases}

Here,
-f_{m}
denotes the force exerted on the mass, and
e_{m}=\frac{p}{m}
is the velocity of the mass.

These separately regarded subsystems are now interconnected through Newton’s third law in the following way:

\displaystyle-f_{k}

\displaystyle=e_{m},

\displaystyle f_{m}

\displaystyle=e_{k}.

The system’s stored energy is given by the Hamiltonian
H\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle H(q,p)=E_{\text{pot}}(q)+E_{\text{kin}}(p)=\frac{1}{2}kq^{2}+\frac{1}{2m}p^{2}.

(2)

The interconnected system can be written in terms of the Hamiltonian as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\dot{q}\\
\dot{p}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial H}{\partial q}(q,p)\\
\frac{\partial H}{\partial p}(q,p)\end{bmatrix}.

(3)

These are the so-called Hamiltonian equations for the mass-spring system. Note that (
3
) is equivalent to the second-order model (
1
) by the correspondence
q=z-z_{0}
: we have

\displaystyle m\ddot{z}=m\ddot{q}=m\dot{\left(\frac{\partial H}{\partial p}(q,p)\right)}=m\dot{\frac{p}{m}}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial q}(q,p)=-kq=-k(z-z_{0}).

\Diamond

Remark 2.2

The reason for the minus sign in front of
f_{k}
in Example
2.1
is that we want the product
f_{k}e_{k}
between the spring’s velocity and force to be the incoming power with respect to the interconnection. The same applies for the product
f_{m}e_{m}
between the exerted force on the mass and its velocity. This sign convention will be adopted throughout this chapter.

Despite its simplicity, Example
2.1
already displays some of the
main characteristics of port-based network modeling: we regard the system as the coupling of the energy-storing spring system with the energy-storing mass system through a power-conserving interconnection where the power is routed from the mass system
to the spring system and vice versa. This approach can be applied to general multi-physics systems, i.e., systems comprising different physical domains.

More generally, in port-based modeling a physical system is considered as the interconnection of three different types of ideal components:

(i)

Energy-storing elements:
typical examples of energy-storing elements are ideal inductors (by means of the magnetic field), capacitors (by means of the electric field), masses, and springs.

(ii)

Energy-dissipating
(or
resistive
) elements: examples of elements corresponding to energy-dissipation (due to friction, resistance, etc.) are resistors and dampers.

(iii)

Energy-routing elements:
elements such as gyrators and transformers only redirect the power flow in the overall system
[
22
, Part XV]
.

Figure 2:
Port-Hamiltonian System
[
27
, Figure 2.2]

For the port-Hamiltonian formulation the energy-storing elements will be grouped into a single object denoted by
\mathcal{S}
(’storage’), and correspondingly the energy-dissipating elements
are grouped into a single object denoted by
\mathcal{R}
(’resistive’). Moreover, the
interconnection of all energy-routing elements can be considered as one energy-routing structure denoted by
\mathcal{D}
, which we will call the system’s underlying
Dirac structure
.

The principle of port-Hamiltonian systems modeling is schematically illustrated in Figure
2
: the energy-storing elements
\mathcal{S}
and the energy-dissipating elements
\mathcal{R}
are linked to a central interconnection (energy-routing) structure
\mathcal{D}
. This linking is realized via pairs
(f,e)
of vectors of flow and effort variables of equal dimension. We call a pair
(f,e)
of vectors of flow and effort variables a port, and the total set of variables
f,e
is called the set of port variables. The physical meaning of efforts and flows in various physical domains is listed in Table
1
.

Physical Domain

f\in\mathcal{F}

e\in\mathcal{E}

force
F

force
F

v

angular velocity
\omega

T

torque
T

\omega

volume flow
\phi

pressure
p

kinetic hydraulic

pressure
p

\phi

chemical potential
\mu

f_{N}

thermal

temperature
T

f_{S}

Table 1:
Flows and Efforts Corresponding to their Physical Domains
[
11
, Table 1.2]

Figure
2
shows three ports: the port
(f_{S},e_{S})
linking to energy-storage, the port
(f_{R},e_{R})
corresponding to energy-dissipation, and the
external port
(f_{P},e_{P})
, by which the system interacts with its environment.

In the following sections, we will provide more details about the fundamental notion of a Dirac structure on finite-dimensional spaces. To this end, we discuss the constituting ports of Dirac structures in the finite-dimensional case. We will treat successively energy-storing elements, energy-dissipating (resistive) elements, and
external ports. After a short comment on state choice of different physical domains, we finally get to the basic geometric definition of a port-Hamiltonian system. Subsequently, we will present some simple yet important examples of such systems.

4
Dirac Structures

The key property of a Dirac structure is power conservation, meaning that it links the port variables in such a way that the total power entering or leaving a Dirac structure always amounts to zero. For the definition of a Dirac structure, we need to define the spaces of flows and efforts as well as the power flow defined on these spaces. We denote by
\mathcal{F}
some finite-dimensional linear
space of flows
. Its elements will be denoted by
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and are called
flow
(or
energy
)
variables
. The
space of effort
(or
co-energy
)
variables
is given by the dual linear space
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and its elements are denoted by
e\in\mathcal{E}
. The total space of flow and effort variables is
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
, and is called the
space of port variables
or the
bond space
. The power on the total space of port variables is defined by

\displaystyle P\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 14.22636pt(f,e)\mapsto\langle e\mid f\rangle,

(4)

where
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
is the so-called
power pairing
. In the finite-dimensional case the power pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle
equals the natural pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{F}}
on the respective vector space, see Section
9
. Usually, one may take
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
and thus
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and the power pairing equals the standard inner product:

\displaystyle\langle e\mid f\rangle=\langle e,f\rangle_{2},\hskip 14.22636pt(f,e)\in\mathcal{B}.

With the data provided in Table
1
one may check that the (inner) product of flow and effort variables indeed equals power. However, we note that there are interesting cases where it is beneficial not to take
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
. For instance, in three-dimensional mechanical systems one usually works with Lie algebras of different matrix groups (see
[
21
, Section 1.4]
). For further details we refer to
[
27
, Chapter 3]
. We will see in the Chapters
4
and
5
that in the infinite-dimensional case, the power pairing does, in general, not coincide with the natural pairing.

Now we are able to define the notion of a Dirac structure in the finite-dimensional case.

Definition 2.3
(Dirac Structure)

Consider a finite-dimensional linear space of flows
\mathcal{F}
and its dual space of efforts
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Let
P
be the power mapping on the bond space
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
given by (
4
). A linear subspace
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}
is called a (constant) Dirac structure if

(i)

P_{|\mathcal{D}}\equiv 0
, i.e.,
P(f,e)=0
for all
(f,e)\in\mathcal{D}
, and

(ii)

\dim(\mathcal{D})=\dim(\mathcal{F})
.

Property
(i)
in Definition
2.3
corresponds to power conservation, and states the previously mentioned
fact that the total power flow always equals zero. It can be shown that the maximal dimension of any subspace
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}
satisfying property
(i)
is equal to
\dim(\mathcal{F})
. This claim follows immediately from an equivalent definition of a Dirac structure, which we will state next. Another advantage of this equivalent definition of a Dirac structure is that it can be generalized to the case of an infinite-dimensional vector space
\mathcal{F}
, leading to the definition of an infinite-dimensional Dirac structure, as we will see in Chapter
4
.

In order to give this equivalent characterization of a Dirac structure, we define a canonical bilinear form
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
on the space
\mathcal{B}
which is closely related to the power pairing:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg\colon\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}&\to\mathbb{R},\\
((f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2}))&\mapsto\hskip 2.84544pt\ll(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\gg\hskip 2.84544pt:=\langle e^{1}\mid f^{2}\rangle+\langle e^{2}\mid f^{1}\rangle.\end{split}

(5)

This bilinear form is
non-degenerate
, that is, if
\ll(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\gg=0
for all
(f^{2},e^{2})\in\mathcal{B}
, then necessarily
(f^{1},e^{1})=0
. However, it is not positive definite. We call this bilinear form a
plus pairing
.

We will now show that the following equivalent definition of a Dirac structure indeed satisfies the properties stated in Definition
2.3
.

Proposition 2.4
(Proposition 2.1 in
[
27
]
)

Consider a finite-dimensional linear space of flows
\mathcal{F}
and its dual space of efforts
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Let
P
be the power mapping on the bond space
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
given by (
4
). A linear subspace
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}
is a Dirac structure if and only if

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp},

(6)

where
\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the plus pairing
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
.

Proof 4.1
.

”
\Rightarrow
”: Let
\mathcal{D}
be a Dirac structure in the sense of Definition
2.3
. Let
(f^{1},e^{1})
,
(f^{2},e^{2})\in\mathcal{D}
be arbitrary port variables. By linearity, we have
(f^{1}+f^{2},e^{1}+e^{2})\in\mathcal{D}
. By virtue of property
(i)
in Definition
2.3
, we conclude that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1}+e^{2}\mid f^{1}+f^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1}\mid f^{1}\rangle+\langle e^{1}\mid f^{2}\rangle+\langle e^{2}\mid f^{1}\rangle+\langle e^{2}\mid f^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1}\mid f^{2}\rangle+\langle e^{2}\mid f^{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\hskip 5.69046pt\ll(e^{1},f^{1}),(e^{2},f^{2})\gg,

whence
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
. Moreover, as the plus pairing
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
is non-degenerate, we have

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp})=\dim(\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E})-\dim(\mathcal{D})=2\dim(\mathcal{F})-\dim(\mathcal{D}),

and due to property
(ii)
in Definition
2.3
, it follows that
\dim(\mathcal{D})=\dim(\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp})
and thus
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
.

”
\Leftarrow
”: Let
\mathcal{D}
satisfy (
6
). Then for every
(f,e)\in\mathcal{D}
we have in particular that

\displaystyle 0=\ll(f,e),(f,e)\gg=2\langle e\mid f\rangle=2P(f,e).

Thus, property
(i)
is satisfied. Again, as the plus pairing
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
is non-degenerate, we obtain

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp})=2\dim(\mathcal{F})-\dim(\mathcal{D}).

(7)

Equation (
6
) now implies that
\dim(\mathcal{D})=\dim(\mathcal{F})
, and hence property
(ii)
of a Dirac structure is satisfied as well.

In order to prove the alternative characterization, we first note that we have shown that property
(i)
implies
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
. Together with equation (
7
) this yields that any subspace
\mathcal{D}
satisfying property
(i)
has the property that
\dim(\mathcal{D})\leq\dim(\mathcal{F})
. In conclusion, a Dirac structure indeed is the linear subspace of maximal dimension satisfying property
(i)
of Definition
2.3
.

There are several examples of constant Dirac structures. We will present only a few of them and refer to
[
27
, Section 2.2]
for more.

Example 4.2

(Dirac Structures; cf. Section 2.2 in
[
27
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Consider the following examples of Dirac structures:

1.

Let
J\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{F},

e\mapsto f=Je
, with
J\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
a skew-symmetric matrix, i.e.,
J=-J^{\top}
. Then one can readily show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{J}:=\gra(-J):=\left\{(f,e)\in\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}\mid-f=Je\right\}

is a Dirac structure. According to Proposition
2.4
, we need to show that
\mathcal{D}_{J}=\mathcal{D}_{J}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
with respect to
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
defined in (
5
). Due to skew-symmetry of
J
, for
(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\in\mathcal{D}_{J}
we have

\displaystyle\ll(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\gg

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1},f^{2}\rangle_{2}+\langle e^{2},f^{1}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{2},-Je^{1}\rangle_{2}+\langle e^{1},-Je^{2}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1},Je^{2}\rangle_{2}-\langle e^{1},Je^{2}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=0.

We conclude that
\mathcal{D}_{J}\subset\mathcal{D}_{J}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
. Conversely, let
(f,e)\in\mathcal{D}_{J}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
, that is, for all
(f^{1},e^{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{J}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\ll(f^{1},e^{1}),(f,e)\gg

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1},f\rangle_{2}+\langle e,f^{1}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1},f\rangle_{2}+\langle e,-Je^{1}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{1},f\rangle_{2}+\langle e^{1},Je\rangle_{2}.

Consequently, for this equation to hold for all
(f^{1},e^{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{J}
, we need
f=-Je
, whence
(f,e)\in\mathcal{D}_{J}
and therefore
\mathcal{D}_{J}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}\subset\mathcal{D}_{J}
. Altogether,
\mathcal{D}_{J}=\mathcal{D}_{J}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{J}
is a Dirac structure.

As we will see, graphs of skew-symmetric linear maps in the finite-dimensional case, and (formally) skew-symmetric matrix differential operators in the infinite-dimensional case play a substantial role in the port-Hamiltonian framework.

2.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{F}
be any subspace. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\bot}:=\left\{e\in\mathcal{E}\mid\langle e\mid f\rangle=0\hskip 2.84544pt\text{for all }f\in\mathcal{K}\right\}.

Then
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{K}^{\bot}
is a so-called separable Dirac structure (cf.
[
27
, Definition 2.2]
), i.e., for all
(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\displaystyle\langle e^{1}\mid f^{2}\rangle=0.

One can show that any separable Dirac structure is of the form
\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{K}^{\bot}
for an appropriately chosen subspace
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{F}
, see
[
27
, Proposition 2.2]
. A typical example for a separable Dirac structure is given by transformers. A transformer is an element linking two scalar bonds with flow and effort variables
(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle f^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha f^{1},

\displaystyle e^{1}

\displaystyle=-\alpha e^{2},

with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
the transformer ratio. More precisely, let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and set
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}=\left\{(f,\alpha f)\in\mathcal{F}\mid f\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
. It is clear that
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{\bot}=\left\{(-\alpha e,e)\in\mathcal{E}\mid e\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
, and that
\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{\bot})=\dim(\mathcal{F})=2
, which is why
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}\times\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}^{\bot}
is indeed a separable Dirac structure. This can be easily generalized to the multi-port case.

\Diamond

As illustrated in Figure
2
, the Dirac structure describes how energy is exchanged among various components in a physical system. In the following, we want to specify the port variables that constitute a (finite-dimensional) Dirac structure. We will give an overview concerning port variables related to energy-storing and energy-dissipating system elements as well as external ports.

5
Energy-Storing Elements

The energy-storing multi-port element
\mathcal{S}
unifies all energy-storing elements of the system. The port variables of the Dirac structure associated with
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{F}_{S}\times\mathcal{E}_{S}
will be denoted by
(f_{S},e_{S})\in\mathcal{S}
. The total energy storage of the system is defined by a Hamiltonian function
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
on the state space
X
. We restrict ourselves to the case where the state space is given by
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In general, the state space is a smooth (finite-dimensional) manifold, see
[
27
, Chapter 3]
,
[
30
]
. In Chapter
4
we will discuss the extension to the infinite-dimensional case.

Before providing more details about energy-storing multi-port elements, we want to clarify briefly how the state variables have to be chosen in various physical domains, as they are closely related to the flows and efforts. In the common classification of domains, many domains are characterized by two types of states, namely the
generalized displacement

q\in X
and the
generalized momentum

p\in X^{\prime}
. Depending on the physical domain, one obtains the generalized displacement
q
by time-integration of the flow variable
f\in\mathcal{F}
, whereas the generalized momentum
p
refers to time-integration of the effort variable
e\in\mathcal{E}
. Table
2
is an extension of Table
1
and gives a summary of the generalized states in the respective physical subdomain. For more background information concerning this topic, we recommend the introductory chapter in
[
11
]
.

Effort

e\in\mathcal{E}

p=\int e\,dt

electric

current
I

V

charge
Q

\lambda

v

\theta

\Gamma

\mu

Table 2:
Flows, Efforts, and Generalized State Variables in Different Physical Domains

[
11
, Table 1.1]

The vector of flow variables is given by the rate
\dot{x}
of the state
x\in X
. Thus, for any (time-dependent) state
x\in X
the flow vector
\dot{x}
will be an element of the linear space
\mathcal{F}_{S}=T_{x}X
, the
tangent space
of
X
at
x\in X
. In the given case
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the tangent space
T_{x}X
may be identified with
X
.

Furthermore, the vector of effort variables of the energy-storing multi-port element is given by the gradient vector
\nabla H(x)\in\mathcal{E}_{S}=T_{x}^{\prime}X
, the dual space of the tangent space
T_{x}X
, which again may be identified with
X^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that we define
\nabla H(x)
,
x\in X
, to be a column vector instead of a row vector. This convention will be applied throughout this work.

Now, in case of a time-dependent state
x(t)\in X
,
t\geq 0
, we obtain the following power-balance for the energy-storing multi-port element:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(x(t))=\nabla H^{\top}(x(t))\dot{x}(t).

(8)

In the present case, the bond space is given by
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{F}_{S}\times\mathcal{E}_{S}
, and the power-pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{E}_{S}\times\mathcal{F}_{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}
. The interconnection of the energy-storing elements to the storage port
(f_{S},e_{S})\in X\times X^{\prime}
of some Dirac structure
\mathcal{D}\subset X\times X^{\prime}
is done by setting

\displaystyle f_{S}=-\dot{x}\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pte_{S}=\nabla H(x).

(9)

Note that the minus sign in (
9
) has been placed in order to have a consistent power flow convention:
\nabla H^{\top}(x)\dot{x}
is the power flowing into the energy-storing elements, whereas
e_{S}^{\top}f_{S}
is the power flowing into the Dirac structure. Hence, with (
4
) and (
9
) the power-balance (
8
) can also be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(x(t))=-e_{S}^{\top}(t)f_{S}(t)=-\langle e_{S}(t)\mid f_{S}(t)\rangle=0,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0,

(10)

provided that
(f_{S}(t),e_{S}(t))\in\mathcal{D}
for
t\geq 0
. Equation (
10
) then expresses power conservation of the system.

6
Energy-Dissipating Elements

The second multi-port element
\mathcal{R}
corresponds to internal energy dissipation, and its port variables are
denoted by
(f_{R},e_{R})
. These port variables are terminated on a static energy-dissipating (resistive) relation
\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{R}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathcal{E}_{R}=\mathcal{F}_{R}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the dual space of
\mathcal{F}_{R}
. In general, a resistive relation
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}
is a set of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\left\{(f_{R},e_{R})\in\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}\mid R(f_{R},e_{R})=0,\hskip 2.84544pt\langle e_{R},f_{R}\rangle_{2}\leq 0\right\}.

(11)

We will call the subset
\mathcal{R}
an
energy-dissipating relation
or a
resistive structure
. If we restrict ourselves to linear resistive structures, the mapping
R\colon\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}\to\mathbb{R}
will be of the form

\displaystyle R(f_{R},e_{R})=R_{f}f_{R}+R_{e}e_{R},

where
R_{f},R_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
. In order to guarantee that the second constitutive relation in (
11
) is satisfied whenever the first one is, we need to impose the following conditions on those matrices:

(i)

R_{f}R_{e}^{\top}=R_{e}R_{f}^{\top}\geq 0
and

(ii)

\rank\left(\begin{bmatrix}R_{f}&R_{e}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\dim(\mathcal{F}_{R})=m
.

If these conditions are satisfied, then we can equivalently rewrite the first constitutive condition in (
11
) as the requirement that there exists some vector
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\displaystyle f_{R}=R_{e}^{\top}\lambda\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pte_{R}=-R_{f}^{\top}\lambda.

It is easy to check now that under these conditions we have
\langle e_{R},f_{R}\rangle_{2}\leq 0
, and hence the second constitutive condition in (
11
) can be omitted. Altogether, a linear resistive structure is of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\left\{(f_{R},e_{R})\in\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}\mid R_{f}f_{R}+R_{e}e_{R}=0\right\}

(12)

with suitably defined matrices
R_{f},R_{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
. For a system with energy-storing and resistive elements, the bond space is given by
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}=(X\times\mathcal{F}_{R})\times(X^{\prime}\times\mathcal{E}_{R})
, and the power pairing again equals the natural inner product. By definition, the Dirac structure
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}
of such a system satisfies the power-balance

\displaystyle\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,\Bigg|\,\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{R}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle=e_{S}^{\top}f_{S}+e_{R}^{\top}f_{R}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\left(\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{R}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{D}.

(13)

This leads, by substitution of (
10
) and (
13
), to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(x(t))=-e_{S}^{\top}(t)f_{S}(t)=e_{R}^{\top}(t)f_{R}(t)\leq 0,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

(14)

This inequality expresses the fact that the internally stored energy (the Hamiltonian) monotonically decreases in the presence of resistive elements.

An important special case of energy-dissipating relations occurs when
the resistive relation can be expressed as

\displaystyle f_{R}=-F(e_{R}),

where
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfies
e_{R}^{\top}F(e_{R})\geq 0
for all
e_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. For linear resistive elements, we have
F(e_{R})=Re_{R}
with
R\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
being symmetric and positive semi-definite. A typical example of a linear resistive structure is provided by a linear damper in a spring-mass-damper system, where
\mathcal{R}_{d}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{d}=\{(f_{R},e_{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid f_{R}=-de_{R}\},

(15)

and where
d\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the damping constant.

7
External Ports

The
external port

(f_{P},e_{P})\in\mathcal{F}_{P}\times\mathcal{E}_{P}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{P}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{P}=\mathcal{F}_{P}^{\prime}
, models the interaction of the system with its environment. This comprises different situations. For instance, there are port variables which are accessible for controller action (see
[
11
, Chapter 5]
,
[
26
, Chapter 7]
), or there is an external port describing the interaction with the physical environment. Another important type of external port variables corresponds to source connections, as it is often the case in electrical circuits. For instance, in an electrical circuit with voltage source the system’s input is the voltage of the source, and the current through the source is set to be the resulting output variable. If one wishes to distinguish between these types of external ports, which in some cases may even be inevitable, one can divide the external port into a control port and an interaction port (cf.
[
11
, Subsection 2.2.1]
). However, we will not distinguish between them.

Now, taking the external port into account, for a Dirac structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\subset X\times\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{F}_{P}\times X^{\prime}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{P}

the power-balance (
13
) extends to

\displaystyle\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\\
e_{P}\end{pmatrix}\,\Bigg|\,\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{R}\\
f_{P}\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle=e_{S}^{\top}f_{S}+e_{R}^{\top}f_{R}+e_{P}^{\top}f_{P}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\left(\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{R}\\
f_{P}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\\
e_{P}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{D},

and (
14
) accordingly extends to

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(x(t))=-e_{S}^{\top}(t)f_{S}(t)=e_{R}^{\top}(t)f_{R}(t)+e_{P}^{\top}(t)f_{P}(t)\leq e_{P}^{\top}(t)f_{P}(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

This inequality states that the increase of the internally stored energy is always less
than or equal to the externally supplied power.

8
Port-Hamiltonian Dynamics

Now that we have discussed the constitutive ports of a Dirac structure and the corresponding power-balance, we want to present the underlying geometrical structure of port-Hamiltonian systems. We will begin with the definition of linear port-Hamiltonian systems by means of Dirac structures, and give a more familiar representation of a big class of such systems. At the end of this section, we will give some final remarks regarding non-linear port-Hamiltonian systems and port-Hamiltonian differential algebraic systems.

We begin with the definition of linear port-Hamiltonian systems with respect to an underlying Dirac structure.

Definition 8.1

(Linear Port-Hamiltonian System)
.

Consider the state space
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a Hamiltoninan
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
defining energy storage. Furthermore, let
\mathcal{F}_{R}=\mathcal{E}_{R}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathcal{F}_{P}=\mathcal{E}_{P}=\mathbb{R}^{k}
. A linear port-Hamiltonian system on
X
is defined as a triple
(\mathcal{D},H,\mathcal{R})
, consisting of a Dirac structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}=(X\times\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{F}_{P})\times(X^{\prime}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{P})

defined with respect to the power pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
, with energy-storing ports
(f_{S},e_{S})\in X\times X^{\prime}
, resistive ports
(f_{R},e_{R})\in\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}
, external ports
(f_{P},e_{P})\in\mathcal{F}_{p}\times\mathcal{E}_{P}
, the Hamiltonian
H
, and a linear resistive structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{F}_{R}\times\mathcal{E}_{R}

of the form (
12
). Its dynamics are geometrically specified by the requirement that for all
t\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\dot{x}(t)\\
f_{R}(t)\\
f_{P}(t)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\nabla H(x(t))\\
e_{R}(t)\\
e_{P}(t)\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{D}\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt(f_{R}(t),e_{R}(t))\in\mathcal{R}.

At the beginning of Chapter
2
, we have already seen how a mass-spring system is modeled as a port-Hamiltonian system. We revisit this example to show that this is indeed a linear port-Hamiltonian system in the sense of Definition
8.1
.

Example 8.2

(Mass-spring System Revisited)
.

Consider the mass-spring system from Example
2.1
. The dynamics of this system are defined by equation (
3
). Defining the state space
X=\mathbb{R}^{2}
with state vector
x=\begin{bmatrix}q\\
p\end{bmatrix}
, and taking
\mathcal{F}_{S}=\mathcal{E}_{S}=X
, one immediately sees that the underlying Dirac structure of this system is given by the graph of the skew-symmetric matrix
-J\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
with

\displaystyle J=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix},\text{ i.e.,}\hskip 8.5359pt\mathcal{D}_{J}=\{(f_{S},e_{S})\in\mathcal{F}_{S}\times\mathcal{E}_{S}\mid-f_{S}=Je_{S}\}.

Here we have
-f_{S}=\begin{bmatrix}\dot{q}\\
\dot{p}\end{bmatrix}
and
e_{S}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial H}{\partial q}\\
\frac{\partial H}{\partial p}\end{bmatrix}
, where
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
is the Hamiltonian given by (
2
). Note that the matrix
J
is the one given on the right-hand side in (
3
). As there are neither external ports nor resistive elements, the dynamics of this linear port-Hamiltonian system is geometrically specified by

\displaystyle(f_{S}(t),e_{S}(t))=(-\dot{x}(t),\nabla H(x(t)))\in\mathcal{D},\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

Moreover, by skew-symmetry of
J
, the power-balance as expected yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(q(t),p(t))=\left\langle\begin{pmatrix}kq(t)\\
\frac{p(t)}{m}\end{pmatrix}\Bigg|\begin{pmatrix}\dot{q}(t)\\
\dot{p}(t)\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle=-\langle e_{S}(t)\mid f_{S}(t)\rangle=0,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

\Diamond

A state-space representation, as established for the mass-spring system in Example
2.1
, is quite common. More general, an important class of linear port-Hamiltonian systems is obtained by considering Dirac structures which are the graph of a skew-symmetric map of the form

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\\
e_{P}\\
\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{R}\\
f_{P}\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}-J&-G_{R}&-G\\
G_{R}^{\top}&0&0\\
G^{\top}&0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}e_{S}\\
e_{R}\\
e_{P}\\
\end{bmatrix},

with
J=-J^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, a linear dissipation relation
f_{R}=-\bar{R}e_{R}
for some positive-semidefinite matrix
\bar{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, and
G_{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
,
G\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. This yields so-called
input-state-output port-Hamiltonian systems
of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}&=\left[J-R\right]\nabla H(x)+Gu,\\
y&=G^{\top}\nabla H(x),\end{split}

(16)

with
R:=G_{R}\bar{R}G_{R}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and with
u=e_{P}
the input vector and
y=f_{P}
the output vector. This is a convenient representation for the investigation of control related problems for port-Hamiltonian systems, see
[
11
, Chapter 5]
,
[
26
, Chapter 7]
,
[
27
, Chapter 15]
.

The following example aims to emphasize port-based network modeling of multi-physics systems. We also want to point to Example 2.5 in
[
27
]
, where a port-Hamiltonian formulation for the dynamics of a DC motor is derived.

Example 8.3

(Levitated Ball System; Example 2.4 in
[
27
]
)
.

Figure 3
:
Magnetically Levitated Ball of Example
8.3

[
27
, Figure 2.4]

Consider the dynamics of an iron ball that is levitated by the magnetic field of a controlled inductor, as schematically depicted in Figure
3
. The Hamiltonian
H\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
of this system - with
q\in\mathbb{R}
the height of the ball,
m>0
the ball’s mass,
p\in\mathbb{R}
the vertical momentum,
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}
the magnetic flux-linkage of the inductor,
R_{c}>0
the coil’s resistance,
g>0
the gravitational constant,
L(\cdot)
the inductance depending on the height
q
,
V\in\mathbb{R}
the voltage across the inductor, and
I\in\mathbb{R}
the current through the inductor - is defined as

\displaystyle H(q,p,\varphi)=E_{\text{pot}}(q)+E_{\text{kin}}(p)+E_{\text{mag}}(q,\varphi)=mgq+\frac{p^{2}}{2m}+\frac{\varphi^{2}}{2L(q)}.

The system dynamics are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}\dot{q}\\
\dot{p}\\
\dot{\varphi}\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&-R_{c}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial H}{\partial q}\\
\frac{\partial H}{\partial p}\\
\frac{\partial H}{\partial\varphi}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}V,\\
I&=\frac{\partial H}{\partial\varphi}.\end{split}

(17)

Here,
e_{P}=V
denotes the input and
f_{P}=I
denotes the system’s output. Furthermore, the matrices
J
,
R\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
, and
G\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
from (
16
) are given by

\displaystyle J=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
-1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},\hskip 8.5359ptR=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&R_{c}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359ptG=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}.

While the system description (
17
) may at first glance indicate that the mechanical and the magnetic part of the given system are decoupled, they are actually coupled, namely via the Hamiltonian
H
: the magnetic energy
E_{\text{mag}}
depends both on the flux
\varphi
and the mechanical variable
q
. Consequently, the evolution of the (mechanical) momentum variable
p
depends on the magnetic variable
\varphi
, and the evolution of the magnetic variable
\varphi
depends on the mechanical variable
q
.

\Diamond

Needless to say, there exist nonlinear port-Hamiltonian systems as well. The major difference to linear port-Hamiltonian systems is that the state space
X
becomes a smooth manifold, and the Dirac structure is not constant anymore, but is modulated by the state variables. Moreover, the spaces
\mathcal{F}_{S}
and
\mathcal{E}_{S}
become the tangent space and the cotangent space, respectively, as mentioned in Section
5
. Furthermore, the input-state-output description (
16
) of nonlinear port-Hamiltonian systems becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}&=[J(x)-R(x)]\nabla H(x)+G(x)u,\\
y&=G^{\top}(x)\nabla H(x).\end{split}

(18)

For details and some examples we refer to
[
26
, Chapter 6]
,
[
27
, Chapter 3]
, and the references specified therein.

When modeling dynamical systems, the appearance of algebraic equations next to differential equations is ubiquitous. For instance, in network modeling of physical systems, the interconnections between different dynamical components of the overall system almost unavoidably lead to algebraic constraints. Such systems are called
linear port-Hamiltonian descriptor systems
(
port-Hamiltonian differential-algebraic systems
), or short
pHDAEs

[
6
]
. The geometric definition of port-Hamiltonian systems is quite handy, as it can even characterize the dynamics of pHDAEs by choosing the underlying Dirac structure suitably. This is the reason why in Definition
8.1
one usually refers to the triple
(\mathcal{D},H,\mathcal{R})
as a (linear) port-Hamiltonian differential-algebraic system.

In this chapter, we have presented the basic concepts and main characteristics of the port-Hamiltonian framework on finite-dimensional spaces. We have learned that the centerpiece of this approach is the power-conserving interconnection structure
\mathcal{D}
, called the Dirac structure. We have presented the basic definition of a Dirac structure and discussed its constituting ports in the finite-dimensional case, namely ports associated with energy-storing and energy-dissipating elements as well as external ports. Lastly, we have geometrically specified the dynamics of linear port-Hamiltonian systems by means of their underlying Dirac structures and have given a state-space representation for a huge class of port-Hamiltonian systems.

Now that we have gained a basic understanding of port-based modeling of physical systems, we want to study infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems in the upcoming chapters, to finally deal with the main topic of this thesis, namely the port-Hamiltonian formulation of a system of two conservation laws with a moving interface. In order to do so, we need to provide some background information concerning semigroup theory, infinite-dimensional systems theory, the concept of admissibility, and evolution equations. We will treat these subjects in the next chapter.

Chapter 3
Some Background on Functional Analysis and Systems Theory

In preparation for the main part of this thesis, we recall some well-known concepts and results on selected topics. Most of the time, we will give no proofs, but provide references regarding the respective topics. Furthermore, we will omit various topics that are usually covered in the literature, and only focus on aspects that are useful for the discussion in the following chapters.

This chapter is organized as follows. In Section
9
we recall the definition of the Hilbert space adjoint of a densely defined operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
. Moreover, we introduce the notion of the formally adjoint operator of a matrix differential operator, and discuss the difference between the Hilbert space adjoint and the formal adjoint. As we will deal with infinite-dimensional (control) systems, we need a tool for the analysis of such systems. In Section
10
we are going to study strongly continuous semigroups and characterize their infinitesimal generators. In Section
11
we cover abstract (i.e. Hilbert space-valued) linear differential equations and give sufficient and necessary conditions for well-posedness of such systems. We will see that the framework of strongly continuous semigroups is a powerful tool for the analysis of so-called abstract Cauchy problems. Afterwards, we are going to shortly discuss infinite-dimensional control systems in Section
12
. For a proper treatment of such systems, we need to introduce the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
and the concept of admissible control operators. We will give criteria for when the mild solution of an abstract control system is a continuous
X
-valued function. Section
13
is the last section of this chapter and deals with linear, time-variant evolution problems. We will study (two-parameter) evolution systems and define the notion of stability for a family of infinitesimal generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups. We assume that the reader is acquainted with Riemann integrals of vector-valued functions
[
19
, Section 3.1]
, the Lebesgue theory of vector-valued measurable functions, and Bochner intergrals
[
1
, Section 1.1]
.

Unless stated otherwise, throughout this chapter we assume that
X
is a real or complex separable Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X}
.

9
Adjoint and Formally Adjoint Operators

In this section, we want to define and highlight the difference of the Hilbert space adjoint of a matrix differential operator to its formal adjoint, and provide a simple example to illustrate this difference. For a detailed, more general discussion on formally adjoint operators we refer to
[
3
, Chapter 13]
.

For the first part of this section, we follow
[
25
, Section 2.9]
.

Definition 9.1
.

Let
V
and
Z
be Hilbert spaces. An operator
J\in\mathcal{L}(V,Z)
is called an isomorphism, or a unitary operator, from
V
to
Z
, if we have

\displaystyle\langle Jv,Jw\rangle_{Z}=\langle v,w\rangle_{V},\hskip 14.22636ptv,w\in V.

One may verify that
J\in\mathcal{L}(V,Z)
is unitary if and only if
\|Jv\|_{Z}=\|v\|_{V}
holds for all
v\in V
, and
\ran(J)=Z
.

For any Hilbert space
V
, we denote by
\langle z,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}
the functional
z\in V^{\prime}
applied to
v\in V
, so that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}
is linear in the first component and antilinear in the second component. This pairing is called the
natural (duality) pairing
on
V
. The norm on
V^{\prime}
is defined as

\displaystyle\|z\|_{V^{\prime}}=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v\in V}}{{\|v\|_{V}=1}}}|\langle z,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}|,\hskip 14.22636ptz\in V^{\prime}.

There is an operator
J_{R}\colon V\to V^{\prime}
satisfying

\displaystyle\langle J_{R}v,w\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}=\langle v,w\rangle_{V},\hskip 14.22636ptv,w\in V.

According to the Riesz representation theorem,
J_{R}
is an isomorphism. We say that we identify the Hilbert space
V
with its dual
V^{\prime}
, if we do not distinguish between
v
and
J_{R}v
for all
v\in V
.

In the following, we identify
X
with its dual
X^{\prime}
. Consider a linear and densely defined operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
. We wish to associate a
Hilbert space adjoint

A^{\ast}
to
A
. Its domain is given by

\displaystyle D(A^{\ast}):=\left\{y\in X\mid\langle A\cdot,y\rangle_{X}\colon D(A)\to\mathbb{R},\,x\mapsto\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}\text{ is continuous}\right\}.

If
y\in D(A^{\ast})
, then the Hahn-Banach theorem extends the functional

\displaystyle x\mapsto\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}

to a continuous linear functional on
X
. The Riesz representation theorem yields that there exists a unique element
z_{y}\in X
such that this functional has the form

\displaystyle\langle\cdot,z_{y}\rangle_{X}.

For
y\in D(A^{\ast})
we define
A^{\ast}y=z_{y}
. Hence, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,A^{\ast}y\rangle_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A),\,y\in D(A^{\ast}).

In other words, the domain of the Hilbert space adjoint
A^{\ast}
of an operator
A
is given by

\displaystyle D(A^{\ast})=\left\{y\in X\mid\exists!\,z_{y}\in X\colon\forall x\in D(A)\colon\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,z_{y}\rangle_{X}\right\}.

(19)

The following definitions can be found in Section 3.2 and Section 3.7 in
[
25
]
, and in
[
8
, Appendix A.3.2]
.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
and
B\colon D(B)\subset X\to X
be linear and densely defined.

(i)

A
is called symmetric, if

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,Ay\rangle_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in D(A).

(ii)

A
is called skew-symmetric, if

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=-\langle x,Ay\rangle_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in D(A).

(iii)

If for all
x\in D(A)
,
y\in D(B)
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,By\rangle_{X},

then
B\subset A^{\ast}
, that is,
D(B)\subset D(A^{\ast})
and
By=A^{\ast}y
for all
y\in D(B)
.

(iv)

A
is called self-adjoint if
A^{\ast}=A
, that is,
D(A)=D(A^{\ast})
and
Ax=A^{\ast}x
for all
x\in D(A)
.

(v)

A
is called skew-adjoint if
A^{\ast}=-A
.

(vi)

A
is called coercive if it is self-adjoint and there exists some
m>0
such that
A\geq mI_{X}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle Ax,x\rangle_{X}\geq m\|x\|_{X}^{2},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

For the rest of this section, we restrict ourselves to matrix differential operators defined on a Sobolev space. For this type of operators, we want to introduce the notion of
formally adjoint operators
. Roughly speaking, the only difference to the Hilbert space adjoint is that the constitutive relations of its domain are relaxed in a way such that

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,A^{\ast}y\rangle_{X}\hskip 5.69046pt+\text{boundary terms},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A),\,y\in D(A^{\ast}).

The discussion in Section
15
will clarify the necessity of allowing boundary terms concerning the relation between an operator and its formal adjoint.

Now, let
X=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})
be endowed with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle x,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,y\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{a}^{b}y^{\top}(z)x(z)\,dz,\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in X.

Let us define the Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\,\big|\,x\text{ is absolutely continuous},\,\frac{dx}{dz}\in X\right\},

\displaystyle H^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\,\big|\,x\text{ is absolutely continuous},\,\frac{dx}{dz}\in H^{N-1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\},\hskip 14.22636ptN\geq 2,

as well as

\displaystyle H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\mid x(a)=x(b)=0\right\},

\displaystyle H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in H^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap H_{0}^{N-1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\,\big|\,\frac{dx}{dz}\in H_{0}^{N-1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\right\},\hskip 14.22636ptN\geq 2.

Furthermore, define the
boundary trace operator

\trace\colon H^{N}\left([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\to\mathbb{R}^{2nN}
by

\displaystyle\trace(x):=\begin{bmatrix}x(b)\\
\vdots\\
\frac{d^{N-1}x}{dz^{N-1}}(b)\\
x(a)\\
\vdots\\
\frac{d^{N-1}x}{dz^{N-1}}(a)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in H^{N}\left([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}\right).

(20)

The boundary trace operator
\trace
is linear, bounded, and surjective, see
[
3
, Section 7.8]
.
It allows us to equivalently write for
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})=\left\{x\in H^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\mid\trace(x)=0\right\}.

Now, consider the matrix differential operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(A)&=\left\{x\in X\mid x\in H^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}),\,W\trace(x)=0\right\},\\
Ax&=\sum_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}\frac{d^{i}x}{dz^{i}},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A),\end{split}

(21)

with
P_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
i=0,\ldots,N
, and
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2n}
. In particular, we have
H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset D(A)
.

Definition 9.3
.

For the domain of the formal adjoint
A_{0}^{\ast}\colon D(A_{0}^{\ast})\subset X\to X
of the matrix differential operator
A
defined in (
21
) it holds that

\displaystyle D(A_{0}^{\ast})=\left\{y\in X\mid\langle A\cdot,y\rangle_{X}\colon H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 2.84544ptx\mapsto\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}\text{ is continuous}\hskip 2.84544pt\right\}.

In other words, the formal adjoint of the differential operator
A
defined in (
21
) is simply given by the adjoint of its restriction
A_{|H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

Next, we present the formal pendant of some of the notions introduced in Definition
9.2
.

Definition 9.4
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the matrix differential operator defined in (
21
).

(i)

A
is called formally symmetric, if

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,Ay\rangle_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(ii)

A
is called formally skew-symmetric, if

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=-\langle x,Ay\rangle_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in H_{0}^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Let us clarify the difference by reference to some simple examples.

Example 9.5
.

Consider the following examples.

(i)

[cf. Example 13.4 in
[
24
]
] Let
X=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
. Consider the operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle D(A)

\displaystyle=H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle Ax

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dz}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

We claim that the Hilbert space adjoint and the formal adjoint of the operator
A
are given by
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, with

\displaystyle D(A_{1})

\displaystyle=H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle D(A_{2})

\displaystyle=D(A),

\displaystyle A_{k}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dz}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A_{k}),\,k=1,2.

Note that for all
x\in D(A)
,
y\in D(A_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\int_{a}^{b}\frac{d}{dz}x(z)y(z)\,dz=\big[x(z)y(z)\big]_{a}^{b}-\int_{a}^{b}x(z)\frac{d}{dz}y(z)\,dz=\langle x,A_{1}y\rangle_{X}.

Thus,
A_{1}\subset A^{\ast}
. In particular, by virtue of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for
y\in D(A_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in D(A)\\
\|x\|_{X}=1\end{subarray}}|\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}|\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in D(A)\\
\|x\|_{X}=1\end{subarray}}\int_{a}^{b}|x(z)|\left|\frac{d}{dz}y(z)\right|\,dz\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in D(A)\\
\|x\|_{X}=1\end{subarray}}\|x\|_{X}\left\|\frac{d}{dz}y\right\|_{X}\leq C_{y}.

Conversely, suppose that
y\in D(A^{\ast})\subset X
. Set
\psi=A^{\ast}y
and define
\Psi=\int_{a}^{\cdot}\psi(r)\,dr\in H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
. Then for all
x\in D(A)
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,A^{\ast}y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,\psi\rangle_{X}=\langle x,\frac{d}{dz}\Psi\rangle_{X}=x(b)\Psi(b)-\langle Ax,\Psi\rangle_{X}.

Since this equation holds in particular for all non-zero constant functions, which are obviously contained in
D(A)
, we conclude that
\Psi(b)=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y+\Psi\rangle_{X}=0

for all
x\in D(A)
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle y+\Psi\in(\ran(A))^{\bot}=X^{\bot}=\left\{0\right\},

whence
y=-\Psi
. This implies that
y\in H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
,
\trace(y)=0
, and

\displaystyle y=-\int_{a}^{\cdot}\psi(r)\,dr=-\int_{a}^{\cdot}A^{\ast}y(r)\,dr.

By taking the derivative on both sides, we conclude that
A^{\ast}y=-\frac{d}{dz}y
for all
y\in H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
. Altogether, the Hilbert space adjoint is given by
A^{\ast}=A_{1}
.

To prove that the formal adjoint of
A
is given by
A_{2}
, we proceed similarly. Note that for all
x\in H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
,
y\in D(A_{2})
we have

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}=\langle x,A_{2}\rangle_{X}.

Conversely, we again suppose that
y\in X
lies in the domain of the formal adjoint, and define
\psi=A^{\ast}y
as well as
\Psi=\int_{a}^{\cdot}\psi(r)\,dr
. Since
x(b)=0
for all
x\in H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
, we find that
y+\Psi\in(\ran(A_{|H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})}))^{\bot}
. Note that
\ran(A_{|H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})})
consists of all functions
y\in X
satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}y(z)\,dz=0.

Denoting by
Y
the one-dimensional subspace of
X
that contains the constant functions on
[a,b]
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\ran(A_{|H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})})=Y^{\bot}.

Hence,
y+\Psi\in Y
. As a result,
y
is absolutely continuous with
\frac{d}{dz}y\in X
, and therefore lies in
H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
. Summing up, the formal adjoint is given by
A_{2}
. We see that we obtain the domain of the formal adjoint by simply dropping the boundary conditions from the domain of the Hilbert space adjoint.

(ii)

Let
X=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Consider the operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle D(A)

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\mid x\in H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2}),\,x(a)=0\right\},

\displaystyle Ax

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}\frac{d}{dz}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

The boundary condition is therefore given by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1\end{bmatrix}\trace(x)=0,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

For all
x,y\in D(A)
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle Ax,y\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle=-\int_{a}^{b}y_{1}(z)\frac{d}{dz}x_{2}(z)+y_{2}(z)\frac{d}{dz}x_{1}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=-\big[x_{1}(z)y_{2}(z)+y_{1}(z)x_{2}(z)\big]_{a}^{b}+\int_{a}^{b}\frac{d}{dz}y_{1}(z)x_{2}(z)+\frac{d}{dz}y_{2}(z)x_{1}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=-x_{1}(b)y_{2}(b)-y_{1}(b)x_{2}(b)-\langle x,Ay\rangle_{X}.

Thus,
A
is not skew-symmetric, but formally skew-symmetric.

\Diamond

After clarifying the difference between Hilbert space adjoints and formally adjoint operators of matrix differential operators, we are going to introduce the powerful concept of strongly continuous semigroups.

10
Strongly Continuous Semigroups

The theory of (one-parameter) semigroups of linear operators has a broad variety of applications, and has proven to be a successful tool for the analysis of linear infinite-dimensional (control) systems. For a matrix operator
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we know that the family
(e^{At})_{t\geq 0}
of matrix exponentials describes the evolution of the state subject to the linear system

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)=Ax(t).

In particular, the mapping
t\mapsto e^{At}
is differentiable and satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}e^{At}=Ae^{At}.

The concept of strongly continuous semigroups is commonly used to generalize the above scenario in order to study (linear) systems with values in an arbitrary Hilbert space.

In this section, we give a concise overview concerning strongly continuous semigroups, and collect some facts about the infinitesimal generators of such semigroups as well as some important criteria regarding the generation of strongly continuous semigroups. The material covered in this section can be found in
[
1
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
23
]
, or
[
25
]
, to name a few.

10.1
Strongly Continuous Semigroups and their Generators

We start by introducing the necessary terminology.

Definition 10.1

(Semigroup)
.

A family
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}\in\mathcal{L}(X)^{[0,\infty)}
is called a semigroup of bounded linear operators on
X
, or short semigroup, if it satisfies

(i)

T(0)=I_{X}
, and

(ii)

the semigroup property:

\displaystyle T(t+s)=T(t)T(s),\hskip 14.22636ptt,s\geq 0.

Definition 10.2
.

A semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
is called

(i)

strongly continuous (or a
C_{0}
-semigroup) if for all
x\in X
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\searrow 0}\|T(t)x-x\|_{X}=0,

(ii)

uniformly continuous if it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\searrow 0}\|T(t)-I_{X}\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}=0.

The strong continuity of
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is equivalent to the mapping
t\mapsto T(t)x
being continuous for all
x\in X
. Analogously, the mapping
t\mapsto T(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is continuous provided that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is uniformly continuous.

In the following, we restrict ourselves to strongly continuous semigroups, since the presented results hold in particular for uniformly continuous semigroups. An overview of the main differences to strongly continuous semigroups is given in
[
23
, Section 1.1]
.

An essential property of
C_{0}
-semigroups is the following.

Theorem 10.2

(Theorem 1.2.2 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
. Then there exist constants
M\geq 1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq Me^{\omega t},\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

We say that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is of type
C_{0}(M,\omega)
.

Theorem 10.2

(cf. Proposition 2.1.2 in
[
25
]
)
.

For a strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
, the mapping

\displaystyle[0,\infty)\times X\to X,\hskip 8.5359pt(t,x)\mapsto T(t)x

is continuous.

Proof 10.3
.

Let
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in X^{\mathbb{N}}
be sequences converging to
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x_{0}\in X
, respectively. Theorem
10.2
yields

\displaystyle\|T(t_{n})x_{n}-T(t_{0})x_{0}\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\|T(t_{n})(x_{n}-x_{0})\|_{X}+\|T(t_{n})x_{0}-T(t_{0})x_{0}\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq Me^{\omega t_{n}}\|x_{n}-x_{0}\|_{X}+Me^{\omega\min\{t_{n},t_{0}\}}\|T(|t_{n}-t_{0}|)x_{0}-x_{0}\|_{X}.

Since the sequence
\left(e^{\omega t_{n}}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded, and since
x_{n}\to x_{0}
by assumption, the first term converges to
0
. The second term converges to
0
as well, because by the strong continuity of
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}T(|t_{n}-t_{0}|)x_{0}=x_{0}.

This proves the claim.

Theorem
10.2
gives rise to the notion of the exponential growth bound for
C_{0}
-semigroups.

Definition 10.4

(Growth Bound)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
. Its (exponential) growth bound is defined as

\displaystyle\omega(T(\cdot)):=\inf\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}\mid\exists M_{\omega}\geq 1\colon\forall t\geq 0\colon\,\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq M_{\omega}e^{\omega t}\right\}.

In this context, we want to introduce the notion of exponential stability for strongly continuous semigroups, which is one of the most important stability concepts in this framework. For an extensive discussion on stability in general and some well-known characterizations of exponential stability we refer to
[
8
, Chapter 4]
.

Definition 10.5

(Exponential Stability)
.

A
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
is called exponentially stable if there exist constants
M\geq 1
and
\alpha>0
such that
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is of type
C_{0}(M,-\alpha)
.

Next, we want to discuss what it means for a linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
to generate a strongly continuous semigroup, and collect some properties of such an operator as well as some relations between a
C_{0}
-semigroup and its infinitesimal generator.

Definition 10.6

(Infinitesimal Generator)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
. The infinitesimal generator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
of
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is defined by

\displaystyle D(A)

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\mid\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{T(t)-I}{t}x\,\text{exists}\right\},

\displaystyle Ax

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\searrow 0}\frac{T(t)-I}{t}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

Theorem 10.6

(Theorem 1.2.4 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. Then the following assertions are true:

(i)

For all
x\in X
and
t\geq 0
it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{h\searrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}T(\tau)x\,d\tau=T(t)x.

(ii)

We have
\int_{0}^{t}T(\tau)x\,d\tau\in D(A)
for all
x\in X
,
t\geq 0
, with

\displaystyle A\left(\int_{0}^{t}T(\tau)x\,d\tau\right)=T(t)x-x.

(iii)

It holds that
T(t)D(A)\subset D(A)
for all
t\geq 0
. Moreover, for all
x\in D(A)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}T(t)x=AT(t)x=T(t)Ax.

(iv)

For all
x\in D(A)
and for all
0\leq\tau\leq t
we have

\displaystyle T(t)x-T(\tau)x=\int_{\tau}^{t}T(r)Ax\,dr=\int_{\tau}^{t}AT(r)x\,dr.

Proof 10.7
.

Ad
(i)
: By continuity of the mapping
t\mapsto T(t)x
we have for all
x\in X
,
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\lim_{h\searrow 0}\left\|\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}T(\tau)x\,d\tau-T(t)x\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\searrow 0}\left\|\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}T(\tau)x-T(t)x\,d\tau\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{h\searrow 0}\frac{1}{h}h\sup_{\tau\in[t,t+h]}\|T(\tau)x-T(t)x\|_{X}

\displaystyle=0.

Ad
(ii)
: Let
h>0
. As
T(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
, it is closed for all
t\geq 0
. Thus, we have for all
x\in X
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}(T(h)-I)\int_{0}^{t}T(\tau)x\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{t}T(\tau+h)x\,d\tau-\frac{1}{h}T(\tau)x\,d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}T(\tau)x\,d\tau-\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{h}T(\tau)x\,d\tau.

By applying property
(i)
, this expression converges to
T(t)x-x
as
h\searrow 0
. This shows
(ii)
.

Ad
(iii)
: Let
x\in D(A)
and
h>0
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}(T(h)-I)T(t)x=T(t)\left(\frac{T(h)-I}{h}\right)x\to T(t)Ax

as
h\searrow 0
. Thus, by definition,
T(t)x\in D(A)
with
AT(t)x=T(t)Ax
for all
t\geq 0
. Moreover, we have for the right-hand derivative that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{+}}{dt}T(t)x=AT(t)x=T(t)Ax.

Now, let
t,h>0
such that
t-h\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\hskip 13.6572pt\left\|\frac{1}{h}(T(t)x-T(t-h)x)-T(t)Ax\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{h}(T(t)x-T(t-h)x)+T(t-h)Ax-T(t-h)Ax-T(t)Ax\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\|T(t-h)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\left\|\frac{1}{h}(T(h)x-x)-Ax\right\|_{X}+\|T(t-h)Ax-T(t)Ax\|_{X}

By strong continuity and the fact that
A
generates
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
, taking the limit
h\searrow 0
yields the same relation for the left-hand derivative.

Ad
(iv)
: This follows from property
(iii)
by integration over
[\tau,t]
.

From these properties one infers:

Corollary 10.8

(Corollary 1.2.5 in
[
23
]
)
.

If
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is the infinitesimal generator of a strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
, then it is densely defined and closed.

Lastly, we note that the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup is uniquely determined.

Theorem 10.8

(Theorem 1.2.6 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
and
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}
be
C_{0}
-semigroups on
X
with infinitesimal generators
A
and
B
, respectively. If
A=B
, then

\displaystyle T(t)=S(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

10.2
Spectrum and Resolvent of Infinitesimal Generators

In the following, we want to characterize infinitesimal generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups. To this end, we introduce the notion of the resolvent operator, and collect some results relevant for the proof of the aforementioned characterization.

Definition 10.9

(Resolvent)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be a linear operator. The resolvent set
\rho(A)
is the set of elements
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
which satisfy the following:

(i)

\overline{(\lambda I-A)(D(A))}=X
.

(ii)

(\lambda I-A)^{-1}
exists and is bounded in

\displaystyle D((\lambda I-A)^{-1}):=(\lambda I-A)(D(A))

with respect to the norm on
X
induced by
(\lambda I-A)(D(A))
.

If
\rho(A)\neq\emptyset
, for
\lambda\in\rho(A)
we call

\displaystyle R(\lambda,A):=(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X)

the resolvent operator of
A
in
\lambda
. The spectrum of
A
is

\displaystyle\sigma(A):=\mathbb{C}\setminus\rho(A).

Definition 10.10
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be a linear operator. The spectrum of
A
is the disjoint union of the following sets:

(i)

The set

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid(\lambda I-A)\text{ is not injective}\right\}

is called the point spectrum of
A
. Its elements are called eigenvalues, and an element
x_{\lambda}\in D(A)
with
Ax_{\lambda}=\lambda x_{\lambda}
is called an eigenfunction with eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma_{p}(A)
.

(ii)

The set

\displaystyle\sigma_{c}(A):=

\displaystyle\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid\overline{(\lambda I-A)(D(A))}=X,\right.

\displaystyle\left.(\lambda I-A)\text{ is injective},\,(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\text{ is unbounded}\right\}

is called the continuous spectrum of
A
.

(iii)

The set

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}(A):=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid\overline{(\lambda I-A)(D(A))}\neq X,\,(\lambda I-A)\text{ is injective}\right\}

is called the residual spectrum of
A
.

Proposition 10.11

(Proposition 2.8.4 in
[
25
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be linear and densely defined, and let
\lambda\in\rho(A)
. Then
\overline{\lambda}\in\rho(A^{\ast})
and

\displaystyle\left[(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\right]^{\ast}=(\overline{\lambda}I-A^{\ast})^{-1}.

An important feature of infinitesimal generators is that the corresponding resolvent operator can be written as the
Laplace transform
of the respective
C_{0}
-semigroup.

Theorem 10.11

(Proposition 2.3.1 in
[
25
]
)
.
