Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
. Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be its infinitesimal generator. Then the following assertions are true:

(i)

\mathbb{C}_{\omega(T(\cdot))}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\realpart(z)>\omega(T(\cdot))\right\}\subset\rho(A)
.

(ii)

For all
x\in X
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\omega(T(\cdot))}
it holds that

\displaystyle(\lambda I-A)^{-1}x=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\,dt.

Proof 10.12
.

If
\alpha=\realpart(\lambda)>\omega(T(\cdot))
, then there are some constants
M\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq Me^{(\alpha-\epsilon)t},\qquad t\geq 0.

Thus, the improper Riemann integral exists, since the integrand converges exponentially to
0
as
t\to\infty
. Now, define

\displaystyle Z(\lambda)x:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\,dt,\qquad x\in X,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\omega(T(\cdot))}.

Then we have for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{\omega(T(\cdot))}
that

\displaystyle\|Z(\lambda)x\|_{X}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\realpart(\lambda))t}Me^{(\realpart(\lambda)-\epsilon)t}\|x\|_{X}\,dt=\frac{M}{\epsilon}\|x\|_{X}.

Thus,
Z(\lambda)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
. Furthermore, by substituting
\eta=t+h
and by Theorem
10.6

(i)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\left(T(h)-I\right)Z(\lambda)x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\left(T(t+h)-T(t)\right)x\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\lambda h}}{h}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\eta}T(\eta)x\,d\eta-\frac{e^{\lambda h}}{h}\int_{0}^{h}e^{-\lambda\eta}T(\eta)x\,d\eta

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\eta}T(\eta)x\,d\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\lambda h}-1}{h}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\,dt-\frac{e^{\lambda h}}{h}\int_{0}^{h}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\,dt

\displaystyle\to\lambda Z(\lambda)x-x

as
h\to 0
. We conclude that
Z(\lambda)x\in D(A)
with

\displaystyle AZ(\lambda)x=\lambda Z(\lambda)x-x.

Hence,
(\lambda I-A)Z(\lambda)x=x
for all
x\in X
. As
A
is closed, for all
x\in D(A)
the following holds:

\displaystyle Z(\lambda)Ax

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)Ax\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}AT(t)x\,dt

\displaystyle=A\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}T(t)x\,dt\right)

\displaystyle=AZ(\lambda)x.

This yields

\displaystyle Z(\lambda)(\lambda I-A)x=(\lambda I-A)Z(\lambda)x=x.

In conclusion, we have that
Z(\lambda)=(\lambda I-A)^{-1}
, and so
\lambda\in\rho(A)
.

In the following, we want to present some characteristics of infinitesimal generators of strongly continuous semigroups. To this end, we need to introduce the
Yosida approximation
of a closed and densely defined operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
. Assume that there exist constants
M>0
and
\omega>0
such that
(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A)
and such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\|R^{n}(\lambda,A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq\frac{M}{(\lambda-\omega)^{n}},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda>\omega.

If so, it has been shown that for every
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, the Yosida approximation defined as

\displaystyle A_{\lambda}:=\lambda^{2}R(\lambda,A)-\lambda I=\lambda AR(\lambda,A)\in\mathcal{L}(X)

has some useful properties which are exploited in order to prove the necessity of the following characterization of infinitesimal generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups. The sufficiency flollows from Corollary
10.8
and Theorem
10.11
.

Theorem 10.12

(Hille-Yosida, cf. Theorem II.3.8 in
[
12
]
)
.

A linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
of type
C_{0}(M,\omega)
if and only if the following holds:

(i)

A
is closed and densely defined.

(ii)

(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A)
.

(iii)

For all
\lambda>\omega
and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
one has

\displaystyle\|R^{n}(\lambda,A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq\frac{M}{(\lambda-\omega)^{n}}.

A similar result can be proved for an important class of strongly continuous semigroups, called contraction semigroups. They are especially interesting in the port-Hamiltonian framework of infinite-dimensional systems.

Definition 10.13

(Contraction Semigroup)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X
.

(i)

It is called a contraction semigroup, if it is of type
C_{0}(1,0)
, that is,

\displaystyle\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq 1,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

(ii)

It is called a unitary semigroup, if for all
x\in X
it holds that

\displaystyle\|T(t)x\|_{X}=\|x\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

Theorem 10.13

(Hille-Yosida, Theorem 1.3.1 in
[
23
]
)
.

A linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is the infinitesimal generator of a contraction semigroup on
X
if and only if the following holds:

(i)

A
is closed and
\overline{D(A)}=X
.

(ii)

(0,\infty)\subset\rho(A)
, and for every
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}.

There is another important characterization for when a linear operator generates a contraction semigroup. To this end, we need to clarify what it means that a linear operator is dissipative.

Definition 10.14

(Dissipative Operator)
.

A linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is called dissipative if for all
x\in D(A)
,

\displaystyle\realpart\langle Ax,x\rangle_{X}\leq 0.

The dissipativity of a linear operator can be characterized in the following way.

Lemma 10.15

(Proposition 6.1.5 in
[
17
]
)
.

A linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is dissipative if and only if for all
x\in D(A)
,
\lambda>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\|(\lambda I-A)x\|_{X}\geq\lambda\|x\|_{X}.

Lemma 10.16

(Lemma 6.1.6 in
[
17
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be a closed, dissipative operator. Then
\ran(\lambda I-A)
is closed for all
\lambda>0
.

Applying Theorem
10.13
as well as Lemma
10.15
and Lemma
10.16
, one can prove that generators of contraction semigroups can be characterized as follows.

Theorem 10.16

(Lumer-Phillips, Theorem 6.1.7 in
[
17
]
)
.

A linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is the infinitesimal generator of a contraction semigroup on
X
if and only if the following holds:

(i)

A
is dissipative.

(ii)

\ran(I-A)=X
.

Now that we have examined some properties of strongly continuous semigroups and their infinitesimal generators, we can finally deal with solving Hilbert space-valued initial value problems. It turns out that
C_{0}
-semigroups are a vital tool for the analysis of infinite-dimensional systems.

11
The Abstract Cauchy Problem

In this section, we introduce the abstract Cauchy problem, i.e., a linear inital value problem with values in a Hilbert space. We will clarify the notion of a solution of such an abstract differential equation, and characterize well-posedness of this class of systems. Again, this is standard material and is covered in
[
8
, Chapter 5]
,
[
12
, Section II.6]
,
[
23
, Chatper 4]
.

11.1
Linear Homogeneous Initial Value Problems

We begin with the definition of an abstract Cauchy problem.

Definition 11.1

(Abstract Cauchy Problem)
.

The linear initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}(t)&=Ax(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,\\
x(0)&=x_{0}\in X,\end{split}

(22)

with
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
a linear operator,
x_{0}
the initial value and
x\colon[0,\infty)\to X
a time-dependent, Hilbert space-valued function is called an abstract Cauchy problem. The function
x
is called a (classical) solution, if the following holds:

(i)

x\in\mathcal{C}([0,\infty),X)
.

(ii)

x_{|(0,\infty)}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,\infty),X)
.

(iii)

x(t)\in D(A)
for all
t>0
.

(iv)

x
satisfies (
22
).

We need to agree on a concept of well-posedness for the abstract Cauchy problem (
22
). Just as in the finite-dimensional case, we require for its solutions existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence on the initial data. This guides us to the following definition.

Definition 11.2

(Well-posedness)
.

The abstract Cauchy problem (
22
) is called well-posed, if the following assertions are true:

(i)

The operator
A
is densely defined.

(ii)

For all
x_{0}\in D(A)
, there exists a unique classical solution
x(\cdot;x_{0})\colon[0,\infty)\to X
.

(iii)

The classical solution depends continuously on the initial value, that is, for all
\epsilon>0
,
\tau>0
, and
x_{0}\in D(A)
there exists some
\delta>0
such that for all
x_{1}\in D(A)
the following holds:

\displaystyle\|x_{0}-x_{1}\|_{X}\leq\delta\hskip 8.5359pt\Rightarrow\hskip 8.5359pt\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|x(t;x_{0})-x(t;x_{1})\|_{X}\leq\epsilon.

There are weaker notions of well-posedness as well, but in this thesis, we work with the concept suggested in Definition
11.2
.

The following theorem shows that if the operator
A
associated with (
22
) is the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup, then one can easily construct a classical solution.

Theorem 11.2

(Proposition II.6.2 in
[
12
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. Then, for every initial value
x_{0}\in D(A)
, the function

\displaystyle x=x(\cdot;x_{0})\colon[0,\infty)\to X,\hskip 8.5359ptt\mapsto T(t)x_{0},

(23)

is the unique classical solution of (
22
), with
x
depending continuously on the initial data.

Proof 11.3
.

The proof that
x
defined in (
23
) is a classical solution follows immediately from property (iii) of Theorem
10.6
. Next, we want to show that
x
is the only solution of (
22
).

Assume that
y\colon[0,\infty)\to X
solves (
22
) as well. For
t>0
, define the mapping

\displaystyle\psi\colon\tau\mapsto T(t-\tau)y(\tau),\hskip 14.22636pt\tau\in[0,t].

By assumption,
y
is continuously differentiable and
y(\tau)\in D(A)
for all
\tau\in(0,t)
. Thus, for all
\tau,h>0
such that
\tau+h<t
it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{\psi(\tau+h)-\psi(\tau)}{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{T(t-\tau-h)y(\tau+h)-T(t-\tau)y(\tau)}{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{T(t-\tau-h)y(\tau+h)-T(t-\tau-h)y(\tau)+T(t-\tau-h)y(\tau)-T(t-\tau)y(\tau)}{h}

\displaystyle=T(t-\tau-h)\frac{y(\tau+h)-y(\tau)}{h}+\frac{T(t-\tau-h)y(\tau)-T(t-\tau)y(\tau)}{h}.

Again, since
y(\tau)\in D(A)
for all
\tau\in(0,t)
, the second term converges to
-AT(t-\tau)y(s)
as
h\searrow 0
. For the first term, we may find the limit by writing

\displaystyle T(t-\tau-h)\frac{y(\tau+h)-y(\tau)}{h}

\displaystyle=T(t-\tau)\frac{y(\tau+h)-y(\tau)}{h}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left[T(t-\tau-h)-T(t-\tau)\right]\left(\frac{y(\tau+h)-y(\tau)}{h}-\frac{d}{d\tau}y(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left[T(t-\tau-h)-T(t-\tau)\right]\frac{d}{d\tau}y(\tau).

Now, since
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is strongly continuous,
T(t-\tau-h)-T(t-\tau)
is uniformly bounded on compact (time) intervals. Consequently, the second term tends to zero as
h\searrow 0
. By virtue of the strong continuity, the same holds for the last expression. Hence, this sum converges to
T(t-\tau)\frac{d}{d\tau}y(\tau)
as
h\searrow 0
. Altogether, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\psi(\tau)

\displaystyle=T(t-\tau)\frac{d}{d\tau}y(\tau)-AT(t-\tau)y(\tau)

\displaystyle=T(t-\tau)Ay(\tau)-AT(t-\tau)y(\tau)

\displaystyle=0.

The last equation follows from Theorem
10.6

(iii)
. Integration yields that

\displaystyle 0=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d}{d\tau}(T(t-\tau)y(\tau))\,d\tau=y(t)-T(t)x_{0},

whence
y(t)=T(t)x_{0}=x(t)
for all
t\geq 0
. This proves the uniqueness of the (classical) solution of (
22
).

Lastly, we need to show the continuous dependence of the solution on the initial data. As
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is strongly continuous, it is of type
C_{0}(M,\omega)
for some
M\geq 1
,
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
(see Theorem
10.2
). Thus, we have for
\tau>0
and
x_{0},x_{1}\in D(A)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|x(t;x_{0})-x(t;x_{1})\|_{X}=\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|T(t)(x_{0}-x_{1})\|_{X}\leq Me^{|\omega|\tau}\|x_{0}-x_{1}\|_{X}.

This shows that the solution is continuously depending on the initial value and, hence, completes the proof.

Theorem
11.2
shows in particular that it is sufficient for well-posedness of the abstract Cauchy problem (
22
) that the associated operator
A
is the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup. In fact, it is necessary as well. However, we will not prove the necessity and refer to
[
12
, Theorem II.6.7]
.

Theorem 11.3

(Corollary II.6.9 in
[
12
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be a linear and closed operator. Then the initial value problem (
22
) is well-posed in the sense of Definition
11.2
if and only if
A
is the infinitesimal generator of a strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. If so, then for any
x_{0}\in D(A)
, the unique classical solution is given by (
23
).

Theorem
11.3
emphasizes the importance of the concept of strongly continuous semigroups, since they are well-suited for the study of abstract Cauchy problems. The starting point for the analysis of abstract Cauchy problems is therefore to check whether the associated operator
A
generates a strongly continuous semigroup. In this context, let us quickly come back to dissipative operators, and consider the following example.

Example 11.4
.

Consider the abstract initial value problem (
22
) associated with an operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
generating a contraction semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
, and with initial value
x_{0}\in D(A)
. Then, by Theorem
11.3
, the system has the unique solution
x=T(\cdot)x_{0}
. Moreover, according to Theorem
10.16
,
A
is dissipative. This yields for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|x(t)\|_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\dot{x}(t),x(t)\rangle_{X}+\langle x(t),\dot{x}(t)\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle=\langle Ax(t),x(t)\rangle_{X}+\langle x(t),Ax(t)\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle=2\realpart\langle Ax(t),x(t)\rangle_{X}

\displaystyle\leq 0.

Integration on both sides over
[0,\tau]
yields
\|x(\tau)\|_{X}^{2}\leq\|x(0)\|_{X}^{2}
for all
\tau\geq 0
. In many physical problems, the value
\|x\|_{X}^{2}
,
x\in X
, represents the energy level of the state
x
. Thus, for solutions of abstract Cauchy problems associated with a dissipative operator, the energy of the corresponding solution is dissipating. As we will see, this property is essential in the port-Hamiltonian framework of infinite-dimensional systems.

\Diamond

11.2
Linear Inhomogeneous Initial Value Problems

As a preparation for the analysis of abstract control systems, in this section, we shortly discuss inhomogeneous initial value problems of the following form.

Definition 11.5

(Inhomogeneous Abstract Cauchy Problem)
.

The initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}(t)&=Ax(t)+f(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,\\
x(0)&=x_{0}\in X,\end{split}

(24)

with
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
a linear operator,
x_{0}
the initial value, and
f\colon[0,\infty)\to X
,
x\colon[0,\infty)\to X
time-dependent,
X
-valued functions is called an inhomogeneous abstract Cauchy problem. Let
\tau>0
. The function
x
is called a (classical) solution on
[0,\tau)
, if the following holds:

(i)

x_{|[0,\tau]}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],X)
.

(ii)

x_{|(0,\tau)}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,\tau),X)
.

(iii)

x(t)\in D(A)
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
.

(iv)

x
satisfies (
24
) on
(0,\tau)
.

From the discussion in Subsection
11.1
it makes sense to assume that the operator
A
associated with (
24
) generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup, so that the equation with
f\equiv 0
has a uniquely determined classical solution.

We want to show first that if (
24
) has a solution, then it has a particular form. More precisely, it is given by the
variation of constants
formula.

Lemma 11.6

(cf. Corollary 4.2.2 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],X)
,
x_{0}\in D(A)
, and assume that
x\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,\tau],X)
is a classical solution of (
24
) on
[0,\tau)
. Then
x
is of the form

\displaystyle x(t)=T(t)x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptt\in[0,\tau].

(25)

Proof 11.7
.

For
0<t<\tau
, define the mapping

\displaystyle\psi\colon s\mapsto T(t-s)x(s),\hskip 14.22636pts\in[0,t].

With similar arguments as in the proof of Theorem
11.2
one can show that
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,t),X)
, and its derivative is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\psi(s)=T(T-s)\dot{x}(s)-AT(t-s)x(s),\hskip 14.22636pts\in(0,t).

Using Theorem
10.6

(iii)
and the fact that
x
is a classical solution, we find

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\psi(s)=T(t-s)(Ax(s)+f(s))-T(t-s)Ax(s)=T(t-s)f(s).

(26)

Due to the continuity of
f
, the mapping
s\mapsto T(t-s)f(s)
is integrable. Thus, by integration of (
26
) and by definition of
\psi
, we obtain

\displaystyle x(t)-T(t)x(0)=\psi(t)-\psi(0)=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)ds.

In conclusion,
x
is of the form (
25
). This proves the claim.

The map (
25
) can be defined for any
x_{0}\in X
and any
f\in L^{1}([0,\tau],X)
. In this case, it is an element of
\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],X)
and is called the
mild solution
of (
24
). It is clear that, in general, the mild solution (
25
) is not a classical solution of (
24
). Furthermore, the continuity of
f
we required in Lemma
11.6
is not sufficient to guarantee that there is a classical solution, even if
x_{0}\in D(A)
. To see this, let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup with generator
A
and the property that there exist some
\hat{t}>0
and
\hat{x}\in X
such that
T(\hat{t})\hat{x}\notin D(A)
. Now, consider the initial value problem

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=Ax(t)+T(t)\hat{x},\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

\displaystyle x(0)

\displaystyle=0.

One may show that the mild solution is given by
t\mapsto tT(t)\hat{x}
, which is not differentiable on
(0,\infty)
, since
\hat{t}T(\hat{t})\hat{x}\notin D(A)
. Therefore, by Lemma
11.6
, for this system there does not exist a classical solution at all.

In the following, we want to present criteria that guarantee the existence of classical solutions of (
24
) for any initial value
x_{0}\in D(A)
.

Theorem 11.7

(Theorem 4.2.4 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. Let
f\in L^{1}([0,\tau],X)\cap\mathcal{C}((0,\tau],X)
and define

\displaystyle V(t)=\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)f(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptt\in[0,\tau].

Then (
24
) has a classical solution on
[0,\tau)
for all
x_{0}\in D(A)
if one of the following properties hold:

(i)

V_{|(0,\tau)}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((0,\tau),X)
.

(ii)

For all
t\in(0,\tau)
it holds that
V(t)\in D(A)
and
AV(\cdot)
is continuous on
(0,\tau)
.

Conversely, if (
24
) has a solution on
[0,\tau)
for some
x_{0}\in D(A)
, then it satisfies both
(i)
and
(ii)
.

From this theorem we may deduce the following two corollaries, which we will not prove.

Corollary 11.8

(Corollary 4.2.5 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. If
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,\tau],X)
, then for every
x_{0}\in D(A)
the initial value problem (
24
) has a unique classical solution on
[0,\tau)
.

Corollary 11.9

(Corollary 4.2.6 in
[
23
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the infinitesimal generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. Let
f\in L^{1}([0,\tau],X)\cap\mathcal{C}((0,\tau),X)
. If
f(s)\in D(A)
for all
s\in(0,\tau)
and
Af(\cdot)\in L^{1}([0,\tau],X)
, then for every
x_{0}\in D(A)
the initial value problem (
24
) has a unique classical solution on
[0,\tau)
.

Next, we want to have a quick discussion on linear control systems.

12
Linear Control Systems

The inhomogeneous system (
24
) can be written as an abstract control system, if we replace the function
f(\cdot)
by
Bu(\cdot)\colon[0,\tau]\to X
, where
u\colon[0,\infty)\to U
is some input with the input space
U
being a separable Hilbert space, and
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X)
is the input operator. However, the assumption that the input operator is a bounded linear operator from
U
to
X
is quite restrictive. In many applications, e.g., boundary and point control problems, the input operators are unbounded.

For a proper treatment of systems with an unbounded input operator, we make use of the concept of admissible control operators. To this end, we lift the state space
X
to a larger space
X_{-1}
, called the extrapolation space, that has the property that the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
generated by
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
extends uniquely to a
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T_{-1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
, and its generator
A_{-1}
is the extension of
A
. We then want to find those
X_{-1}
-valued input operators for which the lifted control system admits uniquely determined,
X
-valued solutions. Hence, admissibility is a well-suited concept to prove well-posedness of abstract control systems.

We proceed as follows. In Subsection
12.1
we introduce the necessary terminology as well as the concept of duality with respect to a pivot space, where we follow the exposition from Section 2.9 and Section 2.10 in
[
25
]
. Then, in Subsection
12.2
we give a concise overview about
X_{-1}
-valued control systems and the significance of admissible control operators, which is based on Section 4.1 and Section 4.2 in
[
25
]
.

12.1
The Spaces
\mathbf{X}_{1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-1}

As a preparation for abstract control systems and the definition of a solution of such systems, we are going to introduce the interpolation space and the extrapolation space for a linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
. Besides that, we need to explain what it means that two Hilbert spaces are dual with respect to a pivot space.

Let
V
and
H
be Hilbert spaces with
V\subset H
. We say that the
embedding

V\hookrightarrow H
is
continuous
provided that there exists some
m\geq 0
such that for all
v\in V
it holds that

\displaystyle\|v\|_{H}\leq m\|v\|_{V}.

In other words, the identity operator from
V
to
H
is an element of
\mathcal{L}(V,H)
.

Theorem 12.0

(Proposition 2.9.2 in
[
25
]
)
.

Let
V
and
H
be Hilbert spaces with
V\subset H
. Assume that the embedding
V\hookrightarrow H
is continuous and densely defined. Define the function
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}\colon H\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\|z\|_{\ast}=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v\in V}}{{\|v\|_{V}=1}}}|\langle z,v\rangle_{H}|,\hskip 14.22636ptz\in H.

(27)

Then
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
is a norm on
H
. Let
V^{\ast}
denote the completion of
H
with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
. Define the operator
J\colon V^{\ast}\to V^{\prime}
as follows: for any
z\in V^{\ast}
,

\displaystyle\langle Jz,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle z_{n},v\rangle_{H},\hskip 14.22636ptv\in V,

(28)

with
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in H^{\mathbb{N}}
a sequence converging to
z
in
V^{\ast}
. Then
J
is an isomorphism from
V^{\ast}
to
V^{\prime}
.

Proof 12.1

(Sketch of the proof of Theorem
12.0
)
.

We will skip the proofs that
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
is a norm, that the limit (
28
) exists and is independent of the choice of the sequence
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, as long as
z_{n}\to z
in
V^{\ast}
. We will show that
\langle Jz,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}
depends continuously on
v\in V
, and thus
Jz\in V^{\prime}
. From the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
we deduce

\displaystyle|\langle z,v\rangle_{H}|\leq\|z\|_{\ast}\|v\|_{V},\hskip 14.22636ptz\in H,\,v\in V.

Hence, for any
z\in V^{\ast}
and any
v\in V
,

\displaystyle|\langle Jz,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\langle z_{n},v\rangle_{H}|\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\|z_{n}\|_{\ast}\|v\|_{V}=\|z\|_{\ast}\|v\|_{V}.

As a consequence,
Jz\in V^{\prime}
. In particular,
\|Jz\|_{V^{\prime}}\leq\|z\|_{\ast}
for all
z\in V^{\ast}
, whence
J\in\mathcal{L}(V^{\ast},V^{\prime})
. From the definition of
J
we infer that

\displaystyle\langle Jz,v\rangle_{V^{\prime}\times V}=\langle z,v\rangle_{H},\hskip 14.22636ptz\in H,\,v\in V,

and so
\|Jz\|_{V^{\prime}}=\|z\|_{\ast}
for all
z\in H
. Since
H
is dense in
V^{\ast}
and
J
is continuous, we conclude that
\|Jz\|_{V^{\prime}}=\|z\|_{\ast}
even holds for all
z\in V^{\ast}
. The verification that
J
is onto can be looked up in the proof of Proposition 2.9.2 in
[
25
]
. Altogether,
J
is an isomorphism from
V^{\ast}
to
V^{\prime}
.

If
V
,
H
, and
V^{\ast}
are as in Theorem
12.0
, then we say that we identify
V^{\ast}
with
V^{\prime}
if we do not distinguish between
z
and
Jz
for all
z\in V^{\ast}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle V\subset H\subset V^{\prime},

with continuous and densely defined embeddings. We call
V^{\prime}
the
dual of

V

with respect to the pivot space

H
. Moreover, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\ast}
defined in (
27
) is called the
dual norm of

\|\cdot\|_{V}

with respect to the pivot space

H
.

Note that the space
V
is uniquely determined by
V^{\prime}
, as it consists of all
v\in H
for which the mapping
z\mapsto\langle z,v\rangle_{H}
has a continuous extension to
V^{\prime}
. We therefore call
V
the
dual of

V^{\prime}

with respect to the pivot space

H
.

Now that we have clarified duality with respect to a pivot space, we can finally turn to the definition of the interpolation space and the extrapolation space of a linear operator.

Definition 12.2

(Interpolation Space
\mathbf{X_{1}}
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be linear and densely defined with
\rho(A)\neq\emptyset
. For a fixed
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, define the interpolation space
X_{1}
=
(D(A),\|\cdot\|_{1})
, where

\displaystyle\|x\|_{1}:=\|(\lambda I-A)x\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

According to
[
25
, Proposition 2.10.1]
the interpolation space
X_{1}
is a Hilbert space which is continuously embedded in
X
. Furthermore, for every
\lambda\in\rho(A)
,
\|\cdot\|_{1}
is equivalent to the
graph norm

\|\cdot\|_{D(A)}
of
A
, and if
L\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is such that
LD(A)\subset D(A)
, then
L\in\mathcal{L}(X_{1})
.

For the adjoint operator
A^{\ast}\colon D(A^{\ast})\subset X\to X
of
A
one can analogously define the Hilbert space
X_{1}^{d}=(D(A^{\ast}),\|\cdot\|_{1}^{d})
with

\displaystyle\|x\|_{1}^{d}:=\|(\overline{\lambda}I-A^{\ast})x\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A^{\ast}),

where
\overline{\lambda}\in\rho(A^{\ast})
(
\Leftrightarrow\lambda\in\rho(A)
, see Proposition
10.11
).

Definition 12.3

(Extrapolation Space
\mathbf{X_{-1}}
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be linear and densely defined with
\rho(A)\neq\emptyset
. For a fixed
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, define the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
as the completion of
X
with respect to the norm

\displaystyle\|x\|_{-1}:=\|(\lambda I-A)^{-1}x\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

According to
[
25
, Proposition 2.10.2]
the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
is again a Hilbert space, and the norms for different
\lambda\in\rho(A)
are equivalent. In particular,
X_{-1}
is independent of the choice of
\lambda
. Moreover,
X_{-1}
is the dual of
X_{1}^{d}
with respect to the pivot space
X
. To see the last assertion, define
\|\cdot\|_{-1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1}^{d}
with respect to the same
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, and apply Proposition
10.11
. Then, for every
z\in X
,

\displaystyle\|z\|_{-1}

\displaystyle=\|(\lambda I-A)^{-1}z\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\in X}}{{\|x\|_{X}=1}}}|\langle(\lambda I-A)^{-1}z,x\rangle_{X}|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x\in X}}{{\|x\|_{X}=1}}}|\langle z,(\overline{\lambda}I-A^{\ast})^{-1}x\rangle_{X}|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle y\in X_{1}^{d}}}{{\|y\|_{1}^{d}=1}}}|\langle z,y\rangle_{X}|.

Next, we present an important result for the upcoming section. Assume that
A
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup. Then this semigroup has a unique extension to
\mathcal{L}(X_{-1})
.
Subsequently, we show an example where we specify the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
for an infinitesimal generator.

Proposition 12.4

(Proposition 2.10.3 in
[
25
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be linear and densely defined with
\rho(A)\neq\emptyset
. For
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, define
X_{1}
and
X_{-1}
as in Definition
12.2
and
12.3
, respectively. Then
A\in\mathcal{L}(X_{1},X)
and
A
has a unique extension
A_{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X,X_{-1})
. Moreover,

\displaystyle(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X,X_{1})\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt(\lambda I-A_{-1})^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X_{-1},X).

In particular,
\lambda\in\rho(A_{-1})
, and these operators are unitary.

Proposition 12.5

(Proposition 2.10.4 in
[
25
]
)
.

Let
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
be the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X
. For
\lambda\in\rho(A)
, define
X_{1}
and
X_{-1}
as in Definition
12.2
and
12.3
, respectively. For
t\geq 0
, the restriction of
T(t)
to
X_{1}
(considered as an operator in
\mathcal{L}(X_{1})
) is the image of
T(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
through the unitary operator
(\lambda I-A)^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X,X_{1})
. Therefore, these operators form a strongly continuous semigroup on
X_{1}
whose generator is
A_{|D(A^{2})}
. The operator
T_{-1}(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X_{-1})
is the image of
T(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
through the unitary operator
(\lambda I-A_{-1})^{-1}\in\mathcal{L}(X_{-1},X)
. Therefore, these extended operators form a strongly continuous semigroup
(T_{-1}(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
, whose generator is
A_{-1}\colon D(A_{-1})\subset X_{-1}\to X_{-1}
with
D(A_{-1})=X
.

Example 12.6

(Example 2.10.7 in
[
25
]
)
.

Consider the left-shift semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X=L^{2}([0,\infty),\mathbb{C})
defined by

\displaystyle T(t)x(\cdot)=x(\cdot+t),\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0,\,x\in X.

Its generator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is given by

\displaystyle D(A)

\displaystyle=H^{1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle Ax

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dz}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A).

We want to determine the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
of this semigroup. To this end, we define the Hilbert space adjoint
A^{\ast}\colon D(A^{\ast})\subset X\to X
of
A
, which is given by

\displaystyle D(A^{\ast})

\displaystyle=H_{0}^{1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle A^{\ast}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dz}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A^{\ast}).

From the preceding discussion, we know that the dual of
X_{1}^{d}=(D(A^{\ast}),\|\cdot\|_{1}^{d})
with respect to the pivot space
X
is given by
X_{-1}
. According to Definition 13.4.7 in
[
25
]
, the dual space of
H_{0}^{1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C})
is therefore given by the Sobolev space
H^{-1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle X_{-1}=(H_{0}^{1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C}))^{\prime}=H^{-1}((0,\infty),\mathbb{C}).

Next, we want to specify the norm on
X_{-1}
. Choosing
\lambda=1\in\rho(A)
, Theorem
10.11
yields

\displaystyle(\lambda I-A)^{-1}x(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}x(z+t)\,dt,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,\,z\in(0,\infty).

Thus, the norm
\|\cdot\|_{-1}
can be presented as

\displaystyle\|x\|_{-1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}x(\cdot+t)\,dt\right\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

\Diamond

Having introduced the necessary concepts, we can have a quick discussion on
X_{-1}
-valued control systems in the following segment.

12.2
Abstract Control Systems and Admissibility

In this section, we collect some facts about existence and uniqueness of solutions of abstract control systems with values in the extrapolation space
X_{-1}
of the form

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=A_{-1}x(t)+Bu(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

\displaystyle x(0)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X_{-1}.

In the following, we will denote the extension
A_{-1}
of
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
and the extension
T_{-1}(t)
of
T(t)
,
t\geq 0
, by
A
and
T(t)
again, respectively. We will not give any proofs and refer to
[
25
, Chapter 4]
.

First, we want to clarify what we want to understand under a solution in
X_{-1}
.

Definition 12.7

(Solution in
\mathbf{X_{-1}}
)
.

Consider the differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)=Ax(t)+Bu(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

(29)

with
A\colon D(A)\subset X_{-1}\to X_{-1}
,
u\in L_{\text{loc}}^{2}([0,\infty),U)
,
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X_{-1})
, and with
U
a separable Hilbert space. A solution of (
29
) is a function

\displaystyle x\in L_{loc}^{1}([0,\infty),X)\cap\mathcal{C}([0,\infty),X_{-1})

which satisfies the following equations in
X_{-1}
:

\displaystyle x(t)-x(0)=\int_{0}^{t}Ax(s)+Bu(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

Just as it is the case for the inhomogeneous Cauchy problem, if (
29
) has a solution, then it has a particular form.

Proposition 12.8

(Proposition 4.1.4 in
[
25
]
)
.

With the notation in Definition
12.7
, suppose that
x
is a solution of (
29
) in
X_{-1}
. Then
x
is given by

\displaystyle x(t)=T(t)x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}T(t-s)Bu(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0,

and is called the mild solution of (
29
). In particular, for every
x_{0}\in X
there exists at most one solution in
X_{-1}
of (
29
) satisfying the initial condition
x(0)=x_{0}
.

Next, we give a criterion for the initial value
x_{0}
and the function
f
that guarantees the existence of a unique solution satisfying the initial condition
x(0)=x_{0}
.

Theorem 12.8

(Theorem 4.1.6 in
[
25
]
)
.

If
x_{0}\in X
and
Bu(\cdot)\in H_{loc}^{1}([0,\infty),X_{-1})
, then (
29
) has a unique solution in
X_{-1}
, denoted by
x
, that satisfies
x(0)=x_{0}
. Moreover, this solution is such that

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{C}([0,\infty),X)\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,\infty),X_{-1}),

and for all
t\geq 0
it satisfies (
29
) in a classical sense.

Now, let us consider the following abstract control system:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}(t)&=Ax(t)+Bu(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,\\
x(0)&=x_{0}\in X.\end{split}

(30)

Motivated by the study of systems of the form (
30
), the concept of admissibility was introduced. Generally speaking, one wants to specify all those control operators
B
for which all mild solutions
x
of (
30
) are continuous
X
-valued functions. Operators
B
with this property are called admissible.

Definition 12.9

(Admissible Control Operator)
.

Let
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
X_{-1}
. Let
\tau\geq 0
. Define the mapping
\Phi_{\tau}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}([0,\infty),U),X_{-1})
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\tau}u:=\int_{0}^{\tau}T(\tau-\eta)Bu(\eta)\,d\eta,\hskip 14.22636ptu\in L^{2}([0,\infty),U).

(31)

An operator
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X_{-1})
is called an admissible control operator for
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
if for some
\tau>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\ran(\Phi_{\tau})\subset X.

In other words: if
B
is admissible, then in (
31
) the integrand is
X_{-1}
-valued, but the integral itself is an element of
X
.

Proposition 12.10

(Proposition 4.2.2 in
[
25
]
)
.

Suppose that
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X_{-1})
is admissible for the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
. Then for every
t\geq 0
it holds that

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}([0,\infty),U),X).

The operators
\Phi_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, are called the
input maps corresponding to the system
(
30
), and have the following property.

Proposition 12.11

(Proposition 4.2.4 in
[
25
]
)
.

Suppose that
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X_{-1})
is admissible for the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
. Then the mapping

\displaystyle[0,\infty)\times L^{2}([0,\infty),U),\hskip 8.5359pt(t,u)\mapsto\Phi_{t}u

is continuous.

The main result of this section states under which criteria the mild solutions of the abstract control system (
30
) are continuous
X
-valued functions, and is given below.

Theorem 12.11

(Proposition 4.2.5 in
[
25
]
)
.

Suppose that
B\in\mathcal{L}(U,X_{-1})
is admissible for the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
on
X_{-1}
generated by
A\colon D(A)\subset X_{-1}\to X_{-1}
. Then for every
x_{0}\in X
and every
u\in L_{loc}^{2}([0,\infty),U)
, the initial value problem (
30
) has a unique solution in
X_{-1}
. This solution is given by

\displaystyle x(t)=T(t)x_{0}+\Phi_{t}u,\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0,

and satisfies

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{C}([0,\infty),X)\cap H_{loc}^{1}((0,\infty),X_{-1}).

This theorem shows that admissibility is a valuable tool to prove well-posedness of the abstract control system (
30
). In
[
25
, Section 4.2]
one can find an example where an admissible operator has been determined for the unilateral right-shift semigroup.

The following and last section of this chapter deals with the generalization of the abstract Cauchy problem discussed in Section
11
, where the operator associated with the system changes over time as well.

13
Evolution Equations

In this section, we want to give a concise overview concerning evolution problems. Roughly speaking, evolution problems are abstract, time-variant Cauchy problems, that is, the operator associated with the Cauchy problem is changing over time. We will introduce the necessary terminology and study the concept of stability for this class of systems, as this is a key property that has to be satisfied for proving well-posedness. This section relies substantially on
[
23
, Chapter 5]
.

As in the autonomous case, let us begin with the definition of an evolution problem.

Definition 13.1

(Evolution Problem)
.

Let
0\leq t\leq\tau
, and let
A(t)\colon D(A(t))\subset X\to X
be a linear operator. The time-variant initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{split}\dot{x}(t)&=A(t)x(t),\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq s<t\leq\tau,\\
x(s)&=x_{0}\in X\end{split}

(32)

is called an evolution problem. A function
x\colon[s,\tau]\to X
is called a classical solution of the evolution problem (
32
) if the following holds:

(i)

x\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
.

(ii)

x_{|(s,\tau)}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((s,\tau),X)
.

(iii)

x(t)\in D(A(t))
for all
s<t\leq\tau
.

(iv)

x
satisfies (
32
).

The proof of the following theorem is based on using Picard’s iteration method and applying the Banach contraction principle.

Theorem 13.1

(Theorem 5.1.1 in
[
23
]
)
.

For
0\leq t\leq\tau
, let
A(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
. If the mapping

\displaystyle[0,\tau]\to\mathcal{L}(X),\hskip 8.5359ptt\mapsto A(t)

is continuous, then for every
x_{0}\in X
the initial value problem (
32
) has a unique classical solution.

Proof 13.2
.

We make use of Picard’s iteration theorem. Since the mapping
t\mapsto A(t)
is continuous, we may define

\displaystyle\alpha=\max_{t\in[0,\tau]}\|A(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}.

Furthermore, for
x_{0}\in X
define the mapping

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}\colon\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{0}+\int_{s}^{\cdot}A(\eta)u(\eta)\,d\eta,

where
\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
is endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\infty}=\max_{t\in[s,\tau]}\|u(t)\|_{X},\hskip 14.22636ptu\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X),

making
(\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
a Banach space.
For all
u,v\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
and for all
s\leq t\leq\tau
the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\|S_{x_{0}}u(t)-S_{x_{0}}v(t)\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|x_{0}+\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)u(\eta)\,d\eta-x_{0}-\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)v(\eta)\,d\eta\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)\left[u(\eta)-v(\eta)\right]\,d\eta\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s}^{t}\|A(\eta)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\|u(\eta)-v(\eta)\|_{X}\,d\eta

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(t-s)\|u-v\|_{\infty}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\|S_{x_{0}}^{2}u(t)-S_{x_{0}}^{2}v(t)\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|S_{x_{0}}\left(x_{0}+\int_{s}^{\cdot}A(\eta_{1})u(\eta_{1})\,d\eta_{1}\right)(t)-S_{x_{0}}\left(x_{0}+\int_{s}^{\cdot}A(\eta_{1})v(\eta_{1})\,d\eta_{1}\right)(t)\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta_{2})\left(x_{0}+\int_{s}^{\eta_{2}}A(\eta_{1})u(\eta_{1})\,d\eta_{1}\right)\,d\eta_{2}-\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta_{2})\left(x_{0}+\int_{s}^{\eta_{2}}A(\eta_{1})v(\eta_{1})\,d\eta_{1}\right)\,d\eta_{2}\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta_{2})\left(\int_{s}^{\eta_{2}}A(\eta_{1})\left[u(\eta_{1})-v(\eta_{1})\right]d\eta_{1}\right)\,d\eta_{2}\right\|_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{\eta_{2}}\|A(\eta_{1})\left[u(\eta_{1})-v(\eta_{1})\right]\|_{X}\,d\eta_{1}\,d\eta_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{2}\|u-v\|_{\infty}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{\eta_{2}}1\,d\eta_{1}\,d\eta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{2}(t-s)^{2}}{2}\|u-v\|_{\infty}.

By induction it follows that

\displaystyle\|S_{x_{0}}^{n}u(t)-S_{x_{0}}^{n}v(t)\|_{X}\leq\frac{\alpha^{n}(\tau-s)^{n}}{n!}\|u-v\|_{\infty}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
u,v\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
and
s\leq t\leq\tau
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\|S_{x_{0}}^{n}u-S_{x_{0}}^{n}v\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\alpha^{n}(\tau-s)^{n}}{n!}\|u-v\|_{\infty}.

Note that for
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough we get
\frac{\alpha^{N}(\tau-s)^{N}}{N!}<1
, and so
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
is a contraction mapping. By the Banach contraction principle,
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
has a unique fixed point
x=x(\cdot;x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
. One can readily see that

\displaystyle S_{x_{0}}^{N}(S_{x_{0}}(x))=S_{x_{0}}(S_{x_{0}}^{N}(x))=S_{x_{0}}(x).

Thus,
S_{x_{0}}(x)
is a fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
. As
x
is the unique fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
, we infer that
S_{x_{0}}(x)=x
and, hence,
x
is a fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}
as well. Finally, the uniqueness of the fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
together with the fact that every fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}
is a fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}^{N}
yield that
x
is the unique fixed point of
S_{x_{0}}
as well, and therefore satisfies

\displaystyle x(t)=x_{0}+\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)x(\eta)\,d\eta,\hskip 14.22636ptt\in[s,\tau].

(33)

Now, since
x
is continuous, the right-hand side of (
33
) is differentiable. Thus,
x
is differentiable with derivative

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}x(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\left(x_{0}+\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)x(\eta)\,d\eta\right)=A(t)x(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt\in[s,\tau].

Furthermore, we have
x(s)=x_{0}
, and so
x
is a solution of the evolution problem (
32
). Note that every solution of (
32
) is in particular a solution of (
33
). Due to the uniqueness of the fixed point
x
of the mapping
S_{x_{0}}
, we conclude that the solution of (
32
) is unique.

Recall that in Theorem
13.1
we required the operators
A(t)
,
0\leq t\leq\tau
, to be bounded linear operators on
X
. However, this result gives us some intuition regarding the behavior of the solutions of the evolution problem (
32
). Using the representation (
33
) of such a solution, we may define a solution operator
U(t,s)\colon X\to X
,
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
, of the initial value problem (
32
) as follows:

\displaystyle U(t,s)x_{0}=x(t;x_{0})=x_{0}+\int_{s}^{t}A(\eta)x(\eta)\,d\eta,\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau,\,x_{0}\in X.

(34)

The family
(U(t,s))_{0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau}
is a two-parameter family of operators on
X
.
The main properties of the family of solution operators are given in the next theorem.

Theorem 13.2

(Theorem 5.1.2 in
[
23
]
)
.

For
0\leq t\leq\tau
, let
A(t)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
, and let the mapping
t\mapsto A(t)
be continuous on
[0,\tau]
. Then for every
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
, the operator
U(t,s)
defined in (
34
) is an element of
\mathcal{L}(X)
and it satisfies the following:

(i)

\|U(t,s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}\|A(\eta)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\,d\eta\right)
.

(ii)

U(t,t)=I_{X}
and for
0\leq s\leq r\leq t\leq\tau
we have

\displaystyle U(t,s)=U(t,r)U(r,s).

(iii)

The mapping
(t,s)\mapsto U(t,s)
is continuous for
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
.

(iv)

For
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}U(t,s)=A(t)U(t,s).

(v)

For
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}U(t,s)=-U(t,s)A(s).

These properties resemble the ones of (uniformly continuous) one-parameter semigroups studied in Section
10
. Indeed, in the time-variant case the two-parameter family of operators
(U(t,s))_{0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau}
replaces the one-paramter semigroup
(U(t))_{t\geq 0}
of the autonomous case, which is the
C_{0}
-semigroup generated by the (autonomous) operator
A
. This motivates to introduce the notion of evolution systems, which play a substantial role in the framework of evolution problems.

Definition 13.3

(Evolution System)
.

A two-parameter family of operators
U(t,s)\in\mathcal{L}(X)
,
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
, is called an evolution system if the following holds:

(i)

U(s,s)=I_{X}
and
U(t,s)=U(t,r)U(r,s)
for
0\leq s\leq r\leq t\leq\tau
.

(ii)

The mapping
(t,s)\mapsto U(t,s)
is strongly continuous for
0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau
.

Just as for one-paramter semigroups, the strong continuity of
(U(t,s))_{0\leq s\leq t\leq\tau}
is equivalent to the mapping
(t,s)\mapsto U(t,s)x
being continuous for all
x\in X
.

In the following, we want to present one important criterion on a given family of (usually unbouded) operators
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
on
X
that guarantees the existence of a unique classical solution of the evolution problem (
32
) for a dense subset of initial values in
X
. The existence of a unique solution allows us to specify an evolution system associated with the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
. Note that the uniqueness of the solution yields property
(i)
, whereas the continuity of the solution at the initial data yields property
(ii)
of the corresponding evolution system. The aforementioned criterion is called stability, and is defined as follows.

Definition 13.4

(Stable Family of Generators)
.

A family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of infinitesimal generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups on
X
is called stable if there exist constants
M\geq 1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, called stability constants, such that the following holds:

(i)

For
0\leq t\leq\tau
we have
(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A(t))
.

(ii)

For every finite sequence
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}\leq\tau
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{j=1}^{k}R(\lambda,A(t_{j}))\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq\frac{M}{(\lambda-\omega)^{k}},\hskip 14.22636pt\lambda>\omega.

(35)

Remark 13.5
.

We give some short remarks concerning Definition
13.4
.

(i)

In general, the operators
R(\lambda,A(t_{j}))
do not commute. Hence, the order of the terms given in (
35
) is important.

(ii)

The stability of
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is preserved when the norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}
is replaced by an equivalent one. However, the stability constants depend on the particular norm
X
is endowed with.

(iii)

If for
0\leq t\leq\tau
,
A(t)
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup of type
C_{0}(1,\omega)
(see Theorem
10.2
), then one can readily see that the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is stable with stability constants
M=1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, every family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of generators of contraction semigroups is stable.

In general, it is not easy to determine whether a given family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of infinitesimal generators is stable. In the next theorem we give a characterization regarding the stability of such a family. For the proof, one exploits Theorem
10.11
.

Theorem 13.5

(Theorem 5.2.2 in
[
23
]
)
.

For
0\leq t\leq\tau
, let
A(t)
be the generator of the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(S_{t}(s))_{s\geq 0}
on
X
. Then the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is stable if and only if there exist constants
M\geq 1
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A(t))
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
, and either one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(i)

For every finite sequence
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}\leq\tau
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and for
s_{j}\geq 0
,
j=1,\ldots,k
,

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{j=1}^{k}S_{t_{j}}(s_{j})\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq M\exp\left(\omega\sum_{j=1}^{k}s_{j}\right).

(ii)

For every finite sequence
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}\leq\tau
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and for
\lambda_{j}>\omega
,
j=1,\ldots,k
,

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{j=1}^{k}R(\lambda_{j},A(t_{j}))\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq M\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}-\omega}.

We want to emphasize once again that the stability of a family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is not the only condition that has to be satisfied in order to guarantee the existence of a unique solution of the evolution problem (
32
) associated with
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
. For instance, there has to exist a densely and continuously embedded subspace
Y
of
X
such that for all
t\in[0,\tau]
, the space
Y
is an invariant subspace of the semigroup
(S_{t}(s))_{s\geq 0}
generated by
A(t)
, and the family
(\tilde{A}(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
given by
\tilde{A}(t)=A(t)_{|D(A(t))\cap Y}
,
t\in[0,\tau]
, has to be stable on
Y
. Together with another condition related to
Y
, one may show that there exists a unique
Y
-valued solution of (
32
). This holds equally for inhomogeneous evolution problems of the form

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=A(t)x(t)+f(t),\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq s<t\leq\tau,

\displaystyle x(s)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X,

provided that
f\in\mathcal{C}([s,\tau],X)
. However, we will not provide any further details concerning this topic. Instead, we refer to
[
23
, Chapter 5]
for more information.

In this chapter, we have defined formally adjoint operators of differential operators defined on Sobolev spaces, and collected some facts about operator semigroups and their infinitesimal generators. Furthermore, we have examined the abstract Cauchy problem associated with operators generating a
C_{0}
-semigroup. After a quick discussion on abstract control systems and the concept of admissible control operators, we have studied the extension of abstract Cauchy problems to evolution problems, where the associated operator is replaced by a time-dependent family of infinitesimal generators, and presented one important criterion for well-posedness of such systems. We have now set the foundation for a comprehensive analysis of the systems considered in the following chapters, namely infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems in Chapter
4
, and said systems of order 1 which are coupled by a moving interface in Chapter
5
.

Chapter 4
Infinite-Dimensional Port-Hamiltonian Systems

In this chapter, we are going to investigate infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems. Throughout the chapter, we will restrict ourselves to systems with a 1-dimensional spatial domain
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
. We start again with some motivational examples in Section
14
, specifically the model of a lossless transmission line in Subsection
14.1
and the model of a vibrating string in Subsection
14.2
. We will see that the evaluation of the co-energy variables at the boundary of the spatial domain play a fundamental role. Hence, in contrast to the finite-dimensional case, we need to introduce further port variables in order to derive a port-Hamiltonian formulation of infinite-dimensional systems, which will be called boundary port-Hamiltonian systems. The examples can be found in
[
11
, Chapter 4]
,
[
15
]
,
[
27
, Chapter 14]
, or in the introductory chapter of
[
31
]
. Subsequently, in Section
15
we will generalize the obtained systems considered in Section
14
and study boundary port-Hamiltonian systems which are associated with formally skew-symmetric differential operators. To that end, we will define suitable matrices and operators in order to give a proper representation of the boundary port variables constituting the infinite-dimensional Dirac structure. Following this, we are able to define the bond space and a suitable power pairing to finally define the underlying Dirac structure associated with such systems, and to specify the dynamics with respect to these so-called Stokes-Dirac structures. This section heavily relies on
[
15
]
and the Sections 2.1 and 2.2 in
[
31
]
. At the end of this chapter, we will shortly discuss the analysis of boundary port-Hamiltonian systems.

We want to stress that we will not introduce an abstract mathematical object or a particular solution concept for the systems considered in this chapter. As a consequence, the notion of a system should be understood as a formal concept rather than a general definition.

Unless stated otherwise, we consider the Hilbert space
X=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})
equipped with the inner product

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{a}^{b}g^{\top}(z)f(z)\,dz,\hskip 14.22636ptf,g\in X.

(36)

14
Motivating Examples

We want to look for a common structure in the models describing the dynamics of various systems. We seek to rewrite the system models as an abstract Cauchy problem of the form

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=Ax(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

\displaystyle x(0)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X,

where
x\colon[0,\infty)\to X
is the state variable, and
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
is a linear operator, as we have discussed in Section
11
in more detail. For the analysis of such systems we use the framework of operator semigroups. In this section, we want to present two examples from which we will infer a general structure for a class of partial differential equations.

Beforehand, we want to clarify the notion of the variational derivative for a special class of functionals based on
[
21
, Section 4.1]
, and give a quick example.

Definition 14.1

(Variational Derivative)
.

Let
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
be a functional defined by

\displaystyle H(x)=\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{H}(x(z))\,dz,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,

(37)

with
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}})
. The variational derivative
\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}\colon X\to X
of
H
given by

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}(x)=\left(\frac{\delta H}{\delta x_{1}}(x),\ldots,\frac{\delta H}{\delta x_{n}}(x)\right),\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,

is an operator uniquely determined by the requirement that it satisfies

\displaystyle H(x+\epsilon\eta)=H(x)+\epsilon\int_{a}^{b}\left(\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}(x)\right)^{\top}(z)\eta(z)\,dz+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})

for any
x\in X,
and for any variation
\epsilon\eta
, where
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
,
\eta\in X
, and
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
is the big O notation with respect to
\epsilon^{2}
.

Example 14.2

(cf. Section 1.6 in
[
31
]
)
.

Consider the functional

\displaystyle H(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}x^{\top}(z)\left(\mathcal{Q}x\right)(z)\,dz,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,

(38)

where
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is a coercive operator on
X
. Then we have for all
x,\eta\in X
,
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle H(x+\epsilon\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}(x(z)+\epsilon\eta(z))^{\top}\left(\mathcal{Q}(x+\epsilon\eta\right)(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}x^{\top}(z)\left(\mathcal{Q}x\right)(z)+\epsilon\left(x^{\top}(z)\left(\mathcal{Q}\eta\right)(z)+\eta^{\top}(z)\left(\mathcal{Q}x\right)(z)\right)+\epsilon^{2}\eta^{\top}(z)\left(\mathcal{Q}\eta\right)(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=H(x)+\epsilon\int_{a}^{b}\left(\mathcal{Q}x\right)^{\top}(z)\eta(z)\,dz+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}).

From this we deduce that
\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}(x)=\mathcal{Q}x
.

\Diamond

For functionals of the form (
37
) one can show that the variational derivative is induced by the gradient of the integrand
\mathcal{H}
in the following way:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta x}H(x)(z)=\nabla\mathcal{H}(x(z)),\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,\,z\in[a,b].

14.1
The Lossless Transmission Line

Figure 4:
Transmission Line
[
17
, Figure 7.2]

Consider an ideal transmission line on the spatial interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
, as depicted in Figure
4
. Telegrapher’s equations constitute the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial Q}{\partial t}(z,t)&=-\frac{\partial I}{\partial z}(z,t)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\varphi(z,t)}{L(z)},\\
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}(z,t)&=-\frac{\partial V}{\partial z}(z,t)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{Q(z,t)}{C(z)}.\end{split}

(39)

Here,
Q(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}
denote the charge and the magnetic flux-linkage at position
z\in[a,b]
and at time
t\geq 0
, respectively, and
C(z)>0
and
L(z)>0
are the distributed capacity and the distributed inductance of the transmission line at
z\in[a,b]
, repsectively. Furthermore, the current and the voltage are given by
I=\frac{\varphi}{L}
and
V=\frac{Q}{C}
, respectively. The electro-magnetic energy density
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(Q,\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{Q^{2}}{C}+\frac{\varphi^{2}}{L}\right),\hskip 14.22636pt(Q,\varphi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The system’s Hamiltonian
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
, describing the internally stored energy, is the integral of the electro-magnetic energy density
\mathcal{H}
: letting
x=(Q,\varphi)\in X
we have

\displaystyle H(Q,\varphi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{Q(z)^{2}}{C(z)}+\frac{\varphi(z)^{2}}{L(z)}\,dz=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\begin{bmatrix}Q(z)&\varphi(z)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{C(z)}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{L(z)}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}Q(z)\\
\varphi(z)\end{bmatrix}\,dz.

(40)

Assuming that both the capacity
C
and the inductance
L
are bounded on
[a,b]
, we may define the coercive multiplication operator
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}x)(z)=\mathcal{Q}(z)x(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{C(z)}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{L(z)}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}Q(z)\\
\varphi(z)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,\,z\in[a,b].

Thus, the Hamiltonian
H
from (
40
) can be written as
H(x)=\frac{1}{2}\langle x,\mathcal{Q}x\rangle_{L^{2}}
for
x\in X
.

As we have seeen in the finite-dimensional case (cf. Section
5
), the co-energy variables correspond to the gradient of the system’s Hamiltonian function. In the infinite-dimensional case, this generalizes to the variational derivative of the Hamiltonian functional.
As shown in Example
14.2
, if a functional is of the form (
38
), then the variational derivative is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}(x)=\mathcal{Q}x=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{Q}{C}\\
\frac{\varphi}{L}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx=(Q,\varphi)\in X.

Altogether, we may equivalently rewrite the system (
39
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\begin{bmatrix}Q\\
\varphi\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\\
-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\delta H}{\delta Q}(Q,\varphi)\\
\frac{\delta H}{\delta\varphi}(Q,\varphi)\end{bmatrix}.

(41)

This system is called an
infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian system
defined with respect to the matrix differential operator
\mathcal{J}\colon D(\mathcal{J})\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle D(\mathcal{J})

\displaystyle=H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}x

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&-\frac{d}{dz}\\
-\frac{d}{dz}&0\end{bmatrix}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathcal{J}),

and with respect to the Hamiltonian
H
defined in (
40
). Necessarily, the matrix differential operator
\mathcal{J}
has to satisfy two properties, namely formal skew-symmetry and the Jacobi identity, as explained in
[
21
, Chapter 7]
. We will only verify the formal skew-symmetry in the sense of Definition
9.4
. To this end, let
e=(e_{1},e_{2})
,
\tilde{e}=(\tilde{e}_{1},\tilde{e}_{2})\in H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2})
be some vectors vanishing at the boundary. Then integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip 14.22636pt\langle\mathcal{J}e,\tilde{e}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle e,\mathcal{J}\tilde{e}\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=-\int_{a}^{b}\tilde{e}_{1}(z)\frac{de_{2}}{dz}(z)+\tilde{e}_{2}(z)\frac{de_{1}}{dz}(z)+e_{1}(z)\frac{d\tilde{e}_{2}}{dz}(z)+e_{2}(z)\frac{d\tilde{e}_{1}}{dz}(z)\,dz\\
&=-\big[e_{1}(z)\tilde{e}_{2}(z)+e_{2}(z)\tilde{e}_{1}(z)\big]_{a}^{b}\\
&=0.\end{split}

(42)

Since
\mathcal{J}
is not modulated by the state variable
x
, Corollary 7.5 in
[
21
]
yields that
\mathcal{J}
is a Hamiltonian operator (in the sense of
[
21
, Definition 7.1]
).

Analogously to the finite-dimensional case, the Hamiltonian structure provides energy conservation: assume that
x\colon[0,\infty)\to X
is a classical solution of (
41
) which is vanishing at the boundary. Then by symmetry of the operator
\mathcal{Q}
and by formal skew-symmetry of
\mathcal{J}
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}H(x(\cdot,t))&=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\langle x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\frac{\partial x}{\partial t}(\cdot,t),\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t),\frac{\partial x}{\partial t}(\cdot,t)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{J}\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{J}\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=0.\end{split}

(43)

We see that the Hamiltonian formulation imposes that there must not occur any energy exchange at the boundaries of the transmission line with the rest of the circuit, and therefore the total energy is conserved. However, this assumption is quite restrictive, and for the purpose of control and for the interconnection of two or more Hamiltonian systems it is essential to take the interaction with
its environment into account. This motivates to introduce a new type of port variables, called
boundary port variables
, and thus to extend the (port-)Hamiltonian framework to boundary port-Hamiltonian systems in the infinite-dimensional case.

Now, if the state variables do not vanish at the boundary of the spatial domain, then from (
42
) we find that some boundary terms appear. Indeed, the energy is not conserved in general, but satisfies the following energy-balance equation, which again can be derived from (
42
) and (
43
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{dt}H(x(\cdot,t))&=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{J}\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{J}\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[2\frac{Q(z,t)}{C(z)}\frac{\varphi(z,t)}{L(z)}\right]_{a}^{b}\\
&=-\big[V(z,t)I(z,t)\big]_{a}^{b}.\end{split}

(44)

Note that the product of voltage and current equals power. Hence, equation (
44
) states that the change of internally stored energy solely occurs via the spatial boundary. This suggests to introduce the evaluation not of the state variables, but of the co-energy variables
V=\frac{Q}{C}
and
I=\frac{\varphi}{L}
at the spatial boundary as new port variables in order to involve the power flow through the boundary of the system (
41
). We will postpone the definition of the boundary port variables to Section
15
.

Remark 14.3

(Energy Space)
.

As
H(x)=\frac{1}{2}\langle x,\mathcal{Q}x\rangle_{L^{2}}
,
x\in X
, and as
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is a bounded and coercive operator, one usually endows the state space
X
with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}}
defined by

\displaystyle\langle x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}}:=\frac{1}{2}\langle x,\mathcal{Q}y\rangle_{L^{2}},\hskip 14.22636ptx,y\in X.

(45)

Note that for all
z\in[a,b]
we have
mI\leq\mathcal{Q}(z)\leq MI
for some
0<m\leq M
. Thus, the standard
L^{2}
-norm is equivalent to the norm defined in (
45
), as we have for all
f\in L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\frac{m}{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}\leq\frac{M}{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

The main reason for doing so is that in this way, the squared norm of the state
x\in X
equals the Hamiltonian, i.e., the internally stored energy, of the system:

\displaystyle\|x(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\langle x(\cdot,t),\mathcal{Q}x(\cdot,t)\rangle_{L^{2}}=H(x(\cdot,t)),\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

(46)

For this choice of the inner product,
X
is known as the energy space. We have already mentioned the usefulness of the relation (
46
) in Example
11.4
.

14.2
The Vibrating String

Figure 5:
Vibrating String

Consider a vibrating string of the length
L=b-a>0
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, held stationary at both ends and free to vibrate transversely. The scenario is illustrated in Figure
5
. The vibration of the string can be modeled by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial w^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}(z,t)=\frac{1}{(\rho A)(z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left(T(z)\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}(z,t)\right),

(47)

where
w(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the vertical displacement of the string at position
z\in[a,b]
, and at time
t\geq 0
, and
T(z)>0
and
(\rho A)(z)>0
,
z\in[a,b]
denote the Young’s modulus of the string and the linear mass density, respectively, which are assumed to be bounded. We take the momentum
p=(\rho A)\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}
and the strain
\epsilon=\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}
to constitute the state variable
x=(p,\epsilon)\in X
. The Hamiltonian
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
of this system is then given by the sum of the kinetic and the elastic energies in the following way:

\displaystyle H(p,\epsilon)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{(\rho A)(z)}p(z)^{2}+T(z)\epsilon(z)^{2}\,dz=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\begin{bmatrix}p(z)&\epsilon(z)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{(\rho A)(z)}&0\\
0&T(z)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}p(z)\\
\epsilon(z)\end{bmatrix}\,dz.

(48)

Again, we may define the coercive multiplication operator
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}x)(z)=\mathcal{Q}(z)x(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{(\rho A)(z)}&0\\
0&T(z)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}p(z)\\
\epsilon(z)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X,\,z\in[a,b].

Additionally, as the Hamiltonian is once again of the form (
38
), the variational derivative of the Hamiltonian
H
equals the coercive matrix operator
\mathcal{Q}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H}{\delta x}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\delta H}{\delta p}(p,\epsilon)\\
\frac{\delta H}{\delta\epsilon}(p,\epsilon)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{p}{\rho A}\\
T\epsilon\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}\\
T\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

Altogether, we may equivalently rewrite equation (
47
) as an infinite-dimensional, first-order Hamiltonian system of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\begin{bmatrix}p\\
\epsilon\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\delta H}{\delta p}(p,\epsilon)\\
\frac{\delta H}{\delta\epsilon}(p,\epsilon)\end{bmatrix}

(49)

with respect to the (Hamiltonian) matrix differential operator
\mathcal{J}\colon D(\mathcal{J})\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle D(\mathcal{J})

\displaystyle=H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}x

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\frac{d}{dz}\\
\frac{d}{dz}&0\end{bmatrix}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathcal{J}),

and with respect to the Hamiltonian
H
given in (
48
). The proof that
\mathcal{J}
is indeed a Hamiltonian operator (see again
[
21
, Definition 7.1]
) works exactly as in the previous example. For a classical solution
x\colon[0,\infty)\to X
of (
49
), the energy-balance equation of the system (
49
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(x(\cdot,t))=\left[\frac{p(z,t)}{(\rho A)(z)}T(z)\epsilon(z,t)\right]_{a}^{b},

(50)

where the product of the co-energy variables, namely the velocity
\frac{\delta H}{\delta p}(p,\epsilon)=\frac{p}{\rho A}
and the stress
\frac{\delta H}{\delta\epsilon}(p,\epsilon)=T\epsilon
, again equals power. In our particular scenario, the ends of the string are both fixed, that is, the velocity at the boundary is zero. In particular,
p(a,t)=p(b,t)=0
,
t\geq 0
, which is why in this case, the energy is conserved. However, if at least one end of the string may move freely or some force may be applied to it, the power flow through the boundary has to be encompassed, as depicted in (
50
).

Remark 14.4
.

We note that the model (
49
) of this system has the same structure as the model (
41
) of the lossless transmission line: the multiplication operator
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is coercive and represents the mechanical and physical properties of the system. Furthermore, the Hamiltonian operators can be written as
\mathcal{J}=P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}
, where the matrix
P_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
is symmetric (and invertible). Given the vibrating string example, the formal skew-symmetry of the differential operator
\mathcal{J}
implies that the kinetic energy is transformed into elastic energy and vice versa. Hence, the internally stored energy is conserved in the absence of any power flow through the boundary. The formally skew-symmetric differential operators can be regarded as the infinite-dimensional counterpart of the skew-symmetric matrices in the finite-dimensional case.

Just as in the finite-dimensional case there may also occur (internal) energy dissipation in infinite-dimensional systems. We refer to
[
11
, Subsection 4.1.3]
and
[
31
, Section 1.4]
and the references mentioned there. In the sequel, we will generalize the systems considered in Section
14
and only study idealized systems where any dissipation phenomena are neglected.

15
Boundary Port-Hamiltonian Systems Associated with Skew-symmetric Operators

We infer from the preceding examples that it is possible to describe a variety of PDEs with the following structure:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial x}{\partial t}(z,t)&=\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{Q}(z)x(z,t)),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,\\
x(z,0)&=x_{0}(z)\end{split}

(51)

on the state space
X=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
\frac{\delta}{\delta x}H=\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
is the variational derivative of the Hamiltonian
H\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle H(x)=\int_{a}^{b}\mathcal{H}(x(z))\,dz=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}x^{\top}(z)(\mathcal{Q}x)(z)\,dz,

(52)

where
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
is the Hamiltonian density. Furthermore, the (multiplication) operator
\mathcal{Q}
is assumed to be coercive.
Let the differential operator
\mathcal{J}\colon D(\mathcal{J})\subset X\to X
be given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(\mathcal{J})&=H^{N}\left([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}\right),\\
\mathcal{J}x&=\sum_{i=0}^{N}P_{i}\frac{d^{i}x}{dz^{i}},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathcal{J}),\end{split}

(53)

with
P_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
i=0,\ldots,N
, where
P_{N}
is supposed to be invertible. Furthermore, we assume that

\displaystyle P_{i}=(-1)^{i+1}P_{i}^{\top},\hskip 14.22636pti=0,\ldots,N,

(54)

that is, the constituting matrices of
\mathcal{J}
are skew-symmetric and symmetric in an alternating fashion. We will see that condition (
54
) implies that the differential operator
\mathcal{J}
is formally skew-symmetric. Recall from Remark
14.4
that the formal skew-symmetry corresponds to an energy-conserving exchange of energy between different physical domains. Finally, following Remark
14.3
, we endow the state space
X
with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}}
defined in (
45
), so that the Hamiltonian
H
can be rewritten as in (
46
).

In this section, we want to define the Dirac structure which is associated with systems of the form (
51
). As the examples in Section
14
suggested, we need to introduce boundary port variables to encompass the power flow through the boundary as well. In Subsection
15.1
we will define this new type of port variables by means of suitably chosen matrices and operators, and give a representation of the evaluation of the effort variables at the spatial boundary with respect to the boundary port variables. Subsection
15.2
deals with the right choice of the bond space
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
and a suitable power pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
constituting the bilinear form (the plus pairing)
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg\colon\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}
in order to define the underlying Stokes-Dirac structure associated with infinite-dimensional linear systems of the form (
51
), leading to the notion of boundary port-Hamiltonian systems.

15.1
Boundary Port Variables

Let us, to begin with, show that the differential operator
\mathcal{J}
defined by (
53
)-(
54
) is indeed formally skew-symmetric.

Theorem 15.0

(Theorem 2.1 in
[
31
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{J}
be the differential operator defined in (
53
)-(
54
). Then for any two functions
e_{1},e_{2}\in D(\mathcal{J})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{J}e_{1},e_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle e_{1},\mathcal{J}e_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\left[\begin{bmatrix}e_{1}^{\top}(z),\ldots,\frac{d^{N-1}e_{1}^{\top}}{dz^{N-1}}(z)\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}\begin{bmatrix}e_{2}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\frac{d^{N-1}e_{2}}{dz^{N-1}}(z)\end{bmatrix}\right]_{a}^{b},

(55)

with the symmetric matrix
\mathcal{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{nN\times nN}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\begin{bmatrix}P_{1}&P_{2}&P_{3}&\cdots&P_{N-1}&P_{N}\\
-P_{2}&-P_{3}&-P_{4}&\cdots&-P_{N}&0\\
P_{3}&P_{4}&\ddots&\ddots&0&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&\vdots\\
(-1)^{N-1}P_{N}&0&\cdots&\cdots&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(56)

In particular,
\mathcal{J}
is formally skew-symmetric.

Proof 15.1
.

The proof is rather tedious. The claim basically follows from iteratively applying integration by parts. We refer to
[
14
, Theorem 3.1]
.

In order to define a power-conserving interconnection structure in the infinite-dimensional case, we need to incorporate the evaluation of the effort variables at the spatial boundary into the definition of the respective power pairing, which we will specify later on. To this end, we will write the right-hand side of (
55
) subject to the boundary port variables. Note that these port variables will depend on the constituting matrices of
\mathcal{J}
, which are captured in the matrix
\mathcal{P}
defined in (
56
). Since we suppose that the matrix
P_{N}
is invertible, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{P}
is invertible as well.

In order to define the boundary port variables, we need to introduce further matrices.
First, let the matrix
\mathcal{P}_{\extern}\in\mathbb{R}^{2nN\times 2nN}
associated with the differential operator
\mathcal{J}
be defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\extern}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}&0\\
0&-\mathcal{P}\end{bmatrix},

where
\mathcal{P}
is the symmetric and invertible matrix from (
56
). Next, we factorize
\mathcal{P}_{\extern}
in such a way that it allows us to define the port variables, and simultaneously guarantees that the bilinear form - which has yet to be defined - becomes independent of the (matrix) coefficients of the operator
\mathcal{J}
, as we will see shortly.

Lemma 15.2

(Lemma 2.4 in
[
31
]
)
.

The matrix
R_{\extern}\in\mathbb{R}^{2nN\times 2nN}
defined as

\displaystyle R_{\extern}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}&-\mathcal{P}\\
I&I\end{bmatrix}

(57)

is invertible and satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\extern}=R_{\extern}^{\top}\Sigma R_{\extern},

where

\displaystyle\Sigma=\begin{bmatrix}0&I\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2nN\times 2nN}.

(58)

Furthermore, all possible matrices
R\in\mathbb{R}^{2nN\times 2nN}
satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{\extern}=R^{\top}\Sigma R
are of the form

\displaystyle R=UR_{\extern},

with
U\in\mathbb{R}^{2nN\times 2nN}
satisfying
U^{\top}\Sigma U=\Sigma
.

The proof of Lemma
15.2
is fairly easy. For details we refer to the proof of
[
15
, Lemma 3.4]
.

The essential part of defining the Dirac structure associated with the operator
\mathcal{J}
is to introduce the boundary port variables, which we are finally able to do.

Definition 15.3
.

The boundary port variables associated with the differential operator
\mathcal{J}
given by (
53
)-(
54
) are the vectors
f_{\partial},e_{\partial}\in\mathbb{R}^{nN}
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e}\\
e_{\partial,e}\end{bmatrix}:=R_{\extern}\trace(e),\hskip 14.22636pte\in D(\mathcal{J}),

(59)

where
\trace\colon H^{N}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathbb{R}^{2nN}
is the boundary trace operator defined in (
20
).

We usually write
\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}
instead of
\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e}\\
e_{\partial,e}\end{bmatrix}
whenever the dependence of the boundary port variables on the variable
e\in D(\mathcal{J})
is obvious. We call
f_{\partial}\in\mathbb{R}^{nN}
and
e_{\partial}\in\mathbb{R}^{nN}
the
boundary flow
and the
boundary effort
, respectively.

Using Definition
15.3
and Lemma
15.2
, we can rewrite the right-hand side of (
55
) in terms of the boundary port variables in the following way: for
e_{1},e_{2}\in H^{N}\left([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}&e_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\Sigma\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e_{2}}\\
e_{\partial,e_{2}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\trace(e_{1})^{\top}R_{\extern}^{\top}\Sigma R_{\extern}\trace(e_{2})

\displaystyle=\trace(e_{1})^{\top}\mathcal{P}_{\extern}\trace(e_{2})

\displaystyle=\trace(e_{1})^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}&0\\
0&-\mathcal{P}\end{bmatrix}\trace(e_{2})

\displaystyle=\left[\begin{bmatrix}e_{1}^{\top}(z)&\cdots&\frac{d^{N-1}e_{1}^{\top}}{dz^{N-1}}(z)\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{P}\begin{bmatrix}e_{2}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\frac{d^{N-1}e_{2}}{dz^{N-1}}(z)\end{bmatrix}\right]_{a}^{b}.

Thus, for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in D(\mathcal{J})
, equation (
55
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathcal{J}e_{1},e_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle e_{1},\mathcal{J}e_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}&=\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}&e_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\Sigma\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,e_{2}}\\
e_{\partial,e_{2}}\end{bmatrix}\\
&=f_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}e_{\partial,e_{2}}+e_{\partial,e_{1}}^{\top}f_{\partial,e_{2}}.\end{split}

(60)

Now, we are able to define the Dirac structure on a suitable bond space corresponding to the formally skew-symmetric operator
\mathcal{J}
.

15.2
Dirac Structure and Boundary Port-Hamiltonian Systems

We have already discussed that in the finite-dimensional case the space of flows
\mathcal{F}
and the space of efforts
\mathcal{E}
correspond to a finite-dimensional vector space and its dual space, with the power pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
of an element of the flow space and of an element of the effort space being equal to physical power (see Section
4
). In the present case the flow space
\mathcal{F}
is now an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, and the effort space
\mathcal{E}
is again to be defined as the dual space of the space of flows,
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. Throughout, we will identify
\mathcal{F}
with its dual
\mathcal{E}
.

In order to define a Dirac structure, we need to endow the bond space
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
not with the canonical inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{B}}
, but with the plus pairing
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
defined in (
5
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\ll(f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2})\gg\hskip 2.84544pt=\langle e^{1}\mid f^{2}\rangle+\langle e^{2}\mid f^{1}\rangle,\hskip 14.22636pt((f^{1},e^{1}),(f^{2},e^{2}))\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}.

(61)

This symmetric bilinear form depends on the choice of the power pairing
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle\colon\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
, which we will, together with the flow and effort spaces, specify shortly.

We may now define the notion of a Dirac structure on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space.

Definition 15.4
.

Let the flow space
\mathcal{F}
be an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, and let the effort space
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
be its dual space. A linear subspace
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{B}
of the bond space
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{E}
is called a Dirac structure if

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp},

where
\mathcal{D}^{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{D}
with respect to the plus pairing
\ll\cdot,\cdot\gg
defind in (
61
).

Note that this is precisely the characterization of a Dirac structure in the finite-dimensional case (cf. Proposition
2.4
), and that this implies that the Dirac structure
\mathcal{D}
captures the power-conserving interconnection structure of a system. For a general definition of an infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian system with total energy (
52
), see
[
31
, Definition 1.13]
.

In Subsection
15.1
we have defined boundary port variables which we need to incorporate into the definition of the power pairing and thus the plus pairing. By Definition
15.4
, this is necessary for defining the Dirac structure associated with
\mathcal{J}
defined by (
53
)-(
54
). First, we need to select the flow space and the effort space appropriately. Because the bilinear form contains both state space elements and their evaluation at the boundary, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{E}=L^{2}\left([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times\mathbb{R}^{nN},

(62)

endowed with the canonical inner product:

\displaystyle\langle(f^{1},f_{\partial}^{1}),(f^{2},f_{\partial}^{2})\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}=\langle f^{1},f^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle f_{\partial}^{1},f_{\partial}^{2}\rangle_{2},\hskip 14.22636pt(f^{1},f_{\partial}^{1}),(f^{2},f_{\partial}^{2})\in\mathcal{F}.
