\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+x^{\top}(z,\gamma_{1}(b-z))\left(-P_{1}^{-1}+\gamma_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\right)x(z,\gamma_{1}(b-z)).

Since
\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{+}
is bounded, we may find a constant
\kappa_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\leq\kappa_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in(0,b].

Furthermore, we may choose
\gamma_{1}
large enough such that
P_{1}^{-1}+\gamma_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)
and
-P_{1}^{-1}+\gamma_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)
are positive definite. The constant
\tau_{1}
has to be changed accordingly. This yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}F_{1}(z)\geq-\kappa_{1}\int_{\gamma_{1}(b-z)}^{\tau_{1}-\gamma_{1}(b-z)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Interface - Lemma Auxiliary Lemma for Exponential Stability - Function F1}}}{{=}}-\kappa_{1}F_{1}(z).

This implies that for all
z_{1}\in(0,b]
it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{z_{1}}^{b}\frac{\frac{d}{dz}F_{1}(z)}{F_{1}(z)}\,dz\geq-\kappa_{1}\int_{z_{1}}^{b}1\,dz.

Simple computations yield that

\displaystyle F_{1}(b)\geq F_{1}(z)e^{-\kappa_{1}(b-z)}\geq F_{1}(z)e^{-\kappa_{1}b},\hskip 14.22636ptz\in(0,b].

(160)

Next, we define
F_{2}\colon[a,0)\to(0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle F_{2}(z)=\int_{\gamma_{2}\frac{b}{|a|}(z-a)}^{\tau_{2}-\gamma_{2}\frac{b}{|a|}(z-a)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z,t)\,dt,\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,0),

where
\gamma_{2}>0
and
\tau_{2}>-2\gamma_{2}a\frac{b}{|a|}=2\gamma_{2}b
. With similar arguments we may choose
\gamma_{2}
,
\tau_{2}
, and
\kappa_{2}>0
large enough such that

\displaystyle F_{2}(z)\leq F_{2}(a)e^{\kappa_{2}(z-a)}\leq F_{2}(a)e^{-\kappa_{2}a},\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,0).

(161)

Now define
\gamma=\max\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\}
,
\tau=\max\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\}
,
\kappa=\max\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}
, and let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be defined with respect to
\gamma
and
\tau
.

As
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
is of type
C_{0}(1,0)
, we have
\|x(t_{2})\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\leq\|x(t_{1})\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
for all
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma b}^{\tau-\gamma b}\|x(t)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\,dt\geq\|x(\tau-\gamma b)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\int_{\gamma b}^{\tau-\gamma b}1\,dt=(\tau-2\gamma b)\|x(\tau-\gamma b)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}.

(162)

Applying Fubini’s theorem, using the estimates (
160
)-(
162
), and since
\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z),\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)\geq mI
for all
z\in[a,b]
, we infer that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt2(\tau-2\gamma b)\|x(\tau)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\tau-2\gamma b)\|x(\tau-\gamma b)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\gamma b}^{\tau-\gamma b}\|x(t)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\gamma b}^{\tau-\gamma b}\int_{a}^{0}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z,t)\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z,t)\,dz\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{0}\int_{\gamma(b-0)}^{\tau-\gamma(b-0)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\gamma(b-0)}^{\tau-\gamma(b-0)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{0}\int_{\gamma\frac{b}{|a|}(0-a)}^{\tau-\gamma\frac{b}{|a|}(0-a)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\gamma(b-0)}^{\tau-\gamma(b-0)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a}^{0}\int_{\gamma\frac{b}{|a|}(z-a)}^{\tau-\gamma\frac{b}{|a|}(z-a)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\gamma(b-z)}^{\tau-\gamma(b-z)}x^{\top}(z,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z,t)\,dt\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{0}F_{2}(z)\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}F_{1}(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq-aF_{2}(a)e^{-\kappa a}+bF_{1}(b)e^{\kappa b}

\displaystyle=-ae^{-\kappa a}\int_{0}^{\tau}x^{\top}(a,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)x(a,t)\,dt+be^{\kappa b}\int_{0}^{\tau}x^{\top}(b,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(b)x(b,t)\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-ae^{-\kappa a}}{m}\int_{0}^{\tau}\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)x(a,t)\|_{2}^{2}\,dt+\frac{be^{\kappa b}}{m}\int_{0}^{\tau}\|\mathcal{Q}_{0}(b)x(b,t)\|_{2}^{2}\,dt.

Letting
C=\frac{\max\left\{-ae^{-\kappa a},be^{\kappa b}\right\}}{2m(\tau-2\gamma b)}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|x(\tau)\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{\tau}\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)x(a,t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\mathcal{Q}^{+}(b)x(b,t)\|_{2}^{2}\,dt.

This shows (
158
) and, hence, the claim.

Lemma
19.14
shows that there exists some time
\tau>0
such that the energy of a state can be bounded by the sum of the accumulated energies at both boundaries during the time interval
[0,\tau]
. It allows us to prove the following sufficient condition for exponential stability.

Theorem 19.15
.

Let the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold. Assume there exists some
k>0
such that

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x_{0},x_{0}\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq-k\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right)

(163)

holds for all
x_{0}\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
. Then
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
generates an exponentially stable
C_{0}
-semigroup.

Proof 19.16
.

This follows immediately from Lemma
19.14
and the proof of Theorem 9.1.3 in
[
17
]
.

Lastly, we want to present another sufficient condition for the exponential stability of the port-Hamiltonian system (
124
). Once again, we follow the exposition in
[
17
, Chapter 9]
.

Theorem 19.16
.

Let the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold. If the matrix
W_{B}
satisfies
W_{B}\Sigma W_{B}^{\top}>0
, then
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
generates an exponentially stable
C_{0}
-semigroup.

Proof 19.17
.

By Lemma
19.8
, we may write

\displaystyle W_{B}=S\begin{bmatrix}I+V&I-V\end{bmatrix},

where
S\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
is invertible and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
satisfies
VV^{\top}<I
. Moreover, define the matrix

\displaystyle W_{C}:=\begin{bmatrix}I+V^{\top}&-I+V^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}.

As shown in the proof of Lemma 9.1.4 in
[
17
]
, the matrix
W_{1}:=\begin{bmatrix}W_{B}\\
W_{C}\end{bmatrix}
is invertible.

Now, let
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
. By Lemma
19.9
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I-V\\
-I-V\end{bmatrix}\lambda_{x}

for some
\lambda_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By virtue of (
136
), we compute that

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}+\langle x,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\langle\mathcal{J}_{0}\mathcal{Q}_{0}x,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle\mathcal{Q}_{0}x,\mathcal{J}_{0}\mathcal{Q}_{0}x\rangle_{L^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\langle e_{\partial},f_{\partial}\rangle_{2}-e_{I}f_{I}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{x}^{\top}(-I+V^{\top}V)\lambda_{x}-e_{I}f_{I}.

Furthermore, it holds that

\displaystyle y_{x}:=W_{C}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I+V^{\top}&-I+V^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}I-V\\
-I-V\end{bmatrix}\lambda_{x}=2(I-V^{\top}V)\lambda_{x}.

Since
V^{\top}V-I<0
, the matrix
-I+V^{\top}V
is invertible, and
(-I+V^{\top}V)^{-1}\leq-\hat{m}_{1}I
for some
\hat{m}_{1}>0
. Combining the preceding equations and using the passivity condition (
142
) of the interface port variables, we obtain the following estimate:

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}=\frac{1}{8}y_{x}^{\top}(-I+V^{\top}V)^{-1}y_{x}-\frac{1}{2}e_{I}f_{I}\leq-m_{1}\|y_{x}\|_{2}^{2},

(164)

with
m_{1}=\frac{\hat{m}_{1}}{8}
. Using the definition of
y_{x}
and the boundary port variables (
130
), we infer

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
y_{x}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}W_{B}\\
W_{C}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=W_{1}R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\\
(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\end{bmatrix}:=W\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\\
(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\end{bmatrix}.

(165)

As both
R_{\extern}
and
W_{1}
are invertible, the matrix
W=W_{1}R_{\extern}\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times 4}
is invertible as well, and there exists some
m_{2}>0
such that
\|Ww\|_{2}^{2}\geq m_{2}\|w\|_{2}^{2}
holds for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Consequently, taking the norm on both sides of (
165
) yields

\displaystyle\|y_{x}\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|W\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\\
(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq m_{2}\left\|\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\\
(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}=m_{2}\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(166)

Finally, inserting (
166
) into (
164
), we obtain for all
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
,

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\leq-m_{1}\|y_{x}\|_{2}^{2}\leq-m_{1}m_{2}\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right).

This is exactly inequality (
163
), and so Lemma
19.15
yields the exponential stability of the contraction semigroup generated by
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
.

In this section, we have shown that if we impose certain boundary conditions, then we can ensure that the semigroup
(T(t))_{t\geq 0}
generated by the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
is of type
C_{0}(1,-\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|T(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})}\leq e^{-\alpha},\hskip 14.22636ptt\geq 0.

As opposed to the boundary conditions, the interface condition plays a minor role in the proof of Theorem
19.16
. One only has to ensure that there is no power inflow at the interface position.

19.4
The Adjoint Operator

This section aims to specify the Hilbert space adjoint
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}
of the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
defined in (
143
). To this end, we need an auxiliary lemma. For the proof, we follow the idea of the second assertion in
[
17
, Lemma 7.2.1]
.

Lemma 19.18
.

Consider the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
defined in (
143
). Then for every vector

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}\in\ker(W_{B})

there exists an element
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
such that

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}.

(167)

Proof 19.19
.

Let
\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}\in\ker(W_{B})
, and define

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u\\
y\end{bmatrix}=R_{\extern}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix},

with
R_{\extern}
given in (
131
). Now, consider
x_{0}\in X
defined as

\displaystyle x_{0}(z)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}^{-1}(z)\left(\frac{(b-z)z}{(b-a)a}y+\frac{(z-a)z}{(b-a)b}u\right),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

One can readily see that
\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0}\in H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset D(\mathcal{J}_{0})
. Furthermore, we compute

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(a)

\displaystyle=y,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(b)

\displaystyle=u.

Thus,
x_{0}
satisfies the interface condition (
142
), and by definition of the boundary port variables (
130
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0}}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0}}\end{bmatrix}=R_{\extern}\trace_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})=R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(b)\\
(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x_{0})(a)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}\in\ker(W_{B}).

Altogether,
x_{0}
is an element of
D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
and it satisfies (
167
).

The proof of the following theorem is based on the proof of Theorem 2.24 in
[
31
]
.

Theorem 19.19
.

Consider the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
defined in (
143
). Assume that the matrix
W_{B}
may be written as (
144
), with
S\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
invertible and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
satisfying
V^{\top}V\leq I
. On the energy space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
, the Hilbert space adjoint
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}\colon D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})\subset X\to X
of the operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})&=\left\{y\in X\hskip 2.84544pt\Big|\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{Q}_{0}y\in D(\mathcal{J}_{0}),\hskip 2.84544ptf_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}=-re_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y},\hskip 2.84544pt\begin{bmatrix}-(I+V^{\top})&I+V^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\end{bmatrix}=0\right\},\\
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}y&=-\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}y),\hskip 14.22636pty\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}).\end{split}

(168)

Proof 19.20
.

Recall from Section
9
that the domain of the Hilbert space adjoint can be written as (
19
). Let
x\in\mathbf{H}_{0}^{1}(a,b)\times H_{0}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R})\subset D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
, with
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{1}(a,b)
the space of
H^{1}
-functions vanishing both at the boundary and the interface defined in (
93
). If
y\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
, then the following holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x),\mathcal{Q}_{0}y\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}=\langle x,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}y\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{Q}_{0}x,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}y\rangle_{L^{2}}.

From this equation we infer that
\mathcal{Q}_{0}y
has to be an element of
D(\mathcal{J}_{0})
for all
y\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
. This is the first constitutive relation of
D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
, and it allows us to define boundary and interface port variables with respect to
\mathcal{Q}_{0}y
. By virtue of equation (
136
), we get for all
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
,
y\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}&=\langle\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x),\mathcal{Q}_{0}y\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
&=-\langle\mathcal{Q}_{0}x,\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}y)\rangle_{L^{2}}+\langle e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y},f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\rangle_{2}+\langle e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x},f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\rangle_{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}-f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}.\end{split}

(169)

We need to get rid of the boundary and interface terms. With
\Sigma
defined in (
58
), we may write

\displaystyle\langle e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y},f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\rangle_{2}+\langle e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x},f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\rangle_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}^{\top}&e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\Sigma\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\end{bmatrix}.

By assumption,
\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\end{bmatrix}\in\ker(W_{B})
for all
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
. By virtue of Lemma
19.9
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I+V\\
-(I+V)\end{bmatrix}\lambda_{x}

for some
\lambda_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Lemma
19.18
yields that for every
\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}\in\ker(W_{B})
there exists some
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
such that
\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}f\\
e\end{bmatrix}
. Now, since
\dim(\ker(W_{B}))=2
, the equality

\displaystyle\lambda_{x}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}I+V^{\top}&-(I+V^{\top})\end{bmatrix}\Sigma\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\end{bmatrix}=0

has to hold for every
\lambda_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Hence, the second constitutive condition of
D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}\end{bmatrix}\in\ker\left(\begin{bmatrix}-(I+V^{\top})&I+V^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\right).

Lastly, we need to get rid of the interface terms. Due to the interface relation (
142
), we can write

\displaystyle f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}+f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}=re_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}+f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0.

Hence, the last constitutive condition of
D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
is given by

\displaystyle f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}=-re_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{0}y}.

Altogether, the domain of the adjoint operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}
is precisely given as in (
168
). For all
x\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
,
y\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast})
, equation (
169
) becomes

\displaystyle\langle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}x),\mathcal{Q}_{0}y\rangle_{L^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{Q}_{0}x,\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}y)\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle x,-\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}y)\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}.

Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}y=-\mathcal{J}_{0}(\mathcal{Q}_{0}y),\hskip 14.22636pty\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{\ast}).

This completes the proof.

19.5
Resolvent associated with
\mathbf{A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}}

In this section, we want to determine the resolvent operator
R(\lambda,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
of the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
defined in (
143
) for
\lambda\in(0,\infty)\subset\rho(A)
. For that purpose, we will basically replicate Step 3 in the proof of Theorem
19.9
. Recall that for
\mathcal{Q}_{0}\equiv I
, the solution of
(I-A_{I})x=y
for some
y\in X
is given by
\varphi_{y}(\cdot;x(a),x(0^{+}))\in D(A_{I})
defined in (
148
), (
149
), and (
152
). The initial value
x(a)
can be calculated through (
154
), and
x(0^{+})
is given by

\displaystyle x(0^{+})=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}(0^{+})\\
x_{2}(0)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{r}\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}+\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}\\
\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

This yields

\displaystyle\|(I-A)^{-1}y\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\varphi_{y}(\cdot;x(a),x(0^{+}))\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\|\varphi_{y}(z;x(a),x(0^{+}))\|_{2}^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\left[\left(c_{0}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))+\overline{c}_{0}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\right)_{1}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left[\left(c_{0}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))+\overline{c}_{0}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\right)_{2}\right]^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{0}\|\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))\|_{2}^{2}\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}\|\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\|_{2}^{2}\,dz.

Thus, the solution of the equation
(\lambda I-A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})x=y
for some
y\in X
,
\lambda>0
coincides with
R(\lambda,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})y
. Subsequently, we will exploit this fact in order to compute the resolvent operator of
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
.

To this end, consider again the energy space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
with
\mathcal{Q}_{0}=c_{0}\mathcal{Q}^{-}+\overline{c}_{0}\mathcal{Q}^{+}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
a coercive matrix operator, and let the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold, where
r>0
. Let
y\in X
and
\lambda>0
be arbitary. Recall that on the complementary domains
[a,0)
and
(0,b]
, the operator
\mathcal{J}_{0}
given in (
127
) acts as the differential operator
P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}
, with
P_{1}
defined in (
128
). Thus, the equation
(\lambda I-A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})x=y
can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\lambda x^{-}-P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x^{-})

\displaystyle=y^{-}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{on }[a,0),

\displaystyle\lambda x^{+}-P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x^{+})

\displaystyle=y^{+}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{on }(0,b].

Since
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
, observe that we may write

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dz}x^{\pm}(z)=(\mathcal{Q}^{\pm})^{-1}(z)\left(\lambda P_{1}-\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(z)\right)x^{\pm}(z)-(\mathcal{Q}^{\pm})^{-1}(z)P_{1}y^{\pm}(z)

on the respective subdomain.
The variation of constants formula yields

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,y}^{-}(z;x(a))=\Lambda_{\lambda}^{-}(z,a)x(a)-\int_{a}^{z}\Lambda_{\lambda}^{-}(z,s)(\mathcal{Q}^{-})^{-1}(s)P_{1}y^{-}(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,0),

and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,y}^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))=\Lambda_{\lambda}^{+}(z,0)x(0^{+})-\int_{0}^{z}\Lambda_{\lambda}^{+}(z,s)(\mathcal{Q}^{+})^{-1}(s)P_{1}y^{+}(s)\,ds,\hskip 14.22636ptz\in(0,b],

with
\Lambda_{\lambda}^{-}(\cdot,a)
and
\Lambda_{\lambda}^{+}(\cdot,0^{+})
the respective transition matrices defined on the respective subdomain.
The composed solution of
(I-A)x=y
is therefore given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\lambda,y}(z;x(a),x(0^{+}))=c_{0}(z)\varphi_{\lambda,y}^{-}(z;x(a))+\overline{c}_{0}(z)\varphi_{\lambda,y}^{+}(z;x(0^{+})),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

For the remainder of this section, we will omit indicating the dependence on
\lambda
and
y
. Analogously to the proof of Theorem
19.9
, the initial value
x(a)
is chosen such that the boundary condition

\displaystyle W_{B}R_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Q}(b)\varphi(b;x(a))\\
\mathcal{Q}(a)\varphi(a;x(a))\end{bmatrix}=0

is satisfied, and the value
x(0^{+})
is determined by means of the interface relations. In the following, we will compute those values.

According to (
132
)-(
133
), the interface port variables are given as follows:

\displaystyle f_{I}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}=\left(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(0)x(0^{+})\right)_{2}

\displaystyle-e_{I}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(0)x(0^{+})\right)_{1}-\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}.

More precisely,

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{I}&=Q_{12}^{-}(0)\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}+Q_{22}^{-}(0)\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}\\
&=Q_{12}^{+}(0)x_{1}(0^{+})+Q_{22}^{+}(0)x_{2}(0^{+}).\end{split}

(170)

The exact value of
f_{I}
is defined through the first equation. Furthermore,

\displaystyle-e_{I}

\displaystyle=Q_{11}^{+}(0)x_{1}(0^{+})+Q_{12}^{+}(0)x_{2}(0^{+})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\left(Q_{11}^{-}(0)\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}+Q_{12}^{-}(0)\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}\right).

The entries of the initial value
x(0^{+})
can be determined by exploiting the passivity relation (
142
): we have

\displaystyle f_{I}=re_{I}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle Q_{11}^{+}(0)x_{1}(0^{+})+Q_{12}^{+}(0)x_{2}(0^{+})=-\frac{1}{r}f_{I}+\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\hskip 14.22636pt

\displaystyle x_{1}(0^{+})=-\frac{1}{rQ_{11}^{+}(0)}f_{I}+\frac{1}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}-\frac{Q_{12}^{+}(0)}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}x_{2}(0^{+}).

(171)

Plugging this into the second equation of (
170
) yields

\displaystyle f_{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q_{12}^{+}(0)}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}\left(-\frac{1}{r}f_{I}+\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(-\frac{(Q_{12}^{+}(0))^{2}}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}+Q_{22}^{+}(0)\right)x_{2}(0^{+}),

whence

\displaystyle x_{2}(0^{+})=\frac{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}{(Q_{12}^{+}(0))^{2}+Q_{11}^{+}(0)Q_{22}^{+}(0)}\left(f_{I}-\frac{Q_{12}^{+}(0)}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}\left(-\frac{1}{r}f_{I}+\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}\right)\right).

Thus, the initial value
x(0^{+})
is uniquely defined and depends on the initial value
x(a)
. Letting
\tilde{c}_{1}=\frac{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}{(Q_{12}^{+}(0))^{2}+Q_{11}^{+}(0)Q_{22}^{+}(0)}\left(1+\frac{Q_{12}^{+}(0)}{rQ_{11}^{+}(0)}\right)
,
\tilde{c}_{2}=-\frac{Q_{12}^{+}(0)}{(Q_{12}^{+}(0))^{2}+Q_{11}^{+}(0)Q_{22}^{+}(0)}
, we may write

\displaystyle x_{2}(0^{+})=\tilde{c}_{1}f_{I}+\tilde{c}_{2}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}.

Furthermore, letting
\tilde{c}_{3}=-\frac{1+rQ_{12}^{+}(0)\tilde{c}_{1}}{rQ_{11}^{+}(0)}
,
\tilde{c}_{4}=\frac{1-Q_{12}^{+}(0)\tilde{c}_{2}}{Q_{11}^{+}(0)}
, and inserting
x_{2}(0^{+})
in (
171
), we get

\displaystyle x_{1}(0^{+})=\tilde{c}_{3}f_{I}+\tilde{c}_{4}\left(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(0)\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}.

Finally, the choices

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=\tilde{c}_{3}Q_{12}^{-}(0)+\tilde{c}_{4}Q_{11}^{-}(0),\hskip 8.5359ptc_{2}=\tilde{c}_{3}Q_{22}^{-}(0)+\tilde{c}_{4}Q_{12}^{-}(0),

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=\tilde{c}_{1}Q_{12}^{-}(0)+\tilde{c}_{2}Q_{11}^{-}(0),\hskip 8.5359ptc_{4}=\tilde{c}_{1}Q_{22}^{-}(0)+\tilde{c}_{2}Q_{12}^{-}(0)

yield

\displaystyle x_{1}(0^{+})

\displaystyle=c_{1}\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}+c_{2}\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}

(172)

\displaystyle x_{2}(0^{+})

\displaystyle=c_{3}\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{1}+c_{4}\left(\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a))\right)_{2}.

(173)

Defining
C_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}\\
c_{3}&c_{4}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
, we may write

\displaystyle x(0^{+})=C_{0}\varphi^{-}(0^{-};x(a)).

We continue with the determination of the initial value
x(a)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It has to be chosen such that

\displaystyle 0=W_{B}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=W_{B}R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(b)\varphi^{+}(b;x(0^{+}))\\
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(a)\varphi^{-}(a;x(a))\end{bmatrix}=W_{B}R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(b)&0\\
0&\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\varphi^{+}(b;x(0^{+}))\\
x(a)\end{bmatrix}.

(174)

We again wish to write
\varphi^{+}(b;x(0^{+}))
as
Ex(a)+q
for some
E\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
and
q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Similarly to the proof of Theorem
19.9
, we compute that

\displaystyle Ex(a)

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{+}(b,0)\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\left(\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},a)x(a)\right)_{1}+c_{2}\left(\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},a)x(a)\right)_{2}\\
c_{3}\left(\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},a)x(a)\right)_{1}+c_{4}\left(\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},a)x(a)\right)_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\left(\Lambda^{+}(b,0)C_{0}\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},a)\right)x(a)

and

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{+}(b,0)\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\left(\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-},x(a))\right)_{1}+c_{2}\left(\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-},x(a))\right)_{2}\\
c_{3}\left(\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-},x(a))\right)_{1}+c_{4}\left(\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-},x(a))\right)_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\int_{0}^{b}\Lambda^{+}(b,s)(\mathcal{Q}^{+})^{-1}(s)P_{1}y^{+}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{+}(b,0)C_{0}\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-};x(a))-\int_{0}^{b}\Lambda^{+}(b,s)(\mathcal{Q}^{+})^{-1}(s)P_{1}y^{+}(s)\,ds,

where

\displaystyle\varphi_{\text{inhom}}^{-}(0^{-},x(a))=-\int_{a}^{0}\Lambda^{-}(0^{-},s)(\mathcal{Q}^{-})^{-1}(s)P_{1}y^{-}(s)\,ds.

Following the proof, the initial value
x(a)
can be calculated from (
174
) and is given by

\displaystyle x(a)=-\left(W_{B}R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(b)&0\\
0&\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}E\\
I\end{bmatrix}\right)^{-1}W_{B}R_{\extern}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(b)&0\\
0&\mathcal{Q}^{-}(a)\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}q\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(175)

Finally, we can specify the resolvent operator of
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
. For every
\lambda>0
and for every
y\in X
we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\|R(\lambda,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})y\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\varphi_{\lambda,y}(\cdot;x(a),x(0^{+}))\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}\left(c_{0}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))+\overline{c}_{0}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\right)^{\top}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(z)\left(c_{0}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))+\overline{c}_{0}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{a}^{0}\left(\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))\right)^{\top}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))\,dz+\int_{0}^{b}\left(\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\right)^{\top}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(0^{+}))\,dz\right),

with the initial values
x(a)
and
x(0^{+})
given by (
175
) and (
172
)-(
173
), respectively.

We skip specifying the resolvent in the case that the passivity constant is given by
r=0
. To this end, one would basically need to replicate Case 2 in Step 3 of the proof of Theorem
19.9
. Instead, we will come back to the scenario where the interface position is moving over time, and prove a crucial stability property of the resulting system.

19.6
Stability of the Family of Infinitesimal Generators

In the last segment of this chapter we shall analyze the simplified port-Hamiltonian system (
124
) in case of a moving interface. We want to define a family of port-Hamiltonian operators that encompasses the position of the interface, and we want to associate this family with an evolution problem of the form

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=A(t)x(t),\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq s<t\leq\tau,

\displaystyle x(s)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X.

We shall impose assumptions such that this family is a stable family in the sense of Definition
13.4
.

Everything we have proved in Section
19
can be analogously shown for arbitrary interface positions
l\in(a,b)
. We have already come across the operators
\mathbf{d}_{l}
and its formal adjoint
\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast}
in Subsection
18.1
. They are defined by

\displaystyle D(\mathbf{d}_{l})

\displaystyle=H^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{l}x

\displaystyle=-\left[\frac{d}{dz}(c_{l}x)+\frac{d}{dz}(\overline{c}_{l}x)\right],\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathbf{d}_{l}),

and

\displaystyle D(\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\left\{y\in L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R})\mid y_{|(a,l)}\in H^{1}((a,l),\mathbb{R}),\hskip 2.84544pty_{|(l,b)}\in H^{1}((l,b),\mathbb{R})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast}y

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathbf{d}_{l}-\left[\frac{d}{dz}c_{l}+\frac{d}{dz}\overline{c}_{l}\right]\right)y,\hskip 14.22636pty\in D(\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast}),

respectively, where
c_{l}
and
\overline{c}_{l}
are the color functions with a jump discontinuity at the interface position
l
. This allows us to define the formally skew-symmetric matrix differential operator
\mathcal{J}_{l}\colon D(\mathcal{J}_{l})\subset X\to X
as follows:

\displaystyle D(\mathcal{J}_{l})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\mid x_{1}\in D(\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast}),\hskip 2.84544ptx_{2}\in D(\mathbf{d}_{l})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{l}x

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{d}_{l}\\
-\mathbf{d}_{l}^{\ast}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathcal{J}_{l}).

Then the port-Hamiltonian operator
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}\colon D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in X\mid\mathcal{Q}_{l}x\in D(\mathcal{J}_{l}),\hskip 2.84544ptf_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}x}=re_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}x},\hskip 2.84544ptW_{B}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{l}x}\\
e_{\partial,\mathcal{Q}_{l}x}\end{bmatrix}=0\right\},

\displaystyle A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}x

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{l}(\mathcal{Q}_{l}x),\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}),

associated with the abstract Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\dot{x}(t)

\displaystyle=A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}x(t),\hskip 14.22636ptt>0,

\displaystyle x(0)

\displaystyle=x_{0}\in X,

generates a contraction semigroup on the energy space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{l}=c_{l}\mathcal{Q}^{-}+\overline{c}_{l}\mathcal{Q}^{+}\in\mathcal{L}(X)

is coercive, if the assumptions of Theorem
19.9
are satisfied. More precisely, it generates a contraction semigroup
(T_{l}(t))_{t\geq 0}
provided that
W_{B}\Sigma W_{B}^{\top}\geq 0
,
\rank(W_{B})=2
, and
r\geq 0
. Let now the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold (see page
(A1)
). If
W_{B}\Sigma W_{B}^{\top}>0
, then the semigroup
(T_{l}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is even of type
C_{0}(1,-\alpha)
for some
\alpha>0
, see Theorem
19.16
. Its Hilbert space adjoint
A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}^{\ast}\colon D(A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})\subset X\to X
is accordingly defined as in (
168
). Furthermore, the underlying Dirac structure is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{J}_{l}}:=

\displaystyle\left\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}f\\
f_{I}\\
f_{\partial}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}e\\
e_{I}\\
e_{\partial}\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{B}\hskip 2.84544pt\Bigg|\hskip 2.84544pte\in D(\mathcal{J}_{l}),\,f=\mathcal{J}_{l}e,\,f_{I}=e_{2}(0),\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 120.07066pt\left.\,e_{I}=e_{1}(0^{-})-e_{1}(0^{+}),\,\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=R_{\extern}\trace_{l}(e)\right\},

where

\displaystyle\trace_{l}\colon D(\mathcal{J}_{l})\to\mathbb{R}^{4},\hskip 8.5359pte\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}e(b)\\
e(a)\end{bmatrix}.

Together with the resistive structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{r}=\{(f_{I},e_{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid f_{I}=re_{I}\},

the dynamics are geometrically specified by the requirement that for all
t>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\left(\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}x(\cdot,t)\\
f_{I}(t)\\
f_{\partial}(t)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{Q}_{l}x(\cdot,t)\\
e_{I}(t)\\
e_{\partial}(t)\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{J}_{l}},\hskip 8.5359pt(f_{I}(t),e_{I}(t))\in\mathcal{R}_{r}.

Lastly, if
r>0
, then for the resolvent it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\|R(\lambda,A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})y\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{a}^{l}\left(\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))\right)^{\top}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)\varphi^{-}(z;x(a))\,dz+\int_{l}^{b}\left(\varphi^{+}(z;x(l^{+}))\right)^{\top}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\varphi^{+}(z;x(l^{+}))\,dz\right),

with
y\in X
,
\lambda>0
, and the solutions
\varphi^{-}(\cdot;x(a))
,
\varphi^{+}(\cdot;x(l^{+}))
as well as the values
x(a)
and
x(l^{+})
accordingly defined, see Subsection
19.5
.

Unfortunately, the state space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})
has to be changed for different interface positions
l\in(a,b)
to guarantee that the aforementioned statements hold true. However, we have a norm equivalence of the family
(\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}})_{l\in(a,b)}
to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
: Since
mI\leq\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(z)\leq MI
for all
z\in[a,b]
, we get for all
l\in(a,b)
,

\displaystyle\frac{m}{2}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}^{2}\leq\frac{M}{2}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{M}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\leq\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{m}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

The norm equivalence between
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}
for some
l\in(a,b)
follows immediately from the preceding inequalities:

\displaystyle\frac{m}{M}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\leq\frac{m}{2}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l}}^{2}\leq\frac{M}{2}\|x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{M}{m}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2},\hskip 14.22636ptx\in X.

(176)

Note that the constants
\underline{M}=\sqrt{\frac{m}{M}}
and
\overline{M}=\sqrt{\frac{M}{m}}
are independent of the interface position
l\in(a,b)
.

We now want to show the following: Let
0<\tau<\infty
, and let
l\colon[0,\tau]\to(a,b)
be continuously differentiable with
\dot{l}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R})
. Let
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
be a family of port-Hamiltonian operators, where
A(t)=A_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}}\colon D(A(t))\subset X\to X
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(A(t))&=\left\{x\in X\mid\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x\in D(\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}),\hskip 2.84544ptf_{I}(t)=re_{I}(t),\hskip 2.84544ptW_{B}\begin{bmatrix}f_{\partial}\\
e_{\partial}\end{bmatrix}=0\right\},\\
A(t)x&=\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x)\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}\\
-\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}^{\ast}&0\end{bmatrix}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x),\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(A(t)),\end{split}

(177)

with
e_{I}(t)=e_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x}
,
f_{I}(t)=f_{I,\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x}
. The operators
\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}
and
\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}^{\ast}
constituting the matrix differential operator
\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}\colon D(\mathcal{J}_{l(t)})\subset X\to X
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(\mathcal{J}_{l(t)})&=D(\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}^{\ast})\times D(\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}),\\
\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}x&=\begin{bmatrix}0&\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}\\
-\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}^{\ast}&0\end{bmatrix}x,\hskip 14.22636ptx\in D(\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}),\end{split}

(178)

are precisely defined as the operators
\mathbf{d}(t)
and
\mathbf{d}^{\ast}(t)
given in (
115
) and (
119
), respectively. Furthermore, the coercive operator
\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
,
t\in[0,\tau]
, is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}=c_{l}(\cdot,t)\mathcal{Q}^{-}+\overline{c}_{l}(\cdot,t)\mathcal{Q}^{+}.

(179)

We claim that, under certain conditions, this is a stable family on the state space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
.

Remark 19.21
.

We changed the notation of the operators
\mathbf{d}(t)
and
\mathbf{d}^{\ast}(t)
to
\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}
and
\mathbf{d}_{l(t)}^{\ast}
, respectively, to emphasize that the operators explicitly depend on the moving interface position
l(t)
,
t>0
. Recall that the operators
\mathbf{d}(t)
and
\mathbf{d}^{\ast}(t)
introduced in Subsection
18.1
depend implicitly on the interface position through their definition with respect to the color functions
c_{l}
and
\overline{c}_{l}
that we took to be part of the extended state variable
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{X}=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{4})
. Since this is not the case for the simplified system discussed throughout Section
19
, the new notation helps to distinguish the different scenarios.

If the assumptions
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold, then, due to the norm equivalence (
176
) of the norms of the respective energy spaces
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}})
, we can at least guarantee that the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is a family of infinitesimal generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
(or any other state space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{\hat{l}}})
with
\hat{l}\in(a,b)
), which are of type
C_{0}(\overline{M},0)
. However, as we will see, this is not sufficient to prove the stability of this family. Thus, we need to impose more restrictive assumptions. These are given as follows:

(
\tilde{\mathrm{A}}
1)

The matrix operators
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
defining the coercive operator
\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
are diagonal matrix operators, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(z)=\begin{bmatrix}Q_{11}^{\pm}(z)&0\\
0&Q_{22}^{\pm}(z)\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b],

and they satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(0)=1\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(z)=\frac{Q_{22}^{+}}{Q_{22}^{-}}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

(
\tilde{\mathrm{A}}
2)

r=0
,
\rank(W_{B})=2
, and
W_{B}\Sigma W_{B}^{\top}\geq 0
.

Assumption
(

~
A

1)
implies that the ratios of the respective physical properties of the subsystems are identical on the spatial domain
[a,b]
, and that the physical properties even coincide in
z=0
. Further, assumption
(

~
A

2)
implies that
A(t)
generates a contraction semigroup on the respective energy space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}})
, where there is no power flow at the interface position, see Theorem
19.9
.

Since we can prove the stability neither directly nor by means of Theorem
13.5
, we make use of an extension of a Lyapunov theorem
[
9
]
.

Theorem 19.21
.

Let
0<\tau<\infty
, and let
l\colon[0,\tau]\to(a,b)
be continuously differentiable with
\dot{l}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R})
. Let the assumptions
(

~
A

1)
-
(

~
A

2)
hold. Consider the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of port-Hamiltonian operators given by (
177
)-(
179
) defined on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
. Then there exists some
\omega>0
such that for every
t\in[0,\tau]
and for every
x\in D(A(t))
,

\displaystyle\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\leq\omega\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}.

(180)

Proof 19.22
.

Let
t\in[0,\tau]
such that for the interface position we have
a<l(t)<0
. Recall that in this case, the operator
\mathcal{J}_{l(t)}
defined in (
178
) acts as the operator
P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}
on the subdomains
[a,l(t))
and
(l(t),b]
, with
P_{1}
defined in (
128
). Furthermore, as
A(t)
generates a contraction semigroup on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}})
, it is dissipative, see Theorem
10.16
. More precisely, for all
x\in D(A(t))
it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{b}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}(z)A(t)x(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{l(t)}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z))\,dz+\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z))\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{b}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z))\,dz

\displaystyle\leq 0.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{a}^{l(t)}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z))\,dz+\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z))\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{b}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)x(z))\,dz

\displaystyle=\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)^{\top}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(z)-(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)^{\top}(z)P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z))P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\left[(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z))P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\right]x(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\left[(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z))P_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\right]\frac{d}{dz}x(z)\,dz.

We may choose
\omega_{1}^{-}>0
large enough such that (in the sense of the symmetric part)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z))P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)\leq\omega_{1}^{-}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

Now, let us define the matrix operator
\tilde{Q}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}(z)=(\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z))P_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)=-\begin{bmatrix}0&Q_{22}^{+}(Q_{11}^{-}-Q_{11}^{+})\\
Q_{11}^{+}(Q_{22}^{-}-Q_{22}^{+})&0\end{bmatrix}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

(181)

By assumption
(

~
A

1)
, we have
\mathcal{Q}^{+},\mathcal{Q}^{-}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
), and so the matrix operator
\tilde{Q}
is continuously differentiable as well. Moreover, by
(

~
A

1)
, it is easy to check that
\tilde{Q}(z)
is symmetric for all
z\in[a,b]
. Thus, integration by parts yields that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)\frac{d}{dz}x(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\frac{d}{dz}\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\,dz.

Once again, we may choose
\omega_{2}^{-}>0
large enough such that

\displaystyle-\frac{d}{dz}\tilde{Q}(z)\leq\omega_{2}^{-}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

Altogether, we obtain for all
x\in D(A(t))
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}&\leq\frac{\omega_{1}^{-}}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z)\,dz\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{4}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}+\frac{\omega_{2}^{-}}{4}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z)\,dz.\end{split}

(182)

The interface relation (
142
) with
r=0
together with the assumption that
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}
are diagonal matrix operators imply that
x_{2}(l(t)^{+})=x_{2}(l(t)^{-})=0
. Furthermore, by assumption
(

~
A

1)
,
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}
coincide in
z=0
.
Consequently, it holds that

\displaystyle\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}

\displaystyle=-x_{1}(0)x_{2}(0)\left[Q_{22}^{+}(Q_{11}^{-}-Q_{11}^{+})+Q_{11}^{+}(Q_{22}^{-}-Q_{22}^{+})\right](0)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[Q_{22}^{+}(Q_{11}^{-}-Q_{11}^{+})+Q_{11}^{+}(Q_{22}^{-}-Q_{22}^{+})\right](l(t))

\displaystyle=0.

With the choice
\omega^{-}=\max\{\omega_{1}^{-},\frac{\omega_{2}^{-}}{2}\}
we deduce from (
182
) that for all
x\in D(A(t))
, where
t\in[0,\tau]
is chosen such that
a<l(t)<0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\leq\omega^{-}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}.

Now, let
t\in[0,\tau]
such that for the interface position we have
0<l(t)<b
. Analogously to the previous case, we have for all
x\in D(A(t))
,

\displaystyle\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{l(t)}x^{\top}(z)\left[(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z))P_{1}\frac{d}{dz}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)\right]x(z)\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{l(t)}x^{\top}(z)\left[(\mathcal{Q}^{+}(z)-\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z))P_{1}\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)\right]\frac{d}{dz}x(z)\,dz.

One can readily see that the same arguments as in the former case apply, and so there exists some
\omega^{+}>0
such that

\displaystyle\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\leq\omega^{+}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

for all
x\in D(A(t))
with
0<l(t)<b
. If
l(t)=0
for some
t\in[0,\tau]
, then
A(t)
is dissipative on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
. Altogether, the choice
\omega=\max\{\omega^{-},\omega^{+}\}
yields the estimate (
180
) for all
t\in[0,\tau]
and for all
x\in D(A(t))
. This proves the claim.

Remark 19.23
.

We want to emphasize that the choice of the state space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is defined on was arbitrary. In fact, we may endow
X
with any norm of the form
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{\hat{l}}}
, where
\hat{l}\in(a,b)
, and change the assumption
(

~
A

1)
as follows:

(
\tilde{\mathrm{A}}
1)

The matrix operators
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
defining the coercive operator
\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
are diagonal matrix operators and they satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(\hat{l})=1\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(z)=\frac{Q_{22}^{+}}{Q_{22}^{-}}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

In particular, if there is some
\hat{l}\in(a,b)
such that the matrices
\mathcal{Q}^{-}(\hat{l})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{+}(\hat{l})
coincide, then it is advisable to consider the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
defined on the space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{\hat{l}}})
.

Note that the inequality (
180
) implies that for all
t\in[0,\tau]
, the
C_{0}
-semigroup
(S_{t}(s))_{s\geq 0}
on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
generated by
A(t)
is of type
C_{0}(1,\omega)
: By virtue of (
180
), for all
x\in D(A(t))
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{d}{ds}\|S_{t}(s)x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}&=\frac{d}{ds}\langle S_{t}(s)x,S_{t}(s)x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\\
&=2\langle AS_{t}(s)x,S_{t}(s)x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\\
&\leq 2\omega\|S_{t}(s)x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}.\end{split}

(183)

Since
s\mapsto e^{2\omega s}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}
is the unique solution of the initial value problem

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}y(s)

\displaystyle=2\omega y(s),\hskip 14.22636pts>0,

\displaystyle y(0)

\displaystyle=\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2},

we infer from (
183
) that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\|S_{t}(s)x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}\leq\frac{d}{ds}\left(e^{\omega s}\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\right)^{2}

holds for all
x\in D(A(t))
. As
D(A(t))
is a dense subset of
X
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|S_{t}(s)\|_{\mathcal{L}(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})}\leq e^{\omega s},\hskip 14.22636pts\geq 0.

Recall from Remark
13.5
that this yields that the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is stable in the sense of Definition
13.4
with stability constants
M=1
and
\omega>0
. Indeed, by the Hille-Yosida Theorem
10.12
we have
(\omega,\infty)\subset\rho(A(t))
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
. Furthermore, for every finite sequence
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}\leq\tau
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and for all
\lambda>\omega
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\prod_{j=1}^{k}R(\lambda,A(t_{j}))\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left\|R(\lambda,A(t_{j}))\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\lambda-\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\lambda-\omega)^{k}}.

Together with Theorem
19.21
we can summarize these facts as follows:

Corollary 19.24
.

Let
0<\tau<\infty
, and let
l\colon[0,\tau]\to(a,b)
be continuously differentiable with
\dot{l}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R})
. Let the assumptions
(

~
A

1)
-
(

~
A

2)
hold. Then the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of port-Hamiltonian operators given by (
177
)-(
179
) is stable on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
.

\blacksquare

Lastly, we wish to prove the same statement as in Corollary
19.24
for the system where we allow for a power flow at the moving interface. We impose the following assumptions:

(
\tilde{\mathrm{B}}
1)

The matrix operators
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
defining the coercive operator
\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}\in\mathcal{L}(X)
are diagonal matrix operators and they satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(0)=1\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(z)=\frac{Q_{22}^{+}}{Q_{22}^{-}}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,b].

(
\tilde{\mathrm{B}}
2)

r>0
,
\rank(W_{B})=2
, and
W_{B}\Sigma W_{B}^{\top}>0
.

Assumption
(

~
B

2)
implies that
A(t)
generates an exponentially stable contraction semigroup on the respective energy space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}})
, see Theorem
19.16
.

Let
t\in[0,\tau]
such that for the interface position we have
a<l(t)<0
. From assumption
(

~
B

2)
and from the proof of Theorem
19.16
we deduce that for all
x\in D(A(t))
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle e_{\partial},f_{\partial}\rangle_{2}-\frac{1}{2}e_{I}(t)f_{I}(t)

\displaystyle\leq-k\|\trace_{l(t)}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x)\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}e_{I}(t)f_{I}(t)

for some
k>0
small enough. With the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
19.21
, the following estimate holds for all
x\in D(A(t))
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle A(t)x,x\rangle_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}&\leq-k\|\trace_{l(t)}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x)\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}e_{I}(t)f_{I}(t)+\frac{\omega_{1}^{-}}{2}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z)\,dz\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{4}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}+\frac{\omega_{2}^{-}}{4}\int_{l(t)}^{0}x^{\top}(z)\mathcal{Q}^{-}(z)x(z)\,dz,\end{split}

(184)

with
\omega_{1}^{-}>0
and
\omega_{2}^{-}>0
large enough, and with
\tilde{Q}
defined in (
181
). In the following, we estimate the expression

\displaystyle-f_{I}(t)e_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}.

By assumption
(

~
B

1)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[Q_{22}^{+}(Q_{11}^{-}-Q_{11}^{+})+Q_{11}^{+}(Q_{22}^{-}-Q_{22}^{+})\right](l(t)).

By definition of the interface port variables (
132
)-(
133
), we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-f_{I}(t)e_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[Q_{22}^{+}(Q_{11}^{-}-Q_{11}^{+})+Q_{11}^{+}(Q_{22}^{-}-Q_{22}^{+})\right](l(t))\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\text{abcdefghii}

\displaystyle=f_{I}(t)\left[Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[(Q_{11}^{-}Q_{22}^{+})(l(t))-2(Q_{11}^{+}Q_{22}^{+})(l(t))+(Q_{11}^{+}Q_{22}^{-})(l(t))\right]

\displaystyle=f_{I}(t)\left[Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right]-Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})\underbrace{Q_{22}^{+}(l(t))x_{2}(l(t)^{+})}_{=f_{I}(t)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[(Q_{11}^{+}Q_{22}^{-})(l(t))+(Q_{11}^{-}Q_{22}^{+})(l(t))\right]

\displaystyle=-f_{I}(t)Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}(l(t)^{+})x_{2}(l(t)^{+})\left[(Q_{11}^{-}Q_{22}^{+})(l(t))+(Q_{11}^{+}Q_{22}^{-})(l(t))\right]

\displaystyle=-f_{I}(t)Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})+\frac{1}{2}f_{I}(t)Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})+\frac{1}{2}f_{I}(t)\frac{Q_{22}^{-}}{Q_{22}^{+}}(l(t))Q_{11}^{+}(l(t)x_{1}(l(t)^{+})

\displaystyle=f_{I}(t)\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))+\frac{Q_{22}^{-}}{Q_{22}^{+}}(l(t))Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))\right)x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right].

Let us define

\displaystyle\eta^{-}(t):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{Q_{11}^{-}}{Q_{11}^{+}}(l(t))+\frac{Q_{22}^{-}}{Q_{22}^{+}}(l(t))\right)\in\left[\frac{m}{M},\frac{M}{m}\right].

(185)

Now, by virtue of the passivity relation (
142
), we compute

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636ptf_{I}(t)\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))+\frac{Q_{22}^{-}}{Q_{22}^{+}}(l(t))Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))\right)x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right]

\displaystyle=re_{I}(t)\left[\eta^{-}(t)Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right]

\displaystyle=-r\left(Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right)\left[\eta^{-}(t)Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})-Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right]

\displaystyle=-r\left[\eta^{-}(t)(Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+}))^{2}-(\eta^{-}(t)+1)Q_{11}^{+}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{+})Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\left.+(Q_{11}^{-}(l(t))x_{1}(l(t)^{-}))^{2}\right].

Put
e_{1}^{+}(z)=Q_{11}^{+}(z)x_{1}(z)
,
z\in(l(t),b]
, and
e_{1}^{-}(z)=Q_{11}^{-}(z)x_{1}(z)
,
z\in[a,l(t))
. By virtue of the inequality
xy\leq\frac{1}{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we find

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636ptr\left[-\eta^{-}(t)e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}-e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}+(\eta^{-}(t)+1)e_{1}^{+}(l(t))e_{1}^{-}(l(t))\right]

\displaystyle\leq r\left[-\eta^{-}(t)e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}-e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}+\frac{\eta^{-}(t)+1}{2}e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+\frac{\eta^{-}(t)+1}{2}e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=r\left[\frac{1-\eta^{-}(t)}{2}e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+\frac{\eta^{-}(t)-1}{2}e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}\right].

Altogehter, we have

\displaystyle-f_{I}(t)e_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}=r\left[\frac{1-\eta^{-}(t)}{2}e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+\frac{\eta^{-}(t)-1}{2}e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}\right].

We wish to find some
\omega_{3}^{-}>0
large enough that guarantees that (cf. (
184
))

\displaystyle-k\|\trace_{l(t)}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x)\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}f_{I}(t)e_{I}(t)+\frac{1}{4}\big[x^{\top}(z)\tilde{Q}(z)x(z)\big]_{l(t)}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\leq}}\omega_{3}^{-}\|x|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}.

Define

\displaystyle e_{2}^{-}(z)

\displaystyle=Q_{22}^{-}(z)x_{2}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[a,l(t)],

\displaystyle e_{2}^{+}(z)

\displaystyle=Q_{22}^{+}(z)x_{2}(z),\hskip 14.22636ptz\in[l(t),b].

Using the definition of
e_{1}^{\pm}
and
e_{2}^{\pm}
, we have

\displaystyle 2\|x\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{l(t)}Q_{11}^{-}(z)x_{1}(z)^{2}+Q_{22}^{-}(z)x_{2}(z)^{2}\,dz+\int_{l(t)}^{0}Q_{11}^{-}(z)x_{1}(z)^{2}+Q_{22}^{-}(z)x_{2}(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{0}^{b}Q_{11}^{+}(z)x_{1}(z)^{2}+Q_{22}^{+}(z)x_{2}(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{l(t)}\frac{1}{Q_{11}^{-}}(z)e_{1}^{-}(z)^{2}+\frac{1}{Q_{22}^{-}}(z)e_{2}^{-}(z)^{2}\,dz+\int_{l(t)}^{0}\frac{Q_{11}^{-}}{(Q_{11}^{+})^{2}}(z)e_{1}^{+}(z)^{2}+\frac{Q_{22}^{-}}{(Q_{22}^{+})^{2}}(z)e_{2}^{+}(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{0}^{b}\frac{1}{Q_{11}^{+}}(z)e_{1}^{+}(z)^{2}+\frac{1}{Q_{22}^{+}}(z)e_{2}^{+}(z)(z)^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left(\int_{a}^{l(t)}e_{1}^{-}(z)^{2}+e_{2}^{-}(z)^{2}\,dz+\int_{l(t)}^{b}e_{1}^{+}(z)^{2}+e_{2}^{+}(z)^{2}\,dz\right)

for some
\delta>0
small enough. Hence, it amounts to show that there is some
\tilde{\omega}_{3}^{-}>0
such that for all
x\in D(A(t))
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip 14.22636pt-k\|\trace_{l(t)}(\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{r}{4}\left[(1-\eta^{-}(t))e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+(\eta^{-}(t)-1)e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}\right]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\leq}}\tilde{\omega}_{3}^{-}\left(\int_{a}^{l(t)}e_{1}^{-}(z)^{2}+e_{2}^{-}(z)^{2}\,dz+\int_{l(t)}^{b}e_{1}^{+}(z)^{2}+e_{2}^{+}(z)^{2}\,dz\right).\end{split}

(186)

Note that the continuity condition (
132
) implies that

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt(1-\eta^{-}(t))e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+(\eta^{-}(t)-1)e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\eta^{-}(t))[e_{1}^{+}(l(t))^{2}+e_{2}^{+}(l(t))^{2}]+(\eta^{-}(t)-1)[e_{1}^{-}(l(t))^{2}+e_{2}^{-}(l(t))^{2}].

Thus, by using the definition of
e_{1}^{\pm}
and
e_{2}^{\pm}
again, (
186
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-k\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{r}{4}\left[(\eta^{-}(t)-1)\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(l(t))x(l(t)^{-})\|_{2}^{2}+(1-\eta^{-}(t))\|\mathcal{Q}^{+}(l(t))x(l(t)^{+})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\leq}}\tilde{\omega}_{3}^{-}\left(\int_{a}^{l(t)}\|(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)(z)\|_{2}^{2}\,dz+\int_{l(t)}^{b}\|(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(z)\|_{2}^{2}\,dz\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\omega}_{3}^{-}\|\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Similarly, for
t\in[0,\tau]
such that
0<l(t)<b
, the following estimate has to hold for all
x\in D(A(t))
:

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-k\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{r}{4}\left[(1-\eta^{+}(t))\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(l(t))x(l(t)^{-})\|_{2}^{2}+(\eta^{+}(t)-1)\|\mathcal{Q}^{+}(l(t))x(l(t)^{+})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\leq}}\tilde{\omega}_{3}^{+}\|\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

where

\displaystyle\eta^{+}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{Q_{11}^{+}}{Q_{11}^{-}}(l(t))+\frac{Q_{22}^{+}}{Q_{22}^{-}}(l(t))\right)\in\left[\frac{m}{M},\frac{M}{m}\right].

(187)

This can be summarized as follows:

Corollary 19.25
.

Let
0<\tau<\infty
, and let
l\colon[0,\tau]\to(a,b)
be continuously differentiable with
\dot{l}\in\mathcal{C}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R})
. Let the assumptions
(

~
B

1)
-
(

~
B

2)
hold. Let
k>0
be chosen such that (
163
) is satisfied. Then the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
of port-Hamiltonian operators given by (
177
)-(
179
) is stable on
(X,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}_{0}})
, if the following assertions are true:

(i)

There exists some
\tilde{\omega}^{-}>0
such that for all
t\in[0,\tau]
with
a<l(t)<0
and for all
x\in D(A(t))
we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-k\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{r}{4}\left[(\eta^{-}(t)-1)\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(l(t))x(l(t)^{-})\|_{2}^{2}+(1-\eta^{-}(t))\|\mathcal{Q}^{+}(l(t))x(l(t)^{+})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\omega}^{-}\|\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

where
\eta^{-}(t)
is defined in (
185
).

(ii)

There exists some
\tilde{\omega}^{+}>0
such that for all
t\in[0,\tau]
with
0<l(t)<b
and for all
x\in D(A(t))
we have

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-k\left(\|(\mathcal{Q}^{-}x)(a)\|_{2}^{2}+\|(\mathcal{Q}^{+}x)(b)\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{r}{4}\left[(1-\eta^{+}(t))\|\mathcal{Q}^{-}(l(t))x(l(t)^{-})\|_{2}^{2}+(\eta^{+}(t)-1)\|\mathcal{Q}^{+}(l(t))x(l(t)^{+})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\omega}^{+}\|\mathcal{Q}_{l(t)}x\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

where
\eta^{+}(t)
is defined in (
187
).

\blacksquare

As we have discussed in Section
13
, the stability of the family
(A(t))_{t\in[0,\tau]}
is not sufficient for well-posedness of the corresponding evolution problem. However, we will not further analyze this system description and conclude this segment with the preceding stability results.

In this section, we have studied a simplified model closely related to the one formulated in
[
10
]
. We have defined its underlying Dirac structure, and we have worked out passivity conditions that guarantee that the associated port-Hamiltonian operator generates a contraction semigroup on the respective energy space. Those conditions have been defined with respect to the interface and boundary port variables. Moreover, we have presented criteria for exponential stability of the
C_{0}
-semigroup generated by the port-Hamiltonian operator, and we have specified the associated Hilbert space adjoint as well as the resolvent. All these results hold in case of a fixed interface. At the end of this section, we have defined a family of port-Hamiltonian operators that keeps track of the position of the moving interface. We have shown under which assumptions this family is stable, and we have learned that the power flow at the position of the moving interface is a critical factor for the stability.

Chapter 6
Conclusions and Future Work

In this thesis, we have provided a functional-analytic foundation for the port-Hamiltonian formulation of two systems of two conservation laws that are coupled by a moving interface worked out in
[
10
]
. Furthermore, we have analyzed a simplified version of the proposed model by means of the powerful framework of strongly continuous semigroups. The results presented in this work can be considered as a firm basis for the further analysis of this quite new system class.

In Chapter
2
we have introduced the concept of port-based modeling and the necessary terminology related to this framework by reference to finite-dimensional systems. We have studied some basic properties of the underlying Dirac structure, i.e., the power-conserving interconnection structure, as well as the constituting port variables of a multi-physics system, and depicted how the dynamics of a port-Hamiltonian system can be defined with respect to the Dirac structure. Subsequently, in Chapter
3
we have briefly recalled the definition of the Hilbert space adjoint of a linear operator
A\colon D(A)\subset X\to X
defined on some Hilbert space
X
, and we have highlighted the difference between the Hilbert space adjoint and the formal adjoint of a matrix differential operator. This allowed us to define formally skew-symmetric operators, which play a fundamental role in the framework of infinite-dimensional port-Hamiltonian systems. Afterwards, we have reviewed the basics of infinite-dimensional systems theory. For the analysis we have decided for the theory of semigroups of linear operators, so we gave a concise overview of this framework in Section
10
. Our emphasis was to recall sufficient conditions for well-posedness of abstract control systems (see Section
12
) and of evolution problems (see Section
13
). In Chapter
4
we have extended the port-Hamiltonian framework to infinite-dimensional systems, where we have restricted ourselves to systems with a 1-dimensional spatial domain. We have investigated port-Hamiltonian systems that are associated with formally skew-symmetric matrix differential operators, and we have highlighted the main differences compared to its finite-dimensional counterpart. The most important difference is that one has to take the energy flow at the spatial boundary into account. This is accomplished by introducing boundary port variables, which has allowed us to define the infinite-dimensional power-conserving interconnection structure, called the Stokes-Dirac structure, associated with formally skew-symmetric operators.

The preceding chapters have set the foundation for the main part of this thesis, namely the port-Hamiltonian formulation and the analysis of two first-order port-Hamiltonian systems that are coupled by a moving interface, which we have dealt with in Chapter
5
. In Section
17
we have started by formulating the aforementioned systems that are coupled by an interface fixed at
z=0
as a single boundary port-Hamiltonian system, and we have imposed interface conditions on the co-energy variables. Furthermore, we have introduced so-called color functions, which are characteristic functions of the complementary subdomains that keep track of the position of the interface. We have taken them to constitute the extended state space
\tilde{X}=L^{2}([a,b],\mathbb{R}^{4})
. Having regard to the interface relations, we have defined balance equations of the state variables on the composed domain, and we have defined a generalized Hamiltonian system of off these equations. This has allowed us to define a Dirac structure for the fixed interface scenario with respect to a formally skew-symmetric differential operator, see Proposition
17.5
.

In Section
18
we have repeated the exposition from Section
17
in case of a moving interface position. As opposed to the fixed interface case, the proper formulation as a port-Hamiltonian system is fraught with problems. We have stated the main issues in
(P1)
-
(P3)
. Instead of tackling these problems directly, we have studied a simplified model that is closely related to the original system in Section
19
, where we have merely assumed that the color functions are not part of the state variables anymore. Furthermore, we have assumed that the variational derivative of the Hamiltonian functional is linear. For the analysis, we have started again by assuming that the interface is fixed. After specifying the Dirac structure of this system (see Corollary
19.1
), we have analyzed the port-Hamiltonian operator associated with this system. We have shown that if we impose passivity conditions at the boundary and at the interface, then the port-Hamiltonian operator is the infinitesimal generator of a contraction semigroup. In particular, the coupling of two first-order port-Hamiltonian systems coupled by a fixed interface is well-defined, see Theorem
19.9
. We have illustrated this result by reference to the coupling of two transmission lines with distinct physical properties, see Example
19.13
. Furthermore, we have been able to show that under more restrictive assumptions regarding the boundary conditions, the port-Hamiltonian operator generates an exponentially stable contraction semigroup, see Theorem
19.16
. After specifying the Hilbert space adjoint as well as the resolvent of the port-Hamiltonian operator in Subsection
19.4
and Subsection
19.5
, respectively, we have studied a family of port-Hamiltonian operators that encompasses the position of the moving interface. We have provided criteria that ensure that this family is stable (see Subsection
19.6
), which is an essential property for the proof of well-posedness of the evolution problem associated with this family.

One of these criteria is that we have assumed that there is no power flow at the interface (see Corollary
19.24
), which is quite restrictive. If we allow for a power flow at the interface, there is room for improvement concerning the stability result stated in this thesis (see Corollary
19.25
). Furthermore, there are several problems remaining open. For instance, one needs to check for the sufficient conditions for well-posedness apart from stability. Moreover, we have not discussed the input operator associated with the simplified system in case of a moving interface at all. Hence, plenty of control related problems have to be clarified in the future. Additionally, for a complete port-Hamiltonian formulation, we need to find the output port variable conjugated to the velocity of the interface (the input). This is the major topic for further research. After these problems have been successfully tackled for the simplified system, one may eventually deal with the original system (
121
) accompanied by the delicate problems
(P1)
-
(P3)
again (see pages
121
-
(P3)
).

Besides the demanding task of proving well-posedness of the boundary port-Hamiltonian interface control system, as one may call the model proposed in
[
10
]
, there are countless possibilities to generalize the (simplified) system description, e.g., admitting a broader class of color functions, deploying a passive network at the interface position, etc. In conclusion, the port-Hamiltonian model of two systems coupled by a moving interface offers a vast field of potential research.
